Six dials, two outputs. ❗ The depth figures in the profile are illustrative orders of magnitude that show the **structure** of a weathering profile; they are not measured Indian values and none is asserted as such.

### 1. The question, and why it is the most productive in the weathering block

⚠️ Lessons 2 and 3 answered *how* rock breaks down. This lesson answers the question an examiner actually asks: **why does the same rock, exposed to the same processes, weather to thirty metres in one place and barely at all in another?** ⚠️ The answer is that weathering has **six independent controls**, and the landscape is the product of their combination rather than of any one of them.

❗ The payoff is immediate and it is the reason this lesson sits where it does. **Lesson 16's eight groundwater provinces are a map of control one — rock structure — operating across India.** **Lesson 7's eight soil groups are a map of controls one, two and five interacting.** **Lesson 8's erosion geography is largely control six.** A candidate who holds the six controls can derive all three rather than memorise them.

### 2. The six controls, as the owner states them

✅ The core owner is explicit: *"Rate and depth of weathering are controlled by six factors (Majid Husain): (i) rock structure & hardness, (ii) climate, (iii) topography & slope, (iv) natural vegetation, (v) time, and (vi) land use by man."* ✅ It adds the ranking: **climate is decisive**, physical weathering dominating high latitudes and altitudes while chemical weathering dominates hot-humid equatorial, monsoon and sub-tropical wet regions — *"and is generally the more important of the two."* ✅ The control set is reproduced in the source as a figure **after A.H. Strahler**.

⚠️ Each control is worth one precise sentence rather than a paragraph:

- **Rock structure and hardness.** ⚠️ The operative variable is **joint density**, not hardness in the scratch sense. Joints are the pathways by which water reaches the interior, so a closely jointed hard rock weathers faster than a massive soft one. ❗ This is the control that produces corestones in section 4 below, and the control that writes Lesson 16's province map.
- **Climate.** ✅ Decisive at world scale; it selects the **family** of processes and sets the **rate** of reaction. ⚠️ Temperature governs reaction rate; moisture governs both reaction and the removal of products, and removal matters because a saturated, product-clogged profile reacts more slowly.
- **Topography and slope.** ⚠️ A **steep slope exports the regolith**, continually exposing fresh rock and keeping the mantle thin; a **gentle interfluve retains it**, so the profile deepens and the weathering front is progressively insulated from fresh water. ❗ This is why deep regoliths are found on **plateau surfaces and gentle pediments**, not on scarps — a fact Lesson 16 uses directly.
- **Natural vegetation.** ✅ Treated in section 3, because its effect inverts the obvious expectation.
- **Time.** ⚠️ Weathering depth is **cumulative**, so a deep profile is a statement about the **age and stability of the land surface**. ❗ An ancient, tectonically quiet surface such as the Peninsular shield has had the time that a young Himalayan slope has not.
- **Land use by man.** ✅ Named by the owner as a control in its own right, which is unusual and useful. ⚠️ Quarrying and road cutting expose fresh rock; tillage mixes and aerates; irrigation changes the moisture regime and introduces salts; deforestation strips the cover that control four describes. ❗ This is also the control that makes Lesson 8's human factors and Lesson 12's man-made causes continuous with the weathering lesson rather than an afterthought.

### 3. The counter-intuitive result: a soil cover enhances chemical weathering

✅ G.C. Leong records the finding that most candidates get backwards: **a soil cover does not protect the rock beneath it from chemical weathering — it usually enhances it**, because percolating water carries **organic acids** into the rock.

⚠️ Lesson 3 supplied the mechanism, and it is worth restating as a chain because the chain is the answer: **vegetation → litter and roots → respiration and decay → soil air strongly enriched in carbon dioxide and organic acids → percolating water is far more aggressive than the rain that fell → the weathering front at the base of the profile is attacked by the most chemically loaded water in the system.**

❗ Three consequences follow, and each is examinable.

- ⚠️ **Chemical weathering is a subsurface process.** Its most active surface is the **base** of the regolith, not the top. The bare rock outcrop that looks weathered is usually the least active part of the landscape.
- ⚠️ **The profile is self-deepening up to a point.** More weathering produces more soil, which supports more vegetation, which produces more aggressive water. ❗ The limit is **drainage**: once the regolith becomes thick and clay-rich enough to impede percolation, the water reaching the front is no longer renewed and the deepening slows.
- ⚠️ **Removing the vegetation does not protect the rock; it changes which process attacks it.** Chemical attack falls, but detachment and transport rise sharply — which is Lesson 8's entire subject.

### 4. Differential weathering, joint density and corestones

✅ Leong's **Fig. 4.1** shows the structural control working at outcrop scale: **differential weathering produces corestones, and the control is joint density.** ⚠️ The mechanism is geometric. Water enters along joints, so a joint-bounded block is attacked from **all its faces and edges at once**. Edges and corners have more exposed surface per unit volume than faces, so they decay fastest, and the block is progressively **rounded from the outside in**. Where joints were closely spaced, the whole block is consumed and becomes part of the clay-rich matrix; where joints were widely spaced, an unweathered **kernel survives** at the centre. ⚠️ Those kernels are corestones, and a field of them emerging from a stripped regolith is the origin of the boulder-strewn landscapes of weathered granite terrain.

✅ Leong supplies the worked mineral case, at **Tampin, Negri Sembilan, in Malaysia**: in granite, **felspar weathers faster than quartz**, leaving a **coarse sandy residue**. ⚠️ That is mineral-scale differential weathering, and it is the same logic one scale down — hydrolysis consumes the feldspar, while quartz, the most stable common silicate, survives as sand. ✅ Leong also records the **deep tropical regoliths** that result where this process has run for a long time on a stable surface.

❗ **Citation discipline, declared.** ⚠️ Leong's local scan has a **non-uniform printed-to-scan page offset**, so this edition cites Leong **by local-scan page** and gives a printed folio only where one is directly visible on the rendered page. ⚠️ Leong's Chapter 4 uses figure numbers 4.1 to 4.11, while the opening page of the following chapter in the same scan uses a different numbering series, which suggests the scan merges edition sections. ❗ A Leong figure number is therefore cited **only where it was visible on the page that was read**.

### 5. India hook — the Western Ghats laterite profile against the Aravalli crystallines

🇮🇳 ⚠️ Two Indian uplands in broadly the same latitude band produce opposite weathering profiles, and the six dials explain the difference without any new principle. **The Western Ghats** combine very high rainfall with a warm temperature regime, a seasonal wet-dry alternation, dense forest cover and, on their gentler eastern slopes and summit plateaux, surfaces old and stable enough to retain their products. Controls two, four and five are all turned towards deep chemical alteration, and the product is ✅ the laterite of Lesson 3 — thick, clay-and-oxide-rich, and in places deep enough to constitute a landscape in its own right. 🇮🇳 ⚠️ **The Aravalli crystallines**, by contrast, sit in a far drier regime with sparse cover; control two supplies little water and control four supplies few organic acids, so chemical alteration is shallow and the mechanical processes of Lesson 2 dominate. The same quartzite and gneiss that would rot to tens of metres in the Ghats stands as bare, angular rock.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ **No regolith thickness is asserted for either region.** No source available to this edition gives a measured weathering depth for any Indian terrain, and the depth profile in this lesson's visual is explicitly an illustrative structure rather than a measurement. ❗ The comparison is a **control-set argument**, and it is stated as one. ⚠️ A second limitation: the Western Ghats' summit laterite and the steep scarp face behave very differently — the dials differ **within** the region, which is itself the point of control three, and the scarp's thin, unstable regolith is the object of Lesson 10 rather than of this one.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What this lesson explains | What it does not |
|---|---|---|
| The six controls | Why the same rock weathers differently in two places, and why soil belts follow climate belts | Any **rate**; the controls are qualitative in both owners and no weathering rate is quoted in this file |
| The soil-cover result | Why chemical weathering is a subsurface process concentrated at the base of the regolith | The **thickness** at which self-deepening stalls, which the sources do not quantify |
| Joint density and corestones | Why weathered granite terrain is boulder-strewn and why hard rock can weather fast | Why a particular joint set exists — 🔁 that is the crust-and-rocks topic's structural geology |

**Strongest objection.** Six controls is a list, and a list is not an explanation. Any factor can be added to such a list, and nothing in it is falsifiable.

**Reply.** The list is falsifiable in the only way that matters: it makes **predictions about where deep regolith will be found**, and those predictions are testable against the landscape. ⚠️ It predicts deep profiles on **old, gentle, well-vegetated, warm, wet surfaces on jointed rock** and thin profiles on **young, steep, bare, cold or dry surfaces on massive rock**. ❗ The Peninsular shield and the Himalayan scarp satisfy the prediction in opposite directions, and so does the contrast between a Ghats summit plateau and its own scarp face a kilometre away. ⚠️ More decisively, the list produces a **counter-intuitive** prediction — that cover **increases** weathering — which the sources confirm; a list that only predicted the obvious would deserve the objection.

⚠️ **Residual.** The **relative weight** of the six controls is not established by either owner, beyond the statement that climate is decisive, and the weights plainly differ by setting: structure dominates in the Deccan trap, time dominates on the Peninsular shield, land use dominates in the Shiwalik. ❗ No ranking beyond the owner's own should be asserted, and an answer that claims a general hierarchy is claiming more than the sources support.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** foundation for the **2021 Prelims demand on black cotton soil formed from a volcanic rock**, because that demand is a parent-material question and this lesson installs parent material as control one; and **foundation for Lesson 16**, whose province map is control one operating at national scale. ❗ No answer letter is recorded or inferred for any objective demand.
- **Probable framing:** as the explanatory engine of any "why does this soil occur here" or "why is this aquifer poor" answer. The controls are rarely the question; they are almost always the reasoning.
- **Traps:** assuming vegetation protects rock; treating hardness rather than joint density as the structural variable; forgetting that steep slopes have thin regolith because material is **removed**, not because weathering is slower; quoting a regolith depth as an Indian value; claiming a general ranking of the six controls.
- **Answer use:** *"Weathering depth is a product of six controls, of which climate is decisive but structure, slope, cover, time and land use are independent; this is why the deep regolith of the Peninsular interfluves and the bare rock of a Himalayan scarp are the same process under different dial settings."* That sentence can open a soil answer, a groundwater answer or a slope-stability answer.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Six controls:** (i) rock structure and hardness (ii) climate (iii) topography and slope (iv) natural vegetation (v) time (vi) **land use by man**. ✅ Figure after **A.H. Strahler**.
- ✅ **Climate is decisive** at world scale; ✅ chemical weathering is *"generally the more important of the two"* families.
- ⚠️ The structural variable is **joint density**, not scratch hardness — joints are the pathway.
- ⚠️ **Steep slope → regolith removed → thin mantle, fresh rock.** Gentle interfluve → retained → deep profile. ❗ Deep regolith is a **plateau and pediment** phenomenon.
- ⚠️ **Time:** profile depth is cumulative, so a deep regolith is a statement about **landscape age and stability**.
- ✅ **Land use by man is a named control** — quarrying, cutting, tillage, irrigation, deforestation. ❗ This makes Lessons 8 and 12 continuous with the weathering block.
- ✅ **A soil cover ENHANCES chemical weathering** — percolating water carries organic acids; soil air is CO₂-enriched.
- ⚠️ Therefore chemical weathering is a **subsurface process** whose most active surface is the **base of the regolith**, the weathering front.
- ⚠️ The profile is **self-deepening until drainage fails**; a thick clay-rich mantle slows its own deepening.
- ✅ **Fig. 4.1 corestones:** joint-bounded blocks attacked from all faces → rounded from outside in → **wide joint spacing leaves an unweathered kernel**; close spacing consumes the block.
- ✅ **Tampin, Negri Sembilan (Malaysia):** in granite, **felspar weathers faster than quartz** → **coarse sandy residue**. ✅ Deep tropical regoliths result on stable surfaces.
- ❗ **Citation rule:** cite Leong **by local-scan page**; give a printed folio or figure number only where visible on the rendered page.
- 🇮🇳 **Western Ghats laterite profile** (wet, warm, forested, old, seasonal) **against Aravalli crystallines** (dry, sparse cover) — the same dials, opposite settings. ❗ **No regolith thickness asserted for either.**
- ⚠️ **Residual:** the relative weighting of the six controls is not established beyond "climate is decisive"; do not assert a general hierarchy.

### Concept check

**Question:** A field party reports that on a Peninsular plateau the granite is rotted to great depth beneath a forested, gently sloping surface, while on the scarp face of the same plateau, two kilometres away, fresh granite is exposed. A member of the party concludes that the scarp rock "resists weathering better". Assess that conclusion.

**Model answer:** The conclusion confuses **the rate of weathering with the fate of its products**, and it is almost certainly wrong. The rock is the same rock; nothing about its mineralogy or joint structure changes over two kilometres in a single granite body, so control one is held constant. What differs is control three, topography and slope. On the gentle plateau surface the regolith is **retained**, so the profile accumulates, and the retention is itself self-reinforcing: the retained mantle supports forest, forest respiration and litter decay load the percolating water with carbon dioxide and organic acids, and that aggressive water attacks the weathering front at the base of the profile — which is why a soil cover enhances rather than protects. On the scarp the regolith is **continuously stripped** by gravity and runoff, so fresh rock is permanently re-exposed and no profile can develop; the absence of a mantle also means no vegetation, no organic acids and no deepening. The correct statement is therefore the reverse of the conclusion: the scarp rock may well be weathering at a comparable or even greater rate per unit of exposed surface, but it never **accumulates** evidence of that weathering, because the product is removed as fast as it is made. A second, subordinate difference is time: the plateau surface is old and stable, while the scarp is a retreating face whose exposure is geologically recent.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read regolith thickness as a direct measure of weathering rate. Thickness is the balance between production at the weathering front and removal at the surface, so a thin mantle may mean slow production or rapid removal, and only the slope and cover tell you which.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Vegetation cover protects rock from weathering." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is true of one process and false of the other, and the distinction carries it. Vegetation does protect rock and regolith from **mechanical detachment and transport**: it intercepts rainfall, reduces raindrop impact, slows runoff and binds the mantle with roots, which is why removing it accelerates erosion. But it **increases chemical weathering**. Litter decay and root respiration enrich soil air in carbon dioxide and release organic acids, so water percolating through a vegetated soil is more aggressive than the rain that fell on it, attacking the weathering front at the base of the profile, not the surface. A soil cover therefore enhances, not prevents, decay of the rock beneath. The qualification is that the two effects operate at different places and timescales — protection at the surface and over seasons, enhancement at depth and over millennia — so the sentence is not simply wrong but imprecisely scoped.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the protective effect correctly conceded for detachment and transport with a named mechanism; 3 for the enhancement of chemical weathering with the carbon-dioxide and organic-acid mechanism stated; 2 for locating the enhancement at the weathering front rather than at the surface; 1 for the scoping qualification distinguishing the two effects by place and timescale; 1 for not asserting any weathering rate or regolith depth. Deduct 3 for treating the statement as simply true, and 2 for asserting a numerical depth.

## Lesson 5 — Regolith becomes soil: pedogenesis, the profile and the soil system

Progress: 5 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Pedogenesis defined; the four components; horizons O, A, E, B and C; the pedon; texture and the triangle; structure and peds; eluviation against illuviation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: core owner §9.1 — the rock-to-soil chain and the **five soil-forming factors** (climate, parent material, relief, organisms, time) with the trap *"soil is not crushed rock"*; §9.2 — the horizon table O/A/E/B/C/R with **eluviation and illuviation** named as the most testable pair; Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*, printed pp.5.6-5.11 (local scan pp.150-155) — the soil system's four components, humus, horizons, the **pedon**, the **USDA texture triangle (1975, Miami silt loam, Indiana)**, soil structure and peds; D.R. Khullar, printed p.196 (local scan p.115) — soil defined, **pedogenesis** named, and the soil-to-population-density argument.
CA search: "soil health soil organic carbon pedogenesis India official report 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in the last six months that belongs to this lesson's object. ❗ Soil-health and nutrient programmes are 🔁 owned by the agriculture owner and are not annexed here merely to produce a linkage. Nil return recorded.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
  THE SOIL PROFILE AS A RECORD OF PROCESS -- read the ARROWS, not the layers

  ===========================================================================
   O  | leaf litter, partly decayed    |  <-- ADDITIONS: organic matter
      | organic, not mineral           |      thick in cool wet forest;
      |                                |      near-absent in desert AND in
      |                                |      hot humid soils (fast decay)
  -----|--------------------------------|
   A   | TOPSOIL: mineral + HUMUS       |  === ELUVIATION ===
      | dark, biologically active      |   |  removal of clay, Fe, Al,
      | ** the layer agriculture uses  |   |  bases, in suspension and
      |    and erosion removes **      |   v  in solution
  -----|--------------------------------|
   E   | pale, strongly LEACHED         |  === maximum eluviation ===
      | diagnostic of PODZOLISATION    |   |  (cool, wet, acidic)
      | under cool wet acid conditions |   v
  -----|--------------------------------|
   B   | SUBSOIL: zone of ILLUVIATION   |  === ILLUVIATION ===
      | clay, iron, aluminium ACCUMULATE|  ^  deposition of what A and E
      | -> hardpans, iron crusts,      |   |  lost. THE PROFILE IS A
      |    kankar where Ca accumulates |   |  TRANSFER SYSTEM, not a
      |                                |   |  stack of independent layers
  -----|--------------------------------|
   C   | WEATHERED PARENT MATERIAL      |  <-- the WEATHERING FRONT of L4
      | rock texture still visible     |      sits at its base
  -----|--------------------------------|
   R   | UNWEATHERED BEDROCK            |  <-- the base of the profile
  ===========================================================================
   capillary rise (evaporation > percolation) REVERSES the arrows -> L6
   calcification, salinisation;  a HIGH WATER TABLE rewrites the whole
   profile -> gleying, peaty and marshy soils  -> the tarai hook below

   THE PEDON: the smallest volume that can be called a soil -- a column
   from surface to parent material, large enough in plan to show the
   full horizon sequence and the lateral variability of its structure.
```

The arrows are the content. ❗ A soil profile is a **transfer system in which the B horizon is made of what the A and E horizons lost**, not a stack of independent layers — which is why eluviation and illuviation are the single most testable pair in the soil block.

### 1. Plain intuition before terminology

⚠️ Start from Lesson 1's regolith. It is loose, unsorted mineral debris lying on its parent rock — and it is **not soil**. Three things must happen to it. ⚠️ **Organic matter must be added**, from plants above and organisms within. **Water must move through it**, carrying material down and, in dry regimes, back up. And **time must pass**, long enough for those transfers to sort the originally uniform debris into visibly different layers.

✅ The core owner states the trap in exactly those terms: *"soil is not 'crushed rock'. Regolith becomes soil only when organic matter, biological activity and water movement have produced **horizon differentiation**. This is exactly why alluvium deposited last season is fertile but immature."*

✅ Khullar supplies the definition and the name: soil is the thin surface layer formed by rock break-down plus decayed organic material, living organisms, water and air, and the process is **pedogenesis**.

### 2. The soil as a system, and its four components

✅ Majid Husain treats soil as a **system with four components** — mineral matter, organic matter, water and air — and this framing is more useful than a definition because it makes the failure modes obvious. ⚠️ If the air fraction is displaced by water, the system goes anaerobic, which is the gleying of waterlogged ground and Lesson 7's peaty and marshy soils. If the organic fraction falls, structure collapses and the mineral fraction is exposed to detachment, which is Lesson 8. If the water fraction carries salts upward instead of downward, the system salinises, which is Lesson 9.

✅ **Humus** is the decomposed, dark, colloidal fraction of the organic component, and it is doing disproportionate work: it supplies nutrient-holding capacity, binds mineral particles into aggregates, and holds water. ⚠️ Its abundance is a **climatic** outcome. In cool temperate grassland, root turnover is high and decay is slow, so humus accumulates to great depth — which is the chernozem of Lesson 6. In hot humid conditions decay is extremely rapid, so despite enormous plant production the soil holds little humus. ❗ That asymmetry is the reason the core owner records the rainforest trap: ✅ *"rainforest soils are not rich"* — the nutrient capital sits in the living biomass and is cycled rapidly, not stored in the soil.

### 3. The horizons, and the transfer that makes them

✅ The owner's horizon table is the controlling version, and it is set out as a transfer rather than a list:

| Horizon | ✅ Content | ⚠️ What the arrows do here | ✅ Exam significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **O** | Surface organic litter | Additions of organic matter | Thick in cool wet forests; **almost absent in deserts and in hot humid soils where decomposition is very fast** |
| **A** | Mineral matter mixed with humus; zone of **eluviation** | **Removal** of clay, iron, aluminium and bases | The **agriculturally critical layer, and the layer lost to erosion** |
| **E** | Strongly leached, pale | Maximum eluviation | **Diagnostic of podzolisation** under cool, wet, acidic conditions |
| **B** | Zone of **illuviation** — accumulation of clay, iron and aluminium washed down from above | **Deposition** of what A and E lost | Where **hardpans and iron-rich crusts** form |
| **C** | Weathered parent material | Little transfer; this is Lesson 4's regolith | **Links the soil to its rock** |
| **R** | Unweathered bedrock | None | The base of the profile |

✅ The owner names the pair explicitly: *"**Eluviation vs illuviation** is the single most testable pair here: material is removed from A/E and deposited in B. Every leached soil type below is a variation on how far this has gone."*

⚠️ Three consequences follow, and they are the ones that convert this table into marks. **One:** the horizons are not independent, so a thick, clay-rich, iron-stained B horizon is **evidence about the A horizon above it**. **Two:** erosion removes the A horizon first, which is why ✅ Khullar's definition of soil erosion — removal *"more rapidly than the various soil forming processes can replace it"* — is a statement about **the most valuable layer, not about volume**. **Three:** where **evaporation exceeds percolation**, the arrows reverse and material moves upward, which is the calcification and salinisation of Lesson 6 and the capillary mechanism of Lesson 9.

### 4. The pedon, texture and structure — three precision tools

✅ **The pedon** is the smallest volume that can properly be called a soil: a three-dimensional column extending from the surface down to the parent material, and large enough in plan to display the full horizon sequence and the lateral variability within it. ⚠️ The concept matters because it fixes the **scale** at which a soil is an object — below it there are only particles, above it there are soil associations and map units. ❗ This is Lesson 1's scale control applied to soil.

✅ **Texture** is the proportion of sand, silt and clay, and Majid Husain presents it through the **USDA soil texture triangle**, dated **1975**, with a worked example of a **Miami silt loam from Indiana**. ⚠️ The triangle is a genuinely useful visual because texture controls four properties at once — water retention, drainage, aeration and workability — and because it shows that the named classes are **regions of a continuum**, not natural kinds.

⚠️ **Substituting an Indian worked example** makes the same point more usefully. Take a black soil of the Deccan: ✅ Khullar records it as **argillaceous with a clay factor of 62 per cent or more**. On the triangle that plots deep in the clay field, and the consequences follow mechanically — very high water retention, very poor drainage, a narrow workable moisture window, and deep cracking on drying. ❗ Those are exactly the properties Lesson 7 attributes to regur, derived here from one number rather than memorised as a list.

✅ **Structure** is the aggregation of particles into **peds** — the units that give a soil its crumb, blocky, platy or prismatic character. ⚠️ Structure is the property that **management changes fastest**: organic-matter loss destroys aggregates, tillage shatters them, and raindrop impact on a bare surface seals the surface into a crust that sheds water. ❗ Lesson 8's sheet erosion begins there, which is why structure is taught here and not in an agronomy file.

### 5. Why soil is the hinge between physical and economic geography

✅ Khullar makes the economic consequence explicit and it is unusually quotable: the **rich deep soils of the Ganga plain and the Kerala coastal plain support high population density, while the shallow coarse soils of Telangana and Rajasthan support only small populations.**

⚠️ This is the claim that justifies a soil block inside a physical-geography topic, and it has a precise form worth preserving: **parent material and climate → pedogenic direction → soil property → crop and settlement pattern.** ❗ The core owner builds a whole ten-mark spine on this chain, reproduced and worked at Lesson 19.

❗ **The necessary qualification, and it is where the marks are.** ⚠️ **Soil sets probabilities, not destiny.** Irrigation, fertiliser, terracing and drainage repeatedly override the natural suitability — at a cost, and often at the cost of the degradation pathways of Lesson 9. ❗ An answer that treats soil as determinative has over-claimed; an answer that states the probability and then names the overriding technology and its cost has given the examiner both halves.

### 6. India hook — the tarai, where a high water table rewrites the profile

🇮🇳 ✅ The tarai belt along the Himalayan foot is the cleanest Indian demonstration that **the water regime, not the parent material, can control the profile**. ✅ Khullar's groundwater account supplies the mechanism: in the **bhabar** belt, streams emerging from the mountains **disappear into unassorted, coarse, highly permeable material**; downslope, in the **tarai**, that water **seeps out again and the water table is high**.

⚠️ The pedological consequence is direct. ⚠️ Where the water table stands within the profile, percolation is impeded and the downward arrows of this lesson's visual are truncated; the lower horizons remain saturated and anaerobic, iron is chemically reduced rather than oxidised, and the profile develops the grey-blue, mottled character the owner names as **gleying**. ⚠️ Organic matter accumulates because decay is slowed by the lack of oxygen, which is the direction that at its extreme produces Lesson 7's peaty and marshy soils. ❗ Two adjacent belts on the same parent material, differing only in their water table, therefore produce very different soils — which is the five-factor model demonstrated under controlled conditions.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ The pedological description above is an **inference from the hydrology that the source states**, not a soil-survey description that any source available to this edition supplies; it is typed as inference for that reason. ⚠️ Second, the bhabar-tarai contrast is a **hydrological boundary with a soil consequence**, and its 🔁 groundwater significance belongs to Lessons 15 and 16, where it reappears as the spring line and as the richest of the eight groundwater provinces. ❗ Nothing about tarai land use, reclamation history or agricultural economics is claimed here.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Regolith (Lesson 1) | Soil (this lesson) |
|---|---|---|
| Composition | Mineral debris only | Mineral + **organic** + water + air |
| Organisation | Unsorted, structureless | **Horizons**, produced by vertical transfer |
| Biological activity | Incidental | **Constitutive** — it is what makes the transfer and the aggregation |
| Time required | As soon as weathering begins | Long enough for **differentiation**; ✅ last season's alluvium is fertile but **immature** |
| Fate | May be stripped, may become soil, may store water | Supports crops; and is the layer erosion removes first |

**Strongest objection.** Soil belongs to agriculture, not to physical geography. A topic on weathering and groundwater has annexed a subject that is not its own.

**Reply.** The annexation would be real if the soil content were agronomic, and it is not. ⚠️ What is taught here is **pedogenesis as the continuation of weathering** — the same water, the same reactions, the same six controls, now organising rather than merely breaking down. ❗ The core owner is explicit that this section exists because *"weathering produces regolith, and regolith becomes soil"* and because **no other file in its folder taught soil formation at all**, although it is the routed owner for the black-soil demand and other files use soil terms as evidence without defining them. ⚠️ The boundary is held in the other direction too: cropping systems, fertiliser policy and nutrient economics are 🔁 routed to the agriculture owner at every point of contact, and only **crop association as a property of the soil group** is retained.

⚠️ **Residual.** The five-factor model is a **framework, not a predictive equation**: it names the variables but does not weight them, and the sources do not supply weights. ❗ In practice the dominant factor differs by setting — parent material dominates on the Deccan basalt, climate dominates across the northern plain's rainfall gradient, relief dominates in the hills, time dominates on the shield — and an answer should say which factor it is invoking rather than reciting all five.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I). ⚠️ The soil block also underpins the agricultural-geography clause, but that clause is 🔁 owned elsewhere and is not claimed here.
- **Verified linkage:** **foundation for the 2021 Prelims demand on black cotton soil formed from a volcanic rock**, which is a parent-material-and-pedogenesis question. ❗ Answer-neutral: no option is recorded or inferred. Also the premise for the core owner's ten-mark soil-as-hinge spine, reproduced at Lesson 19.
- **Probable framing:** the profile and the eluviation-illuviation pair supply the **mechanism paragraph** of any soil-formation or soil-degradation answer; the hinge argument supplies the **significance paragraph**.
- **Traps:** calling soil "crushed rock"; treating horizons as independent layers; assuming a thick O horizon means a fertile soil; assuming hot humid soils are humus-rich; omitting the E horizon's diagnostic meaning; treating soil as determinative of agriculture.
- **Answer use:** *"A soil profile is a transfer system: the B horizon is composed of what the A and E horizons lost, so a thick illuvial horizon is evidence about the leaching above it."* Follow it with the hinge chain — parent material and climate, to pedogenic direction, to property, to crop and settlement — and the answer has both mechanism and significance in four lines.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Pedogenesis**: rock break-down + decayed organic material + living organisms + water + air → soil.
- ✅ ❗ **Soil is not crushed rock.** Regolith becomes soil only on **horizon differentiation**. ✅ Last season's alluvium is **fertile but immature**.
- ✅ **Four components:** mineral matter · organic matter · water · air. ⚠️ Each failure mode is a later lesson: air displaced → gleying; organic lost → erosion; water inverted → salinity.
- ✅ **Humus** = decomposed colloidal organic fraction; holds nutrients, water, and binds aggregates. ⚠️ Abundant in cool grassland, scarce in hot humid soils because **decay is fast**.
- ✅ **Rainforest soils are not rich** — nutrient capital is in the **biomass**, cycled rapidly.
- ✅ **Horizons O · A · E · B · C · R.** ✅ **A = eluviation** (removal), **B = illuviation** (accumulation). **E** = diagnostic of **podzolisation**.
- ✅ ❗ **Eluviation against illuviation is the most testable pair in the soil block.** The B horizon is made of what A and E lost.
- ⚠️ **Erosion removes the A horizon first** — which is why soil erosion is a statement about the most valuable layer, not about volume.
- ⚠️ Where **evaporation exceeds percolation the arrows reverse** → calcification, salinisation (Lessons 6 and 9).
- ✅ **Pedon** = the smallest volume properly called a soil; surface to parent material, wide enough to show the full horizon sequence.
- ✅ **USDA texture triangle, 1975**, worked in the source on a **Miami silt loam, Indiana**. ⚠️ Indian substitution: black soil at **62 per cent or more clay** plots deep in the clay field → high retention, poor drainage, narrow workable window, deep cracking.
- ✅ **Structure = peds.** ⚠️ The fastest property to lose: organic-matter decline, tillage and raindrop crusting destroy aggregates → Lesson 8 begins there.
- ✅ 🇮🇳 **Soil → agricultural prosperity → population density:** deep soils of the **Ganga plain and Kerala coastal plain** support high density; **shallow coarse soils of Telangana and Rajasthan** support small populations.
- ❗ **Soil sets probabilities, not destiny** — irrigation, fertiliser and terracing override natural suitability, at a cost.
- 🇮🇳 **Tarai:** ✅ streams vanish into the **bhabar** and **seep out in the tarai where the water table is high** → ⚠️ truncated percolation, anaerobic lower horizons, gleying, organic accumulation. ❗ The pedology is typed as inference from the stated hydrology.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two soils developed on the same granite under the same rainfall. Soil X has a thin dark A horizon, a bleached pale horizon below it, and a dense reddish-brown horizon beneath that. Soil Y has a uniform dark layer about a metre thick grading directly into weathered rock, with no bleached horizon. What process difference does the pair record, and what can you infer about the vegetation of each site?

**Model answer:** The pair records a difference in **the dominance of vertical leaching against organic accumulation**, and the diagnostic is the bleached horizon. Soil X has a clear **E horizon** — the pale, strongly leached layer — sitting between an eluvial A and an illuvial B, and a dense reddish-brown horizon beneath it where the clay, iron and aluminium removed from above have accumulated. That triplet is the signature of **strong downward transfer under acidic percolating water**, the pattern the owner names as diagnostic of podzolisation, and it implies a cover producing **acidic litter** — coniferous or heath-type vegetation, or in Indian terms a high-rainfall acidic upland — together with percolation consistently exceeding evaporation. Soil Y has no bleached horizon and no accumulation horizon, but a deep, uniformly dark layer, which means organic matter has been **mixed through the profile from within rather than leached through it from above**. That is the signature of **abundant root turnover and intense biological mixing**, implying a **grassland or deep-rooted herbaceous cover** rather than forest, and a regime in which leaching is weaker relative to organic addition. The inference about the parent rock is that it is doing very little work here: both soils are on the same granite, so the difference must be assigned to vegetation and to the balance of water movement, which is exactly what the five-factor model predicts when parent material is held constant.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read a dark surface layer as evidence of fertility and a pale layer as evidence of poverty in the abstract. Colour here is evidence about **process** — darkness records organic accumulation or mixing, paleness records removal — and the agricultural consequence follows only after the process has been identified.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "A soil profile should be read as a record of process rather than as a sequence of layers." Explain, with Indian illustration. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** A profile's horizons are not independent strata but the outcome of transfers, so each horizon is evidence about the others. Material is removed from the A and E horizons by eluviation and deposited in the B horizon by illuviation; a thick, clay-rich, iron-stained subsoil is therefore direct evidence of leaching above it, and a pale E horizon records leaching taken furthest, under cool, wet, acidic conditions. Where evaporation exceeds percolation the transfers reverse, carbonate accumulates and kankar nodules form. India illustrates both directions. In the tarai, a high water table truncates percolation and keeps the lower profile saturated and anaerobic, so iron is reduced and organic matter accumulates rather than decaying. In canal-irrigated tracts of the north-west, capillary rise carries salts upward to the surface. The qualification is that reading a profile gives the direction and intensity of transfer, not its rate, since no horizon carries a date.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for eluviation and illuviation stated as a single transfer with the B horizon identified as its product; 2 for the E horizon's diagnostic meaning given correctly; 2 for the reversal under evaporation with a named product such as kankar; 2 for two contrasting Indian illustrations, one of impeded percolation and one of capillary rise; 1 for the qualification that a profile records direction and intensity but not rate. Deduct 2 for describing horizons as independent layers, and 2 for calling soil crushed rock.

## Lesson 6 — Classifying soils: world schemes, and why India needs its own

Progress: 6 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Zonal, intrazonal and azonal; the six pedogenic directions; Soil Taxonomy's diagnostic-horizon logic; and the limits of a temperate-derived scheme in the tropics

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: core owner §9.3 — the **six pedogenic directions** (podzolisation, laterisation, calcification, salinisation/alkalisation, gleying, humification/melanisation) with their climatic settings and resulting characters, and the rainforest-fertility trap; Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*, printed pp.5.9-5.11 (local scan pp.153-155) — **Soil Taxonomy (1975, US Soil Conservation Service)**, its **eleven orders** with world land-area percentages sourced to Christopherson, *Geosystems*, 1995, p.464, and the **zonal / intrazonal / azonal** scheme with the azonal diagnostic that it **lacks a B horizon**; Majid Husain, printed p.10.34 (local scan p.366) — the Soil Survey Division of India's **seven-type list** with Table 10.17 (Chatterji, 1973 and 1986).
CA search: "soil classification India NBSS LUP taxonomy official update 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in the last six months. ❗ Nil return recorded; a classification lesson does not require a headline and none is manufactured.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
  A DICHOTOMOUS KEY -- classification is a SEQUENCE OF BINARY CHOICES,
  which is why a key and not a table is the right grammar here

  START: does the profile show HORIZON DIFFERENTIATION?
  |
  +-- NO  -> it is REGOLITH or an IMMATURE deposit, not yet a soil (L5)
  |          [the AZONAL class lives here: ** NO B HORIZON **
  |           - alluvium, aeolian sand, fresh volcanic ash, scree
  |           - diagnostic = YOUTH, not climate, not site]
  |
  +-- YES -> is the profile's character set by CLIMATE and VEGETATION
             acting over a long time on a well-drained upland site?
             |
             +-- YES -> ** ZONAL ** : broad belts that follow climate belts
             |          |
             |          +-- cool, humid, coniferous, acid litter
             |          |     -> PODZOLISATION: Fe and Al carried DOWN
             |          |        pale, leached, acidic, needs liming  [taiga]
             |          |
             |          +-- hot, humid tropics, SEASONAL wet-and-dry
             |          |     -> LATERISATION: silica and bases OUT,
             |          |        Fe and Al oxides LEFT; crust HARDENS
             |          |        red, nutrient-poor, tree crops  [India uplands]
             |          |
             |          +-- semi-arid, evaporation > percolation
             |          |     -> CALCIFICATION: CaCO3 ACCUMULATES
             |          |        base-rich; calcrete / KANKAR nodules
             |          |
             |          +-- temperate grassland, deep root turnover
             |                -> HUMIFICATION / MELANISATION
             |                   deep black humus = ** CHERNOZEM **
             |                   the steppe and prairie granaries
             |
             +-- NO  -> ** INTRAZONAL ** : a LOCAL site factor overrides
                        the regional climate
                        |
                        +-- waterlogged, anaerobic, iron REDUCED
                        |     -> GLEYING: grey-blue mottled; peaty, marshy
                        |
                        +-- arid OR irrigated-with-poor-drainage;
                              capillary rise brings salts UP
                              -> SALINISATION / ALKALISATION
                                 ** a HUMAN-INDUCED pathway, not only
                                    a natural one ** -> L9

  SOIL TAXONOMY (1975) ASKS A DIFFERENT QUESTION ENTIRELY:
     not "what climate made this?" but "what DIAGNOSTIC HORIZONS does
     this profile actually have?"  -> 11 orders, defined on measurable
     properties, deliberately GENETIC-ASSUMPTION-FREE
```

❗ The key is the lesson. Zonal, intrazonal and azonal is a **genetic** classification that asks what made the soil; Soil Taxonomy is a **morphometric** one that asks what the soil now is. They are not rival answers to one question; they are answers to two.

### 1. Why classification needs a lesson of its own

⚠️ Classification looks like the least analytical part of the soil block and is in fact where the argument lives, for one reason: **every classification encodes a theory**. ⚠️ A scheme built around climate belts asserts that climate is the master control. A scheme built around diagnostic horizons asserts that we should describe what we can measure and refrain from asserting origin. A scheme built around eight Indian groups asserts that India's soils are not adequately captured by either.

❗ The examinable payoff is the ability to say **why a label fails**, and this lesson provides two worked cases: the chernozem label applied to regur, and the seven-fold Indian list applied against the owner's eight groups.

### 2. Zonal, intrazonal and azonal — and the azonal diagnostic

✅ Majid Husain sets out the classical three-fold genetic scheme, with the diagnostic that matters: **the azonal class lacks a B horizon.**

⚠️ That single criterion is unusually clean and worth holding. **Zonal** soils are mature, well-drained upland soils whose character reflects the **regional climate and vegetation** acting over long time — so zonal soil belts broadly follow climate belts, which is the strongest evidence for climate as the master control. **Intrazonal** soils are mature too, but a **local site factor** — drainage, parent-rock chemistry, salinity — has overridden the regional climate, so they occur as patches within a zonal belt rather than as belts of their own. **Azonal** soils are **immature**: there has not been time for differentiation, so no illuvial B horizon has formed.

⚠️ The Indian application is immediate and is the reason the scheme survives in Indian textbooks. **Alluvial soils are azonal** — annually renewed, texturally variable, with little horizon development, which is exactly why ✅ the core owner describes khadar as newer and flood-renewed and more fertile than the older bhangar, which has had time enough to develop kankar. ⚠️ **Saline and alkaline soils are intrazonal**, because the site's salt regime overrides whatever the regional climate would otherwise have produced. ⚠️ **Laterite and red soils are zonal**, because they record the regional weathering regime.

### 3. The six pedogenic directions

✅ The core owner's section 9.3 gives six directions rather than the usual two, and this is the better treatment because the four additions are the ones India actually needs:

| ✅ Process | ✅ Climatic setting | ✅ What happens | ✅ Resulting soil character |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Podzolisation** | Cool, humid, coniferous forest | Acidic litter mobilises iron and aluminium downward | Pale, leached, acidic, low-fertility; requires liming — the taiga belt |
| **Laterisation** | Hot, humid tropics | Intense leaching removes silica and bases; iron and aluminium oxides are left and **can harden on exposure** | Red, iron-rich, nutrient-poor; hardened crusts; good for tree crops with management, poor for continuous cereals |
| **Calcification** | Semi-arid, low leaching | **Evaporation exceeds percolation**; calcium carbonate accumulates | Base-rich; **calcrete and kankar nodules** |
| **Salinisation / alkalisation** | Arid, **and irrigated land with poor drainage** | **Capillary rise** brings salts to the surface | Saline and alkali soils — ❗ *"a human-induced degradation pathway, not only a natural one"* |
| **Gleying** | Waterlogged, poorly drained | Anaerobic conditions **reduce iron** | Grey-blue mottled; peaty and marshy soils |
| **Humification / melanisation** | Temperate grassland | Deep root turnover builds abundant humus | **Chernozem** — the deep black earths of the steppe and prairie granaries |

⚠️ Four of the six have direct Indian homes: **laterisation** on the Western Ghats and the eastern uplands; **calcification** producing the kankar of older alluvium and of black soil; **salinisation** in the canal tracts of Lesson 9; **gleying** in the deltas and backwaters. ❗ Only podzolisation and full chernozem humification lack genuine Indian zonal expression, and that absence is itself the point of section 5 below.

✅ The owner attaches its rainforest trap to this table, and it belongs here: *"rainforest soils are not rich ... the equatorial biome's luxuriance rests on a rapid nutrient cycle held in the living biomass, not in the soil ... which is the real reason shifting cultivation uses long fallows."* ❗ That last clause is the link to Lesson 8's shifting-cultivation erosion figures and keeps the two lessons from contradicting one another.

### 4. Soil Taxonomy, and what it does differently

✅ Majid Husain presents **Soil Taxonomy**, produced by the **US Soil Conservation Service in 1975**, with **eleven orders** and their world land-area percentages, the percentages themselves sourced to **Christopherson, *Geosystems*, 1995, p.464**.

⚠️ **Use the logic, not the recitation.** The eleven order names are of limited value to an Indian General Studies answer, and reciting their world percentages is worth nothing. ❗ What is worth a mark is the **design principle**: Soil Taxonomy classifies on **diagnostic horizons and measurable properties** rather than on inferred genesis. It asks what the profile demonstrably has, not what climate is presumed to have made it.

⚠️ Two consequences follow. **The scheme is reproducible** — two surveyors applying the criteria to the same pedon should reach the same class, which a genetic scheme cannot guarantee. **And it is deliberately agnostic**, because by 1975 it was clear that many soils are **polygenetic**, carrying the imprint of climates that no longer operate at their site. ❗ That second point is directly relevant to India, where laterite profiles on surfaces of great age may record regimes quite different from today's.

### 5. Why a temperate-derived scheme strains in the tropics

⚠️ Both classical schemes were built in and for the mid-latitudes, and three strains show in the tropics.

- ⚠️ **Depth.** Temperate profiles are typically a metre or two; tropical regoliths can be tens of metres with horizon boundaries far below the zone a temperate scheme was designed to describe.
- ⚠️ **Hardening.** ✅ Laterite *"hardens irreversibly on exposure"* — an engineering state change with no temperate analogue, and a property that no temperate diagnostic horizon captures.
- ⚠️ **Seasonality.** ✅ Laterite requires a **wet-and-dry alternation**, not perpetual wetness; a scheme keyed to mean annual conditions misses a regime defined by its alternation.

### 6. India hook — regur as "tropical chernozem", a label that reveals the bias

🇮🇳 ✅ Majid Husain, in *Geography of India*, printed p.6.7, records the label directly: **black or regur soils are called 'tropical chernozems'**, and he adds that they are **the third largest soil group**, cover **about 50 million hectares or 15 per cent of the reporting area**, derive their **parent material from the weathered rocks of Cretaceous lava**, occupy the **50 to 75 centimetre rainfall belt**, and crack in the dry season to depths of **10 to 15 centimetres**.

⚠️ The label is instructive precisely because it is **half right and half wrong**, and saying which half is which is an answer-grade move. ⚠️ **What it gets right:** both soils are deep, dark, base-rich and agriculturally outstanding, and both underpin a major crop region. ⚠️ **What it gets wrong, and this is the substantive half:** a true chernozem owes its colour and character to **humification — deep humus accumulation from temperate grassland root turnover**, while regur owes both to an entirely different cause. ✅ The core owner states regur's origin plainly: the **weathering of the Deccan basalt under a semi-arid seasonal climate**, producing a clayey soil rich in iron, lime and magnesia and ❗ **poor in nitrogen and organic matter**. ⚠️ A soil that is poor in organic matter cannot be black because of humus. Its colour comes from its **mineral constituents inherited from the basalt**, and its agricultural value comes from **moisture retention plus self-mulching cracking**, not from humus.

❗ **The lesson the label teaches:** a classification built on temperate genesis reached for the nearest temperate analogue and, in doing so, **attributed to regur a cause it does not have**. ❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook:** ⚠️ the term is a **comparative description in the literature, not a formal taxonomic class**, and this edition uses it only as that. ⚠️ Regur's **distribution, chemistry and crop association belong to Lesson 7**; this lesson claims only the classification argument. ⚠️ And ❗ the two area figures for black soil in this file's sources — **about 50 million hectares, 15 per cent of the reporting area** from one book against Lesson 7's independent figures from another — are reported with their sources and **not reconciled**, because the reporting bases differ.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Zonal / intrazonal / azonal | Soil Taxonomy (1975) | The owner's eight Indian groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| Question asked | **What made this soil?** | **What does this profile have?** | **What does India actually contain?** |
| Basis | Climate, site factor, or youth | **Diagnostic horizons and measurable properties** | Parent material, process, property, crop consequence |
| Strength | Explains the **belt pattern** of world soils | **Reproducible**; survives polygenesis | **Usable** for Indian answers; matched to Indian crops |
| Weakness | Assumes genesis that may no longer operate | Order names carry little explanatory force | Not a formal international taxonomy |
| Status here | Taught for its **azonal diagnostic** and belt logic | Taught for its **design principle**, not recited | ❗ **Controlling**, at Lesson 7 |

**Strongest objection.** Three classifications for one set of soils is confusion presented as scholarship. A candidate needs one scheme, and the Indian one is obviously the right choice.

**Reply.** A candidate does need one scheme for answering, and ❗ the owner's eight groups are that scheme. ⚠️ But the other two are not alternatives to be discarded; they are the **sources of the vocabulary that Indian questions use**. "Azonal" explains why alluvial soils lack horizon development. "Intrazonal" explains why saline soils occur as patches rather than belts. "Chernozem" appears in the literature attached to regur and must be assessed rather than repeated. ❗ The ability to say *why* a label is being applied, and where it breaks, is the difference between knowing a classification and understanding one.

⚠️ **Residual.** The three schemes **do not map onto each other cleanly**, and no source available to this edition provides a crosswalk between the owner's eight groups and Soil Taxonomy's orders. ❗ Any such correspondence asserted in an answer would be an invention, and this edition asserts none.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** **foundation for the 2021 Prelims demand on black cotton soil formed from a volcanic geological rock.** ⚠️ The demand is about **parent rock**, and this lesson supplies the reason the chernozem analogy misdirects on exactly that point. ❗ Answer-neutral: the key is unavailable locally and nothing is inferred.
- **Probable framing:** classification rarely appears as a Mains demand in itself. It appears as the **precision layer** — the sentence that shows a candidate knows why a soil is where it is rather than merely that it is.
- **Traps:** reciting Soil Taxonomy's eleven orders and world percentages; calling regur a chernozem without qualification; describing alluvial soils as mature; treating salinisation as purely natural when the owner names it as human-induced; assuming rainforest soils are rich.
- **Answer use:** *"Regur is described in the literature as a tropical chernozem, but the analogy misleads on causation: a chernozem is black from humus accumulated under temperate grassland, while regur is black from mineral constituents inherited from basalt and is in fact poor in organic matter."* That sentence is worth more than any list.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Zonal** = climate and vegetation, mature, well-drained upland → belts follow climate belts. ✅ **Intrazonal** = a local site factor overrides climate → patches. ✅ **Azonal** = immature, ❗ **lacks a B horizon**.
- ⚠️ Indian application: **alluvial = azonal** (khadar newer and more fertile; bhangar older, with kankar); **saline/alkaline = intrazonal**; **laterite and red = zonal**.
- ✅ **Six pedogenic directions:** podzolisation · **laterisation** · **calcification** · **salinisation/alkalisation** · **gleying** · humification/melanisation.
- ✅ ❗ Salinisation is *"a human-induced degradation pathway, not only a natural one"* → Lesson 9.
- ✅ **Calcification** = evaporation exceeds percolation → CaCO₃ accumulates → **calcrete and kankar**.
- ✅ **Gleying** = waterlogged, anaerobic, **iron reduced** → grey-blue mottled; peaty and marshy soils.
- ✅ **Chernozem** = deep black earths from **humification under temperate grassland**; the steppe and prairie granaries.
- ✅ **Rainforest soils are not rich** — nutrient capital in biomass; ❗ the reason shifting cultivation uses long fallows (→ Lesson 8).
- ✅ **Soil Taxonomy (1975, US Soil Conservation Service), 11 orders**, world shares sourced to **Christopherson, *Geosystems*, 1995, p.464**. ⚠️ Use the **design principle** — classification on **diagnostic horizons and measurable properties** — not the recitation.
- ⚠️ Taxonomy is **reproducible** and **agnostic about genesis**, which matters because many soils are **polygenetic**.
- ⚠️ Temperate schemes strain in the tropics on **depth**, **irreversible hardening** and **seasonal alternation**.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Regur = "tropical chernozem"**; ✅ third largest group, **about 50 million ha = 15 % of the reporting area**; ✅ parent material **weathered rocks of Cretaceous lava**; ✅ **50-75 cm** rainfall belt; ✅ dry-season cracks **10-15 cm** deep.
- ❗ **The analogy fails on causation:** chernozem is black from **humus**; regur is black from **basalt-inherited minerals** and is ✅ **poor in nitrogen and organic matter**.
- ❗ **No crosswalk** between the owner's eight groups and Soil Taxonomy's orders exists in any source here; assert none.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Black soil is India's chernozem, formed by the accumulation of humus under grassland, which is why it is so fertile for cotton." Assess the statement and rewrite it correctly.

**Model answer:** The statement reproduces a real label and then supplies the wrong causation for it, so it is wrong in the part that carries the marks. The label is genuine: one source records black or regur soils as *"tropical chernozems"*, and the comparison is reasonable at the level of appearance and agricultural importance, since both are deep, dark, base-rich soils underpinning a major crop region. But the causal claim inverts the facts. A true chernozem is black because **humification under temperate grassland accumulates abundant humus through deep root turnover**. Regur is black for an entirely different reason: it forms by the **weathering of the Deccan basalt under a semi-arid seasonal climate**, and one source records its parent material specifically as **the weathered rocks of Cretaceous lava**. Critically, the owner records regur as **poor in nitrogen and organic matter**, so it cannot be black because of humus; its colour comes from mineral constituents inherited from the basalt. Nor is its cotton suitability a matter of chemical richness. It follows from **very high moisture retention in a clay-rich soil combined with self-mulching cracking** — the dry-season cracks are recorded at ten to fifteen centimetres — which lets a rain-fed crop survive in a fifty-to-seventy-five-centimetre rainfall belt. A corrected version: *"Black or regur soil, sometimes called a tropical chernozem, forms from the weathering of Deccan basalt under a semi-arid seasonal climate; it is clayey and moisture-retentive and cracks deeply on drying, which suits rain-fed cotton, though it is poor in nitrogen and organic matter."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not let a comparative label import its causation. A name borrowed from another continent describes a resemblance, and the resemblance may rest on entirely different processes — which is exactly the weakness of a classification built on temperate genesis.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Classifications of soil are not neutral descriptions; each encodes a theory of what matters." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Each scheme answers a different question and therefore embeds a different claim. The zonal, intrazonal and azonal scheme asserts that **climate is the master control**: zonal soils follow climate belts, intrazonal soils mark where a local factor overrides it, and azonal soils mark where there has not been time for a B horizon to form at all. Soil Taxonomy of 1975 embeds the opposite caution. Classifying on **diagnostic horizons and measurable properties**, it refuses to infer genesis, because many soils are polygenetic, carrying the imprint of regimes that no longer operate there; the price is that its order names explain little. India's eight-group scheme embeds a third claim — that what matters is the chain from **parent material through process to property to crop consequence** — which is why it is the usable scheme for Indian answers. The qualification is that none is false, only differently scoped.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the genetic scheme's climate claim stated with the azonal no-B-horizon diagnostic; 3 for Soil Taxonomy's diagnostic-horizon basis and the polygenesis reason for it; 2 for the Indian scheme's parent-material-to-crop chain identified as its organising claim; 1 for the "differently scoped rather than false" qualification; 1 for one accurate worked example. Deduct 2 for reciting order names or world percentages, and 2 for treating any scheme as simply correct.
