Two layers read against each other. ❗ The strip is deliberately built to be **laid beside Lesson 16's province map**, because the same six parent materials that produce eight soils produce eight groundwater provinces, and that pairing is the integrating argument of this file.

### 1. The six parent-material classes

✅ Khullar sets out the lithological foundation of Indian pedology as **six classes**: (i) ancient crystalline and metamorphic rocks; (ii) Cuddapah and Vindhyan rocks; (iii) Gondwana rocks; (iv) Deccan basalts; (v) Tertiary and Mesozoic sedimentary rocks of extra-peninsular India; (vi) recent and sub-recent rocks.

⚠️ Three observations convert that list into an argument. **One:** the classes are **geological-historical**, running from the oldest shield to the youngest alluvium, so the soil map is partly a map of India's geological history. **Two:** the classes are **spatially coherent**, which is why Indian soil belts are large and mappable rather than patchy. **Three:** ❗ **the same six classes reappear at Lesson 16 as the basis of India's eight groundwater provinces** — because the lithology that decides how a rock weathers into soil also decides how it stores and transmits water. ⚠️ That is not a coincidence or an analogy; it is the same physical fact observed through two different interfaces.

### 2. The eight groups, with their quantified anchors

✅ The core owner's eight groups control, and each is taught as **parent material → process → property → crop consequence**.

**Alluvial.** ✅ Deposited by rivers; the northern plains, coastal plains and deltas; **India's most extensive soil group**. ✅ Highly variable texture, generally fertile; **khadar** is newer and flood-renewed and more fertile than **bhangar**, which is older and often carries kankar. ✅ It is the foundation of the rice-wheat system, with fertility maintained by annual renewal but **exposed to waterlogging and salinity under canal irrigation** — which is the handover to Lesson 9.

**Black / regur.** ✅ Formed by weathering of the **Deccan basalt** under a semi-arid seasonal climate. ✅ Khullar's chemistry is precise and quotable: **argillaceous, with a clay factor of 62 per cent or more**, about **10 per cent iron oxide**, **6 to 8 per cent alumina**, **9 to 10 per cent lime and magnesium carbonates**, **potash under 0.5 per cent**, with **kankar nodules** present. ✅ Majid Husain adds that it is the **third largest group**, about **50 million hectares or 15 per cent of the reporting area**, in the **50 to 75 centimetre rainfall belt**, cracking **10 to 15 centimetres** deep in the dry season. ❗ The owner's trap is binding: black soil derives from **basaltic lava**, not from "black minerals" and not from alluvium; and its cotton suitability follows from **moisture retention plus self-mulching cracking**, not from chemical richness — it is **poor in nitrogen and humus**, as the potash figure under 0.5 per cent independently indicates.

**Red and yellow.** ✅ Crystalline igneous and metamorphic rocks of the Peninsula under moderate rainfall; red from iron oxide, yellow where hydrated; light-textured and low in nitrogen, phosphorus and humus. ✅ Khullar quantifies: **3.5 lakh square kilometres, 10.6 per cent of the area**, colour from **diffused iron**, with **90 to 100 per cent oxides of iron, aluminium, titanium and manganese**. ✅ The soil responds strongly to fertiliser and irrigation; without them it supports millets, pulses and oilseeds.

**Laterite.** ✅ Intense leaching under a **seasonally alternating wet and dry** tropical climate on high-rainfall uplands; iron-and-aluminium rich, acidic, **hardening irreversibly on exposure**. ✅ Khullar's account supplies the historical and definitional anchors: from the Latin ***later*, meaning brick**; first applied by **Buchanan in 1810 in Malabar**; **Fermor**'s restriction of the term; **George Kuriyan**'s definition as the *"end product of decomposition found in regions of heavy rainfall, more than 200 cm"*; extent **2.48 lakh square kilometres**. ✅ Its usability detail is examinable: laterite **can be cut easily with a spade while in the ground but hardens irreversibly on exposure to air**, which is the basis of its use as building material; it has **low base-exchange capacity and poor fertility**, yet supports **tea, coffee, rubber, cinchona, coconut and arecanut**, and **paddy in low-lying areas**.

**Arid / desert.** ✅ Aeolian and weathering products of the north-western dry belt; sandy, low in organic matter, often calcareous or saline at depth; productive **only with reliable irrigation and then at high salinisation risk**. ✅ Khullar: **1.42 lakh square kilometres, 4.32 per cent**, with **aeolian sand 90 to 95 per cent** and **clay 5 to 10 per cent**, and the **Indira Gandhi Canal Command Area** as the utility case. ❗ **Discrepancy declared (and not resolved):** Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, printed p.6.7, gives desert soils as **about 15 million hectares, 4.42 per cent**. ❗ **Both figures are reported with both sources. They are not averaged, and neither is silently preferred.** ⚠️ Once converted, **1.42 lakh square kilometres = 14.2 million hectares**, so the percentages and absolute areas differ modestly on unstated reporting bases — and no source available to this edition states those bases.

**Saline and alkaline.** ✅ Arid climates and, critically, **poorly drained irrigated tracts**; salt or sodium accumulation at the surface; reclaimed by drainage, gypsum and leaching. ✅ Khullar gives the extent as **68,000 square kilometres** and supplies the nomenclature and the mechanism, treated in section 3. ❗ The owner's framing is important: this is **a degradation class as much as a soil class**, which is why Lesson 9 takes it up again rather than this lesson closing it.

**Peaty and marshy.** ✅ Waterlogged, high-organic settings in humid deltas and backwaters; very high organic content, acidic, anaerobic. ✅ Khullar quantifies the organic fraction at **10 to 40 per cent** and names the soil ***kari*** in **Kottayam and Alappuzha**, recording it also in **coastal Odisha and Tamil Nadu, the Sunderbans, Bihar and Almora**. 🔁 Its **wetland ecosystem** significance is owned by the lakes-and-wetlands topic and is not taught here.

**Forest and mountain.** ✅ Hill slopes under forest cover; thin, immature, with texture varying sharply with altitude and slope; **highly erosion-prone once cleared**; suited to horticulture and plantations on terraces. ✅ Khullar: **2.85 lakh square kilometres, 8.67 per cent**. ❗ The erosion-proneness clause is the handover to Lesson 8 and, through it, to Lessons 12 and 13.

### 3. The saline nomenclature and its mechanism

✅ Khullar records the Indian vernacular names for saline efflorescences — **reh, kallar, usar, thur, rakar, karl and chopan** — and the mechanism behind them, which is Lesson 6's salinisation direction worked in detail: **salts carried in solution by rivers percolate into the sub-soils, and then rise by capillary action under dry-season evaporation in canal-irrigated areas and wherever the sub-soil water table is high.**

✅ The extent figures are specific and usable: **about 1.25 million hectares in Uttar Pradesh and 1.21 million hectares in Punjab are affected by *usar***. ✅ A separate coastal mechanism operates in the **Gulf of Khambhat**, where **salt-laden sea tides** have rendered the **Narmada, Tapi, Mahi and Sabarmati estuaries** infertile.

❗ The sentence to carry forward is the conditional: **capillary rise requires evaporation to exceed percolation and the water table to be near enough to the surface for capillary continuity.** ⚠️ Both conditions are **created by canal irrigation without drainage**, which is why Lesson 9 treats salinity as an engineering-and-governance failure rather than a natural hazard.

### 4. The characteristics and problems of Indian soils, stated as a system

✅ Khullar's eight **characteristics** of Indian soils are worth holding as a set because they frame every degradation argument that follows: formed under varied geographical conditions and differing widely in physical properties, chemical composition and fertility; **most soils old and mature**, with peninsular plateau soils much older than those of the great northern plain; **largely deficient in nitrogen, mineral salts, humus and other organic materials**; thick in plains and valleys and thin on hills and plateaux; alluvial and black soils fertile while laterite, desert and alkaline soils are not; **centuries of cultivation have cost fertility** and demand scientific treatment; **seasonal rainfall makes dry-period irrigation necessary**; and erosion with its allied problems is pervasive.

✅ The **eight "Problems of Indian Soils"** follow directly: (i) soil erosion (ii) deficiency in fertility (iii) desertification (iv) waterlogging (v) salinity and alkalinity (vi) wasteland (vii) **over-exploitation from population growth and rising living standards** (viii) **encroachment of agricultural land by urban and transport development**.

⚠️ The list is more useful read as a **system than as eight items**, and the system has two drivers. **Problems (i), (iii) and (vi) are removal problems** — material leaves. **Problems (ii), (iv) and (v) are accumulation or depletion problems within the profile** — the wrong thing arrives or the right thing is exhausted. ❗ **Problems (vii) and (viii) are demand problems**, and naming them separately is what prevents a soil answer from reading as though degradation were purely physical. 🔁 Desertification is owned by the arid-processes topic and by the environment owner, and is not developed here beyond its appearance in this list and the single water-table cross-link made at Lesson 9.

### 5. The superseded classification, named and dated

❌ ✅ **Normalisation, declared.** Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*, printed p.10.34, gives the **Soil Survey Division of India's seven-type list** with **Table 10.17**, sourced to **Chatterji, 1973 and 1986**. ❗ **The core owner's eight groups control.** ⚠️ The seven-fold list is named here as a **superseded alternative classification with its date**, not as an error and not silently dropped.

❗ **Uncertainty disclosed.** ⚠️ Two rows of Table 10.17 — Mountain and Desert — are **garbled in the available text layer**, reading "Mountain Soil Desert soil 18.2 5.51" and then "Desert Soil 14.6 4.42", although the table's percentages sum to 100.00. ❗ **Those two rows are not quoted anywhere in this file.** The table is used only for its **structure** — that is, as evidence that an official seven-fold Indian classification with area shares existed and was dated — and for no numerical value.

### 6. India hook — the Rajmahal Hills as the eastern limit of regur

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The eastern margin of the black-soil belt is not an agricultural boundary or an administrative one. It is a **lithological boundary that happens to be visible as a soil boundary**, and the **Rajmahal Hills**, where Deccan-type basaltic trap rock gives way eastward to the alluvium of the Ganga's lower course, is its clearest expression.

⚠️ The argument is tight because every step is already established. ✅ Regur's parent material is **the weathered rocks of Cretaceous lava**. ✅ Parent material is one of Khullar's six classes and one of the owner's five soil-forming factors. ⚠️ Therefore the soil **cannot occur where the lava does not**, regardless of climate — and the climatic gradient across this boundary is far gentler than the lithological one. ❗ The boundary is therefore a **natural experiment**: parent material varies sharply while climate varies gently, and the soil follows the parent material.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook, and it is a real one.** ⚠️ Parent-material control is **strongest where the rock is distinctive**, which basalt is; over most of India the boundaries between soil groups are **gradational and climatically mediated**, and a candidate who generalises from this case to the whole soil map will be wrong. ⚠️ Second, the **trap outliers of the Rajmahal region are not continuous with the main Deccan province**, and this edition claims only the boundary argument, not a correlation of flow stratigraphy — 🔁 that is the crust-and-rocks topic's material. ⚠️ Third, no source available to this edition provides a mapped eastern limit line, so the hook is a **structural argument about the control, not a cartographic claim**.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Parent-material control | Climatic control |
|---|---|---|
| Strongest where | The rock is **distinctive** — basalt, pure limestone, fresh alluvium | The rock is **ordinary** and the climatic gradient is steep |
| Indian case | **Regur on the Deccan trap**; its eastern limit at the Rajmahal boundary | The alluvial plain's west-to-east gradient from arid to humid soils on one parent material |
| Soil consequence | A **sharp** soil boundary coinciding with a rock boundary | A **gradational** soil sequence with no sharp line |
| ❗ Error it prevents | Attributing regur to climate alone | Attributing every boundary to lithology |

**Strongest objection.** This lesson is a catalogue of figures. Area percentages and oxide fractions are Prelims trivia, and a Mains answer gains nothing from them.

**Reply.** The objection is right about **reciting** them and wrong about **using** them. ⚠️ Each quantified anchor here is load-bearing for an argument rather than decorative. **Clay at 62 per cent or more** is the number that explains moisture retention, cracking and the narrow workable window — three properties from one figure. **Potash under 0.5 per cent** is the number that refutes the chemical-richness explanation of regur's cotton suitability. **Aeolian sand at 90 to 95 per cent** is the number that explains why desert soil is productive only under reliable irrigation and why it salinises when irrigated badly. **Ten to forty per cent organic matter** is what makes peaty soil a different kind of object rather than a wetter version of alluvium. ❗ The discipline is to **quote a figure only where it does work in the sentence**, and this file enforces that by excluding the national aggregates at section 7 of the coverage matrix precisely because they do none.

⚠️ **Residual.** The area figures in this lesson come from **different sources with different and unstated reporting bases**, which is why the desert-soil discrepancy exists and why it is left unresolved. ❗ More generally, **none of these figures carries an assessment year or a methodology**, which is exactly what the owner's date-and-data control demands of groundwater statistics — and the asymmetry is uncomfortable. ⚠️ The honest treatment is to quote them **as that book's figures**, not as current official values, and this file does.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** ❗ **concept route for the 2021 Prelims General Studies Paper I demand on black cotton soil formed from a volcanic geological rock.** ⚠️ The lesson installs the distinction the demand turns on — **parent rock and pedogenesis** — without resolving the question; **the official key is unavailable locally and no answer is inferred**. The lesson also supplies the evidence base for the owner's ten-mark soil-as-hinge spine at Lesson 19.
- **Probable framing:** as the **evidence layer** of any soil, agricultural-geography or land-degradation answer, and as the geography half of a question on regional crop patterns.
- **Traps:** attributing regur to black minerals, to alluvium or to humus; treating laterite as a perpetually-wet-climate soil; describing alluvial soils as uniformly fertile without the khadar-bhangar distinction; averaging the two desert-soil area figures; quoting the garbled table rows; presenting the seven-fold list as current.
- **Answer use:** build the chain and attach one figure to each link. *"Deccan basalt weathers under a semi-arid seasonal climate to a soil with a clay fraction of sixty-two per cent or more, which retains moisture and cracks ten to fifteen centimetres deep on drying, sustaining rain-fed cotton in a fifty-to-seventy-five-centimetre rainfall belt — although the same soil carries under half a per cent potash and is poor in nitrogen and humus."* Parent material, process, property, crop and limitation in one sentence.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Six parent-material classes:** (i) ancient crystalline and metamorphic (ii) Cuddapah and Vindhyan (iii) Gondwana (iv) **Deccan basalts** (v) Tertiary and Mesozoic sedimentaries of extra-peninsular India (vi) recent and sub-recent.
- ❗ **The same six reappear at Lesson 16 as India's eight groundwater provinces.** One lithological framework, two interfaces.
- ✅ **Eight groups:** alluvial · black/regur · red and yellow · laterite · arid/desert · saline and alkaline · peaty and marshy · forest and mountain.
- ✅ **Regur:** clay **≥ 62 %**; **10 %** iron oxide; **6-8 %** alumina; **9-10 %** lime and Mg carbonates; **potash < 0.5 %**; kankar nodules. ✅ Third largest, **~50 m ha = 15 %** of reporting area; **50-75 cm** rainfall; cracks **10-15 cm**.
- ❗ Regur = **basaltic lava**, not black minerals, not alluvium. Cotton suitability = **moisture retention + self-mulching cracking**, not richness.
- ✅ **Red and yellow:** **3.5 lakh sq km = 10.6 %**; colour from **diffused iron**; **90-100 %** oxides of Fe, Al, Ti, Mn.
- ✅ **Laterite:** Latin ***later*** = brick; **Buchanan, 1810, Malabar**; **Fermor**'s restriction; **Kuriyan** — *"end product of decomposition ... more than 200 cm"*; **2.48 lakh sq km**; cuts with a spade in the ground, **hardens irreversibly**; low base-exchange; tea, coffee, rubber, cinchona, coconut, arecanut, and paddy in low ground.
- ✅ **Desert:** **1.42 lakh sq km = 4.32 %**; aeolian sand **90-95 %**, clay **5-10 %**; **Indira Gandhi Canal Command Area**. ❗ **A second source gives ~15 million ha = 4.42 %. Both reported; neither averaged.**
- ✅ **Saline and alkaline: 68,000 sq km.** ✅ Names: **reh, kallar, usar, thur, rakar, karl, chopan**. ✅ **UP ~1.25 m ha and Punjab ~1.21 m ha** under *usar*. ✅ **Gulf of Khambhat** — salt-laden tides; **Narmada, Tapi, Mahi, Sabarmati** estuaries infertile.
- ✅ **Mechanism:** salts percolate in solution, then **rise by capillary action** under dry-season evaporation where irrigation is canal-fed or the sub-soil water table is high.
- ✅ **Peaty and marshy: 10-40 % organic matter**; ***kari*** in **Kottayam and Alappuzha**; also coastal Odisha, Tamil Nadu, Sunderbans, Bihar, Almora.
- ✅ **Forest and mountain: 2.85 lakh sq km = 8.67 %**; thin, immature, **highly erosion-prone once cleared**.
- ✅ **Eight problems:** erosion · fertility deficiency · desertification · waterlogging · salinity and alkalinity · wasteland · **over-exploitation from population growth** · **urban and transport encroachment**. ⚠️ Read as removal + accumulation + **demand**.
- ❌ ✅ **Superseded:** the **Soil Survey Division's seven-type list**, Table 10.17, **Chatterji 1973 and 1986**. ❗ Named and dated; the owner's eight control. ❗ **Its Mountain and Desert rows are garbled and are not quoted.**
- 🇮🇳 **Rajmahal Hills = the eastern limit of regur** — a lithological boundary that is also a soil boundary. ❗ Parent-material control is strongest where the rock is distinctive; most Indian soil boundaries are gradational.

### Concept check

**Question:** A planner proposes extending canal irrigation into a desert-soil tract on the argument that the soil's only limitation is water. Using the soil's recorded properties, state what the proposal gets right, what it misses, and what single engineering provision would decide the outcome.

**Model answer:** The proposal is right about the primary limitation and wrong about it being the only one. **What it gets right:** desert soils are recorded as sandy, low in organic matter and productive only with reliable irrigation, and one source gives the composition as roughly ninety to ninety-five per cent aeolian sand against five to ten per cent clay. With water supplied, the limitation on plant growth is relieved, and the Indira Gandhi Canal Command Area is the standing demonstration that the transformation is real. **What it misses:** the same source records desert soils as **often calcareous or saline at depth**, and the owner records that irrigation of such soils carries **high salinisation risk**. The mechanism is Lesson 7's capillary chain: canal water raises the sub-soil water table, dry-season evaporation then exceeds percolation, and salts already present at depth are carried **upward** in solution and deposited at the surface as efflorescence. A very sandy soil makes this worse in one respect and better in another — infiltration is rapid, so seepage recharge is efficient and the table rises quickly, but hydraulic conductivity also makes leaching easier if there is somewhere for the leachate to go. **The decisive provision is drainage.** Irrigation without an adequate drainage outlet converts a water-limited soil into a salt-limited one; irrigation with drainage keeps the water table below the depth at which capillary continuity to the surface can be maintained, which is the condition that makes the entire salinisation pathway impossible.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat salinity as an intrinsic property of arid soil that irrigation simply reveals. Salinisation is a **transport outcome** that requires a near-surface water table and evaporative demand, both of which unlined canal irrigation creates — which is why it is correctly described as a human-induced degradation pathway.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "India's soil map is, to a first approximation, a map of its rocks." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim holds strongly in some places and weakly in others. It holds where the parent rock is distinctive. Black or regur soil forms from the weathering of Deccan basalt, so its distribution coincides with the trap country and its eastern margin is a rock boundary rather than a climatic one; red and yellow soils follow the crystalline and metamorphic rocks of the Peninsula; alluvial soils follow the recent and sub-recent deposits of the plains. One source's six parent-material classes map recognisably onto the soil belts. It holds weakly where the rock is ordinary and climate varies sharply. Across the northern plain the parent material is broadly uniform alluvium, yet soils grade from arid and saline in the north-west to humid in the east, a sequence produced entirely by rainfall. The qualification is that relief, time and human land use intervene everywhere, so lithology sets the first approximation only.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for two soil groups correctly tied to named parent rock; 2 for the six parent-material classes invoked as the lithological framework; 3 for the counter-case of the alluvial plain's climatic gradient on uniform parent material; 1 for relief, time or land use named as further controls; 1 for a graded verdict using the "first approximation" framing rather than endorsement or rejection. Deduct 2 for attributing regur to anything other than basaltic lava, and 2 for presenting lithology as the sole control.

A monotonic intensity series with real places attached, and beneath it the cascade. ❗ **This is the file's structural spine.** Two books that do not share an author, a publisher or a dataset independently list *lowered water table* and *increased landslides* among the effects of soil erosion — which is why this topic is one topic.

### 1. The definition, and what it smuggles in

✅ Khullar's definition is deceptively precise: soil erosion is the removal of soil by the forces of nature *"more rapidly than the various soil forming processes can replace it."*

⚠️ Two things are smuggled in, and both earn marks. **One:** the definition is **comparative, not absolute**. Some erosion always occurs and is matched by pedogenesis; erosion becomes a problem only when the **rate** exceeds replacement. ❗ That is why a land-management answer is about **rates and balances**, not about eliminating erosion. **Two:** the definition implies that the relevant quantity is **soil as an organised body**, not mass. ⚠️ Lesson 5 established that the A horizon holds the humus, the nutrients and the structure; sheet erosion removes exactly that layer, so a small depth lost is a large proportion of the value lost.

✅ Khullar adds the perceptual problem that makes the whole subject difficult: the process is **imperceptibly slow, yet the cumulative loss is very great**.

### 2. The water-erosion ladder, rung by rung

✅ **Splash.** Raindrops fall at about **10 metres per second**, and **sands and silts detach most readily**. ⚠️ Two effects follow: particles are thrown into the air and move downslope on a sloping surface, and the impact **destroys surface aggregates and seals the surface**, so infiltration falls and runoff rises — which is the supply side of every rung above.

✅ **Sheet.** Uniform removal across the surface. ⚠️ It is **the most dangerous rung precisely because it is invisible**: there is no channel to see, no gully to photograph, and the loss is detected only by comparison over years. ✅ Khullar's anchor converts the invisibility into arithmetic: **one cubic metre per hectare per week, sustained for thirty years, equals fifteen centimetres off the entire surface.** ❗ That is a quantified anchor of exactly the kind this file encourages, because it does work: it shows how an imperceptible weekly loss becomes the complete removal of the A horizon within one working lifetime.

✅ **Rill.** The first defined channels, small enough to be erased by ordinary tillage. ⚠️ The rill stage is the **last cheap intervention point**, which is the practical content of the rung.

✅ **Gully.** Channels deepen and, critically, **extend headward**, which is why gullies capture ever more catchment and why they cannot be treated by working only at their mouth.

✅ **Ravine.** Depth **may reach 30 metres or more**.

✅ **Badland topography.** Characterised by **an immediate drop of three to four metres**, with the land effectively out of cultivation.

✅ **The rainfall insight, which is the lesson's best transferable sentence:** it is **not total annual rainfall that matters but how and when it comes** — a single heavy downpour of a few hours can do what the same depth spread over a season cannot. ⚠️ This is also the point of contact with Lessons 12 and 13: intensity, not total, is what drives both erosion and slope failure, which is why the two hazards share a trigger.

### 3. The hydraulic scaling law

✅ Khullar states it as a single sentence: *"Theoretically, if the rate of flow is doubled, the carrying capacity is increased four times, the scouring capacity thirty two times and the size of the particles carried sixty four times."*

⚠️ **How to use it, and how not to.** ❗ Do not present it as a measured field relationship; the source itself says *"theoretically"*, and this edition quotes it as the source's statement. ⚠️ What it establishes is **non-linearity**, and non-linearity is the policy-relevant fact: a modest increase in runoff velocity produces a very large increase in erosive work. ❗ From that single fact the entire conservation logic of Lesson 9 is derivable — contour bunding, terracing, strip cropping and vegetative cover all work by **reducing runoff velocity and slope length**, and because the relationship is steeply non-linear, a modest reduction in velocity buys a disproportionate reduction in damage.

⚠️ It also explains why **slope length matters as much as slope angle**: velocity accumulates downslope, so a long gentle slope can deliver more erosive work at its base than a short steep one.

### 4. Wind erosion as a separate machine

✅ Khullar treats wind erosion as a distinct system, accentuated when soil is **dry, weakly aggregated and bare**, with three transport modes and one residue:

- ✅ **Suspension** — humus, clay and silt, held aloft. ⚠️ This is the **most damaging mode**, because it removes exactly the fine and organic fraction on which fertility depends, and exports it from the region entirely.
- ✅ **Saltation** — fine to medium sand, moved in **bounds and leaps**. ⚠️ Saltating grains strike the surface and dislodge further particles, so the process is self-amplifying once started.
- ✅ **Surface creep** — coarse sand, **rolled along**.
- ✅ **Desert pavement** — very coarse sand of **1.0 to 2.0 millimetres**, gravels, peds and clods are too large to roll, so the surface is left **armoured with fragments larger than 1.00 millimetre**.

✅ He names **sea erosion and glacial erosion** as two further natural agents. 🔁 Coastal erosion is owned by the coasts topic and glacial erosion by the glacial topic; both appear here **only as agents of soil loss** and are developed no further.

🔁 ⚠️ **Boundary, stated:** the **aeolian processes of desertification** are owned by the arid-processes topic and by the environment owner. ❗ This lesson owns **wind erosion as a soil-degradation process**, and the 2026 General Studies Paper I demand on aeolian processes in desertification is 🔁 routed, with the transport modes above available to the owning topic if it needs them.

### 5. India's erosion geography, and three map assets

✅ **Fig. 7.2 INDIA: SOIL EROSION** is reproduced below in text, with its legend classes exactly as the source gives them.

                                            ravine belt of UP, MP,
                                            Rajasthan, Gujarat

                                            interior; the Deccan;
                                            the plains
      [3] desert (aeolian) erosion ........ the arid north-west

          CULTIVATION .................... the north-east
      [5] glacial erosion ................. the higher Himalaya
      [6] coastal erosion ................. the littoral  [-> routed]

                                            which aeolian erosion is
                                            not mapped

⚠️ The seventh legend entry is the most answer-worthy, because it is a **line rather than a class**: the map asserts an **eastern limit of wind erosion**, which converts a vague "arid north-west" into a mappable boundary. ❗ It is one of the ten items in this file's minimum India map-marking set at Lesson 19.

❗ **Uncertainty disclosed (U1).** ⚠️ **Table 7.1 carries no visible source line on the printed page.** The attributions adjacent to it on the same page are the Ministry of Home Affairs working group and the Chambal Development Scheme aerial survey, ❗ **but the table is not attributed to either here, because the page does not do so.** The table is reproduced with that limitation stated rather than with a source invented for it.

✅ The ravine geography itself is specific and is attributed where the source attributes it:

- ✅ A **Ministry of Home Affairs working group of 1971** found **39.75 lakh hectares** of ravines across **18 states**, of which **27.65 lakh hectares, or 69.55 per cent**, lie in **Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Gujarat**.
- ✅ The **Chambal Development Scheme** aerial survey put about **50,600 hectares** of ravines at **4.5 to 6.0 metres** depth.
- ✅ **Madhya Pradesh** has **4 to 8 lakh hectares** along the **Chambal and Kali Sindh**, of which **2.4 lakh hectares** lie in **Gwalior, Morena and Bhind**.
- ✅ In **Uttar Pradesh** ravines follow the **Chambal, Yamuna, Gomati and Son**, with **Agra, Etawah and Jalaun** worst affected.
- ✅ The **Chambal-Yamuna badlands cover about 32 lakh hectares and are the product of 1,000 years of erosion at about 0.25 tonnes removed daily.**

✅ **Fig. 6.6 Gully Erosion in Chambal Valley**, from Majid Husain's *Geography of India*, printed p.6.12, is a large-scale sketch map that distinguishes **ravines and bluff** from **cultivated land**. ⚠️ Its value is that it shows the ravine problem as a **land-use boundary**: cultivated land abuts bluff, and the bluff is advancing. ✅ The same source records that gully erosion is also significant in the **Shiwalik tracts of Punjab, Haryana, Himachal Pradesh, Jammu and Kashmir, Uttarakhand and Uttar Pradesh, along the southern slopes of the Himalayas, and in the Western and Eastern Ghats** — which shows that the ravine problem is not a Chambal peculiarity.

✅ **Sediment-transfer anchors.** The **Ganga carries about 30 million tonnes of eroded material per year** from the Ganga plain to the Bay of Bengal; the **Brahmaputra about 10 million tonnes**. ✅ And in coastal terms, of **Kerala's 560-kilometre coast, about 32 kilometres of sandy beach is subject to severe sea erosion**, evidenced by the **uprooting of coconut trees**. 🔁 The coastal figure is used **only as an agent-of-soil-loss datum**; coastal erosion as a process and a management problem is owned elsewhere.

### 6. The human factors — and the quantitative exclusions, declared

✅ Khullar's human factors are the examinable content of this section:

- ✅ **Deforestation**, with the ***chos* of the Shiwalik** as the type case: **nearly a hundred streams debouch across 130 kilometres**, worst in **Hoshiarpur**.
- ✅ **Overgrazing**, in hill **Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand and Jammu and Kashmir**, and in dry **Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh**.
- ✅ **Faulty agriculture and shifting cultivation** — more than **15 lakh hectares** of forest cleared every year, total affected area **45 lakh hectares**, with **207,287 hectares in Assam, 41,963 hectares in Tripura, 21,862 hectares in Manipur and 33,08,502 hectares in Odisha**.
- ✅ **The Nilgiri potato case** — cultivation on slopes **exceeding 60 per cent**, with yields down **about 50 per cent** despite heavy fertiliser, and the engineering rule that **land steeper than 1 in 4 should not be cultivated but put under grasses and trees**.

❗ **Exclusions, declared in full, because the exclusion is itself the method lesson.** ✅ The core owner's section 9.5 carries a binding caution: *"do not quote a national percentage of degraded land, a soil-loss rate in tonnes per hectare, an ICAR survey figure or a soil-organic-carbon value from memory."* ⚠️ This edition converts that into named exclusions, and the reason is that the books themselves are **internally contradictory on exactly these numbers**:

- ❌ **Excluded.** Khullar's printed p.207 states that **"about 21 million hectares"** are subject to severe wind erosion and, **two paragraphs later on the same printed page**, that the figure is **"about 45 million hectares"**. ❗ A single page cannot be used as authority for two incompatible values, and this file quotes neither.
- ❌ **Excluded.** The same page's national aggregates — "over 80 million hectares or about one-fourth of our total area" exposed, "40 million hectares" seriously eroded, and "about 40,000 hectares" permanently lost each year — travel with the contradicted figures and are excluded with them.
- ❌ **Excluded.** The ICAR line that *"the loss due to water erosion is 53.34 million hectares annually"* has **incoherent units**: hectares are an area, not an annual loss rate.
- ❌ **Excluded.** Majid Husain's printed p.5.11 global and Indian soil-loss statistics — **1.2 billion hectares degraded globally**, attributed to the **GLASOD dataset (UNEP and ISRIC, *Map of Status of Human-induced Soil Degradation*, Sheet 2, Nairobi, 1990)**; "75,000 million tonnes of productive soil eroded annually"; and **"in India about 6,000 million tonnes of soil eroded annually"** un-attributed. ❗ The dataset is of 1990 vintage and the Indian figure carries **no attribution at all**.
- ❌ **Excluded.** Un-attributed and undated desert figures — "about 8 million tonnes of soil has been removed from every square kilometre area in desert during the last hundred years", and "34 lakh tonnes of fertile soils is removed by wind every year in the districts of Jodhpur, Bikaner, Kota, Jaipur, Bharatpur, Kishangarh".

❗ **What to use instead, and it is better material.** ⚠️ Where a candidate needs scale, use the **structural** facts that this lesson does carry: the **state-wise ravine table**, the **1971 working group's 39.75 lakh hectares across 18 states with 69.55 per cent in four states**, the **sheet-erosion arithmetic of fifteen centimetres in thirty years**, and the **eight-point effects cascade**. ❗ A structural ratio with a named source and a date beats an unattributed national total every time, and an examiner can check the first.

### 7. The integrating cascade, introduced

✅ Khullar's **"Effects of Soil Erosion"** lists eight consequences: (1) topsoil is eroded, costing fertility and agricultural productivity (2) flooding and leaching strip mineral nutrients (3) ***"Ground water level is lowered and there is decrease in soil moisture"*** (4) natural vegetation dries and arid lands expand (5) the frequency and intensity of floods and droughts increase (6) rivers, canals and tanks silt up and lose holding capacity (7) ***"The incidence and damaging power of landslides increases"*** (8) the economy as a whole suffers a great setback.

✅ Majid Husain's *Geography of India*, printed p.6.17, lists **ten "Consequences of Soil Erosion"** independently, and they include ***"(iii) Lowering of the underground water-table and decrease in soil moisture"*** and ***"(vii) Recurrence of land slides"***, together with the distinctively Indian ***"(ix) Increase in crimes and anti-social activities through the formation of natural hideouts for criminals and dacoits"***.

⚠️ **Why the convergence matters.** ❗ Two separately authored books, working from different materials, place **the same two items** in their effects lists. That is the strongest available evidence that the erosion-to-water-table and erosion-to-landslide links are **part of the standard account**, not this edition's construction.

⚠️ **The mechanisms, stated so the cascade is explanatory rather than asserted.** For point (3): stripping the A horizon and sealing the surface **reduces infiltration and increases runoff**, so a larger share of rainfall leaves as surface flow and a smaller share percolates to recharge — and simultaneously the soil's own moisture-holding capacity falls with its organic matter and structure. For point (7): removing the cover and the mantle **over-steepens and exposes slopes**, while gullying **undercuts them**, which is a direct increase in the driving force and a direct reduction in the resisting force of Lesson 10's balance. ❗ Both mechanisms are stated as inferences from the sources' own content, and both are picked up explicitly — point (3) at Lessons 16 and 17, point (7) at Lessons 12 and 13.

### 8. India hook — the Chambal-Yamuna badlands and the Hoshiarpur *chos*, two machines

🇮🇳 ✅ India runs **two different erosion machines**, and the contrast is the best answer material in the lesson. ✅ In the **Chambal-Yamuna tract**, the machine is **slow, deep and old**: about **32 lakh hectares** of badlands, the product of **1,000 years of erosion at about 0.25 tonnes removed daily**, with ravines reaching **30 metres or more** and the worst concentrations in **Gwalior, Morena and Bhind** in Madhya Pradesh and **Agra, Etawah and Jalaun** in Uttar Pradesh. ⚠️ Here the agent is **concentrated runoff cutting headward into a thick, erodible alluvial fill over centuries**, and the diagnostic is **depth**.

🇮🇳 ✅ In the **Shiwalik *chos***, the machine is **fast, shallow and recent**: **nearly a hundred streams debouch across 130 kilometres**, worst in **Hoshiarpur**, and the named cause is **deforestation**. ⚠️ Here the agent is the sudden delivery of **coarse debris from a freshly destabilised, geologically young and weak catchment onto the plain**, and the diagnostic is **aggradation** — the *chos* bury productive land under sand rather than cutting it away.

❗ **Why the pairing earns marks.** ⚠️ The same process family produces **opposite land-use outcomes** — the Chambal removes land by incision, the *chos* remove land by burial — and the appropriate response differs accordingly. ❗ A candidate who writes "soil erosion affects India" has said nothing; one who contrasts a thousand-year incisional system with a decadal aggradational one has demonstrated that erosion is a **set of distinct machines with distinct geographies**.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ The **0.25 tonnes-per-day and 1,000-year figures are the book's**, and no methodology accompanies them on the page; they are quoted as that source's characterisation of the tract's age and rate, not as a measured sediment budget. ⚠️ The *chos* attribution to deforestation is likewise **the source's attribution**, and the general causal rule this file teaches at Lesson 12 — that a named cause is rarely the complete cause — applies here too. ⚠️ Finally, 🔁 **the fluvial behaviour of the Chambal as a river** — its long profile, channel form and basin transfers — belongs to the fluvial topic; this lesson owns only the **ravine as a soil-degradation landform**.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Water erosion | Wind erosion |
|---|---|---|
| Precondition | Rainfall **intensity**, bare or sealed surface, slope | Soil **dry, weakly aggregated and bare**; slope irrelevant |
| Transport modes | Splash, sheet flow, concentrated channel flow | **Suspension · saltation · surface creep** |
| What is taken | The A horizon, then the whole profile; fines preferentially | **Humus, clay and silt in suspension** — the fertility fraction, exported |
| Residue left | Gullied, ravined, badland surface | **Desert pavement** — armoured with fragments over 1.00 mm |
| ❗ Diagnostic fact | ✅ 1 m³/ha/week for 30 years = **15 cm off the whole surface** | ✅ Particles of **1.0-2.0 mm** and above cannot be rolled |
| Indian geography | ✅ Fig. 7.2 classes 1, 2 and 4 | ✅ Fig. 7.2 class 3, bounded by the **limit of wind erosion** line |

**Strongest objection.** This lesson excludes the very figures a candidate would want — the national extent of erosion, the annual soil loss — and replaces them with a state table and a thought experiment. That is a weaker answer, not a stronger one.

**Reply.** It is a stronger answer, for two reasons that an examiner can verify. ⚠️ **First, the excluded figures are not merely uncertain, they are mutually inconsistent within a single printed page** — twenty-one million hectares and forty-five million hectares for the same quantity, two paragraphs apart — and an answer that quotes one of them has a fifty per cent chance of being contradicted by the same book. ⚠️ **Second, the retained material does more analytical work.** The ravine table shows the **concentration ratio** — four states holding 69.55 per cent of the national ravine area — which is a structural claim about geography; a national total is not. ❗ The core owner is explicit that *"the classification, process and consequence are the marks-bearing content here"*, and this lesson obeys that instruction rather than working around it.

⚠️ **Residual.** The exclusions leave a genuine gap: **this file cannot state the national extent of soil erosion in India**, because no internally consistent, attributed, dated figure was available to it from any source, and the official pages that might carry one were not retrievable in this environment. ❗ The gap is **stated rather than filled**, and a candidate should state it the same way — *"the national aggregates in circulation are of uncertain vintage and are inconsistent between sources"* — which is itself a defensible analytical sentence.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I), and **"Disaster and disaster management" as support only** (General Studies Paper III) — ❗ mechanism and geography are taught; institutional response is 🔁 routed.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the **erosion mechanism** that the 2026 General Studies Paper I demand on aeolian processes in desertification needs, which is 🔁 routed to the arid-processes topic with the environment owner in support; and it **introduces the cascade** on which Lessons 12, 13 and 17 depend.
- **Probable framing:** as the evidence base of a land-degradation answer, or as the physical half of a question on ravine or wasteland reclamation.
- **Traps:** quoting a national soil-loss figure; treating sheet erosion as minor because it is invisible; attributing erosion to total rainfall rather than intensity; treating the scaling law as a measured relationship; forgetting that wind erosion exports the **fertility fraction** rather than mere bulk; attributing Table 7.1 to a source the page does not name.
- **Answer use:** *"Erosion is defined comparatively — removal faster than soil formation replaces — so the policy object is a rate, not an elimination; and because carrying and scouring capacity scale steeply with flow, conservation works by cutting runoff velocity and slope length rather than by resisting water."* That sentence is the bridge into Lesson 9's practice set.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Definition:** removal *"more rapidly than the various soil forming processes can replace it"*. ⚠️ **Comparative, and about the A horizon**, not about bulk. ✅ Imperceptibly slow, cumulatively very great.
- ✅ **Ladder:** splash → sheet → rill → gully → **ravine (30 m or more)** → **badland (immediate drop of 3-4 m)** · **Splash:** drops at **~10 m/sec**; **sands and silts detach most readily**; impact **seals the surface** → runoff rises.
- ✅ ❗ **Sheet anchor: 1 cubic metre per hectare per week for 30 years = 15 cm off the entire surface.**
- ✅ **It is not total rainfall but how and when it comes** — one heavy downpour does what a season's equal depth cannot.
- ✅ **Scaling law (as the source states it, "theoretically"):** double the flow → carrying **×4**, scouring **×32**, particle size **×64**. ⚠️ Use it for **non-linearity**, and note that **slope length matters as much as angle**.
- ✅ **Wind:** **suspension** (humus, clay, silt — the fertility fraction, exported) · **saltation** (fine-medium sand, bounds and leaps) · **surface creep** (coarse sand, rolled). ✅ **Desert pavement** = armour of fragments **> 1.00 mm**; **1.0-2.0 mm** and above cannot be rolled. ✅ **Sea and glacial erosion** named as two further agents.
- ✅ **Fig. 7.2 legend:** gully + sheet · sheet · desert (aeolian) · shifting cultivation · glacial · coastal · ❗ **limit of wind erosion** (a line, not a zone).
- ✅ **Ravines:** **MHA working group, 1971 — 39.75 lakh ha across 18 states**, of which **27.65 lakh ha = 69.55 %** in UP, MP, Rajasthan, Gujarat. ✅ **Chambal Development Scheme aerial survey: ~50,600 ha at 4.5-6.0 m**. ✅ **MP 4-8 lakh ha** on Chambal and Kali Sindh, **2.4 lakh ha** in **Gwalior, Morena, Bhind**. ✅ **UP:** Chambal, Yamuna, Gomati, Son; **Agra, Etawah, Jalaun**.
- ✅ **Table 7.1 (lakh ha):** UP 12.30 · MP 6.83 · Rajasthan 4.52 · Gujarat 4.00 · Punjab 1.20 · West Bengal 1.04 · Bihar 0.60 · Tamil Nadu 0.60 · Maharashtra 0.20. ❗ **No source line is visible on the page; it is not attributed here.**
- ✅ **Chambal-Yamuna badlands: ~32 lakh ha, 1,000 years of erosion at ~0.25 tonnes removed daily.**
- ✅ **Fig. 6.6** distinguishes **ravines and bluff** from **cultivated land**; ✅ gully erosion also in the **Shiwalik tracts**, southern Himalayan slopes, and **Western and Eastern Ghats**.
- ✅ **Human factors:** deforestation (***chos*** — **~100 streams across 130 km**, worst in **Hoshiarpur**) · overgrazing · shifting cultivation (**>15 lakh ha cleared yearly**, **45 lakh ha** affected; **Assam 207,287 ha · Tripura 41,963 ha · Manipur 21,862 ha · Odisha 33,08,502 ha**) · **Nilgiri potato on slopes over 60 %, yields down ~50 %**; ✅ **land steeper than 1 in 4 should not be cultivated**.
- ✅ **Sediment transfer: Ganga ~30 million tonnes/year; Brahmaputra ~10 million tonnes.** ✅ **Kerala: of a 560 km coast, ~32 km of sandy beach under severe sea erosion** (coconut uprooting).
- ❌ ❗ **EXCLUDED:** the 21-against-45 million hectare wind-erosion contradiction on one page; the national aggregates travelling with it; the ICAR "53.34 million hectares annually" unit error; the 1990 GLASOD global figures and the un-attributed "6,000 million tonnes" Indian figure; the undated desert tonnage claims.
- ✅ ❗ **THE CASCADE:** erosion → **(3) ground water level is lowered and soil moisture decreases** → **(7) incidence and damaging power of landslides increases**. ✅ Independently repeated in a second book's ten-point list, which adds **"(ix) ... natural hideouts for criminals and dacoits"**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A district officer reports that erosion in his district is "not serious, since there are no gullies". Assess the inference, and state what evidence would actually settle the question.

**Model answer:** The inference is unsafe, and the reasoning inverts the risk. Gullies are **rung four** of a six-rung intensity ladder that runs splash, sheet, rill, gully, ravine, badland; their absence establishes only that the upper rungs have not been reached. The dangerous rung is **sheet erosion**, precisely because it is **uniform and therefore invisible**: there is no channel to observe, and the source records the process as imperceptibly slow while the cumulative loss is very great. The arithmetic shows the scale concealed by that invisibility — a loss of one cubic metre per hectare per week, sustained for thirty years, removes fifteen centimetres from the entire surface, which is the whole of the agriculturally critical A horizon in most profiles. A district with no gullies may therefore be losing its topsoil faster than one with conspicuous gullies confined to a small area. Three kinds of evidence would settle it. **Direct:** repeated measurement of surface level against fixed datums, or of profile depth against earlier survey records. **Indirect but strong:** the state of the surface — whether aggregates survive or the surface has been sealed into a crust by raindrop impact, since sealing both signals and causes increased runoff. **Downstream:** sediment accumulation in tanks, canals and reservoirs within the catchment, since the source lists siltation of rivers, canals and tanks among the effects of erosion. A declining water table and reduced soil moisture would also be consistent, since the same list records both as consequences of erosion through reduced infiltration.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not equate visibility with severity. The erosion forms that are easiest to photograph are the ones that have already taken the land out of cultivation; the form that is destroying cultivated land now is the one that leaves no mark an observer can see in a single visit.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "In soil erosion, the geography of the process matters more than its national magnitude." Discuss with Indian examples. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is sound, and India's own data demonstrate it. National magnitudes for Indian soil erosion are inconsistent between sources and sometimes within a single page, and diagnose nothing about where intervention should occur. Structural geography does both. The ravine problem is extremely concentrated: a 1971 working group recorded 39.75 lakh hectares across eighteen states, of which 27.65 lakh hectares, or 69.55 per cent, lie in just four — Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Gujarat. Within that, Gwalior, Morena and Bhind hold 2.4 lakh hectares. The process differs too: the Chambal-Yamuna badlands are an incisional system about 32 lakh hectares in extent, attributed in the source to a thousand years of erosion, whereas the Shiwalik *chos*, nearly a hundred streams across 130 kilometres and worst in Hoshiarpur, destroy land by burial rather than by cutting. The qualification is that national aggregates remain necessary for budgeting, if dated and attributed.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the ravine concentration ratio quoted with its 1971 source and its four states; 3 for the incisional-against-aggradational contrast with both Indian cases named; 2 for the explicit reason national aggregates are unusable here, including the internal inconsistency; 1 for one district-level anchor; 1 for the budgeting qualification. Deduct 3 for quoting an unattributed national soil-loss total, and 2 for treating all erosion in India as one undifferentiated process.

A vertical mechanism and a branching response. ❗ The gate's lower branch is this file's **structural bridge**: the tubewell that reclaimed waterlogged saline land in the Punjab-Haryana plain is the same tubewell that Lesson 17 finds drawing more water than natural replenishment supplies.

### 1. Salinity and alkalinity, precisely distinguished

✅ Khullar separates two things that are routinely merged, and the separation is examinable.

- ✅ **Salinity** is a layer of **white salt raised by capillary action**, with a predominance of **chlorides and sulphates of sodium, calcium and magnesium** in quantities that seriously interfere with plant growth.
- ✅ **Alkalinity** is the **dominance of sodium salts, especially sodium carbonate**.

✅ The two **occur in the same areas but differ chemically**, and there is a textural preference: ✅ **sandy soils are more prone to alkalinity, loamy soils to salinity-alkalinity**.

⚠️ **Why the distinction matters practically** — and this is where the mark is. The two have **different remedies**. Salinity is fundamentally an **excess-of-salt** problem, treatable by **leaching the salt out**, provided there is drainage to take it away. Alkalinity is a **sodium-on-the-exchange-complex** problem, which destroys soil structure by dispersing clay, and leaching alone will not fix it; ✅ the sodium must first be **displaced chemically by calcium, which is what gypsum supplies**. ❗ An answer that proposes "drainage and leaching" for alkali land has prescribed the wrong treatment.

✅ **Extent and geography:** about **80 lakh hectares, or 2.43 per cent of India**, worst in **western Uttar Pradesh**; also the **canal tracts of Punjab and Haryana**, arid **Rajasthan**, semi-arid **Maharashtra, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana and Karnataka**, and **coastal Odisha, Gujarat and West Bengal**. ⚠️ Note that this 80 lakh hectare figure and Lesson 7's 68,000 square kilometre saline-and-alkaline soil-group figure are **close but not identical** and come from different passages of the same book; both are reported as that source's figures and neither is reconciled against the other here.

### 2. Waterlogging — extent, cause, and the fact that makes it the hinge

✅ **Waterlogging covers about 12 million hectares in India — half along the coast and half inland in existing or newly created irrigation command areas**, as shown in **Fig. 7.5 INDIA: WATERLOGGED SOILS**.

✅ **Causes:** flat surfaces and **saucer-like depressions**, plus **seepage from unlined canals**.

✅ ❗ **And the hinge fact: waterlogging is itself a chief cause of salinity.** ⚠️ That single clause converts two problems into one chain, and the chain is the lesson's core causal content: **unlined canal → seepage → water table rises → capillary continuity to the surface is established → dry-season evaporation draws water up → dissolved salts are deposited at the surface → the land becomes saline → and if the dominant cation is sodium, alkali.** ❗ Every link is physical and every link is breakable, which is why the decision gate above has three exits.

```text
  Fig. 7.5  INDIA: WATERLOGGED SOILS -- structure as printed
  (D.R. Khullar, printed p.214).  Re-drawn in text; no image copied.

   ~12 MILLION HECTARES, SPLIT ROUGHLY IN HALF
   +------------------------------+------------------------------+
   |  COASTAL HALF                |  INLAND HALF                 |
   |  the littoral tracts, where  |  EXISTING or NEWLY CREATED   |
   |  a high sea-influenced water |  IRRIGATION COMMAND AREAS    |
   |  table and poor outfall keep |  -- canal seepage + flat or  |
   |  the profile saturated       |  saucer-like terrain         |
   +------------------------------+------------------------------+
            |                                    |
            v                                    v
      quality constraint on                 ** THE AVOIDABLE HALF **
      coastal aquifers (L16)                engineering + drainage
                                            decide the outcome
```

⚠️ The **structure** of that figure is the examinable content: roughly half of India's waterlogging is **coastal and largely given**, while roughly half is **inland and the direct product of irrigation engineering**. ❗ That split is what makes salinity an engineering-and-governance failure rather than a natural hazard, which is exactly the core owner's own evidence unit: ✅ *"irrigation can degrade the very land it makes productive ... it reframes salinity as an engineering-and-governance failure rather than a natural hazard ... the binding constraint is cost and collective action, not knowledge."*

### 3. The two drainages, and the one that reaches the water table

✅ Khullar gives two remedies with their **conditions of use**, which is more useful than a list:

- ✅ **(a) Surface drainage** — an **open system with an adequate outlet**. ✅ Useful where soils are **deep with low infiltration rates**, **rainfall intensity is high**, **terrain is level to nearly level**, and **the water table is high**.
- ✅ **(b) Vertical drainage** — **any bore or well from which the underlying water is extracted**. ✅ Its success depends on **a favourable aquifer** and on **re-usable water quality**.

✅ ❗ **And the Indian result: vertical drainage succeeded in the Indo-Gangetic plain — tubewell pumping lowered the Punjab-Haryana water table and reclaimed waterlogged saline lands.**

⚠️ **This is the sentence that makes the file one file.** A **soil-reclamation** measure works by **changing the groundwater table**, which means the soil arc and the groundwater arc are not two subjects that happen to share a chapter — they are **one hydrological system observed from two directions**. ❗ And the irony is the examinable part: ⚠️ the instrument that solved the waterlogging problem in the Punjab-Haryana plain is the same instrument that Lesson 17 finds responsible for extraction exceeding replenishment in the same states. ❗ **A technology is not a policy.** Its effect depends on whether the objective is lowering a water table that is too high or extracting from one that is already too low, and the sources show both outcomes from the same device in the same region.

### 4. Reclamation chemistry and the cropping answer

✅ **Alkali reclamation**, as the source sets it out: liberal application of **gypsum to the upper 15 centimetres** to convert alkalis into soluble compounds; removal of alkali by **sulphuric acid or acid-forming substances such as sulphur and pyrite**; addition of **rice husk and rice straw**, which generate mild acid on decomposition; and **flushing the salt by flooding** — ❗ with the source's own explicit caveat that this **can accumulate saline water in the downstream area**.

⚠️ That caveat is worth a sentence of its own in an answer, because it generalises: **leaching relocates salt, it does not destroy it.** ❗ Without an outlet that takes the leachate out of the system, reclamation of one field is salinisation of another — which is why "adequate outlet" appears in the surface-drainage condition above and why drainage, not chemistry, is the binding constraint.

✅ **The cropping answer**, which belongs with the chemistry: the salinity-alkalinity-**acidity** trio is addressed with **salt-tolerant crops — rice, berseem, barley, jowar and sugarcane** — alongside **farmyard and green manures, gypsum, lime and sulphur**. ⚠️ The agronomic logic is to **use the land while repairing it** rather than withdrawing it from production, which is the only politically survivable form of reclamation on smallholdings. 🔁 Cropping economics, input subsidy and crop-choice policy are owned by the agriculture and economy owners and are not developed here.

### 5. Conservation: doctrine, practice and the engineering thresholds

✅ **The doctrine, with its named authorities.** **Prof. S.P. Chatterjee** — *"Soil erosion is the greatest single evil to Indian agriculture and animal husbandry."* **G.T. Renner**'s definition of conservation as *"the greatest good to the largest number for the longest time."* **S.I. Kayastha** — *"With soil conservation people rise and with its destruction they fall."* ✅ And the estimate that **about two-thirds of India's arable land needs conservation measures**.

✅ **The forest-cover norm, dated:** **20 to 25 per cent** was considered healthy, **raised to 33 per cent in the Second Five Year Plan — 20 per cent for the plains and 60 per cent for hilly and mountainous regions.** ⚠️ Quote it as a **Plan-era norm with its date**, not as a current statutory target; 🔁 current forest policy belongs to the environment owner.

✅ **The practice set, with its figures.** **Crop rotation; strip cropping (Fig. 7.6); early-maturing varieties; contour ploughing (Fig. 7.7); terracing and contour bunding (Fig. 7.8).** ✅ Two further practices complete the set: **checking shifting cultivation** by persuading tribal communities to switch to settled agriculture with residential accommodation, implements, seeds, manures and cattle on reclaimed land; and **ploughing in the right direction — perpendicular to the wind**.

```text
  Figs. 7.6-7.8  THE CONSERVATION PRACTICE SET, re-drawn in text
  (D.R. Khullar, printed pp.215-216).  No image reproduced.

  Fig. 7.6  STRIP CROPPING ON THE CONTOUR
    ~~~~~~~ close-growing strip (grass / legume)  <- traps runoff
    ======= row-crop strip                           and sediment
    ~~~~~~~ close-growing strip                      from the strip
    ======= row-crop strip                           upslope
    PRINCIPLE: break the SLOPE LENGTH into short segments

  Fig. 7.7  CONTOUR PLOUGHING
    furrows run ACROSS the slope, not down it
    ---->  ---->  ---->       each furrow is a small storage basin
    ---->  ---->  ---->       PRINCIPLE: remove the downslope
    ---->  ---->  ---->       pathway; cut velocity to near zero

  Fig. 7.8  TERRACING WITH RETAINING WALLS
    original hill slope  \                    TWO WAYS of terracing a
                          \  ___ bench 1      steep hill slope, each
                           \|   |             with a RETAINING WALL
                            |___|___ bench 2  built against the
                                |   |         ORIGINAL hill slope
                                |___|___      PRINCIPLE: convert one
                                    |   |     long slope into several
                                    |___|     level surfaces

  THE ENGINEERING THRESHOLDS -- the numbers that make this answerable
    bunding becomes expensive and less effective above ....  8 %  = 1 in 12
    NOTHING should be terraced above .....................  20 % = 1 in 5
    fields steeper than this should leave the plough ......  15 % = 1 in 6
      (benches would have to be uneconomically close together)
    and, from L8, land steeper than ....................... 1 in 4
      should not be cultivated at all but put under grasses and trees
```

⚠️ The thresholds are the most useful numbers in the lesson because they convert "conservation" from an aspiration into a **decision rule keyed to slope**, and because they are internally consistent with the Nilgiri case of Lesson 8, where cultivation on slopes exceeding 60 per cent produced yields down about 50 per cent despite heavy fertiliser.

### 6. The institutional record, named and dated — and bounded

✅ Soil and water conservation was adopted from the **First Five Year Plan**; a conservation division was set up under the **Department of Agriculture and Cooperation**; a **Third Plan** scheme targeted premature reservoir siltation; the **Flood-Prone Rivers scheme** began in the **Sixth Plan**; and both were clubbed in the **Ninth Plan** as catchment management of **River Valley Projects and Flood Prone Rivers — 53 catchments across 27 states, total catchment area 96.14 million hectares, priority area 26 million hectares, of which 5.86 million hectares treated to 2003-04**.

✅ ***Usar* reclamation:** a centrally sponsored alkali-soil scheme began in the **Seventh Plan** in **Haryana, Punjab and Uttar Pradesh** and was extended in the **Eighth Plan** to **Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh and Rajasthan** — **0.62 million hectares of 3.5 million hectares of alkali land reclaimed to 2003-04**.

✅ **Ravine reclamation** was launched in **1987-88** and transferred to the state sector in **1991-92** by decision of the **National Development Council**, with **about 1.266 lakh hectares treated**.

✅ **Shifting-cultivation control** began in **Arunachal Pradesh and Mizoram in 1986-87** and widened in **1987-88 to nine states**, with **26,532 families selected for rehabilitation by 1990-91**, **67,000 hectares treated in the Eighth Plan**, **1.5 lakh hectares to March 2002** and **0.4 lakh hectares in the Tenth Plan to 2003-04**.

✅ The **All India Soil and Land Survey Organisation uses remote sensing** to stay abreast of trends.

❗ **Boundary, stated plainly.** ⚠️ This record is included because it is **evidence about the scale and the completion ratio of reclamation**, which is a geographical fact: **0.62 of 3.5 million hectares of alkali land, and 5.86 of 26 million hectares of priority catchment, is a one-in-six to one-in-four completion ratio over decades.** ❗ That ratio is the answer-worthy content. ⚠️ **All of this is Plan-era data ending at 2003-04**, and it is quoted with that terminus attached; it is **not** presented as the current programme architecture. 🔁 The design, financing and administration of current watershed and land-reclamation programmes belong to the governance and agriculture owners and are **routed, not taught**.

### 7. The conservation-to-recharge coupling — the cascade's return journey

✅ Here is the second of the file's three structural couplings, stated in the source's own words: *"Contour bunding and levelling have been very effective in checking soil erosion and in increasing crop yields. In Maharashtra, Karnataka, Telangana, Andhra Pradesh, Gujarat and Punjab, an increase of 25 per cent in the crop yields has been recorded as a result of contour bunding. **This has also increased the water recharging capacity of wells in the adjoining areas.**"*

✅ With supporting yield data from the **National Commission on Agriculture**: gains of **35 per cent from bunding and 60 per cent from levelling** for kharif and rabi in the **semi-arid alluvial plains of Uttar Pradesh**, and **15 to 65 per cent** potato-yield gains from **bench terracing in the Kunda catchment of the Nilgiris**.

✅ And the third coupling: **afforestation reduces runoff intensity and increases the seepage of water to the underground water table**; ✅ **shelterbelts** — rows of trees planted by farmers in the drier parts of **Gujarat, Haryana, Rajasthan and western Madhya Pradesh** — cut wind velocity.

⚠️ **The mechanism is a single sentence and it closes the cascade.** Lesson 8's cascade ran erosion → reduced infiltration → lowered water table. ⚠️ Conservation **runs that cascade backwards**: measures that slow runoff and increase detention time necessarily **increase the share of rainfall that infiltrates**, and infiltration is recharge. ❗ So **soil conservation is groundwater policy conducted by other means** — which is why this lesson sits immediately before the mass-movement block and immediately upstream of the groundwater block, and why Lesson 18's rural recharge techniques will turn out to be the same structures under a different name.

🔁 ⚠️ **One bounded cross-link only.** The desertification literature records *"lowering of water table leading to acute water shortage"* among the implications of advancing aridity. ❗ That single line is taken as a **cross-link confirming the same coupling in a third body of material**; **desertification itself — its extent, its dune geography, its research institutions and the UNCCD framework — is owned by the arid-processes topic and by the environment owner and is not taught here.**

### 8. India hook — the Punjab-Haryana plain, where the cure became the disease

🇮🇳 ✅ The Punjab-Haryana plain is the single most instructive degradation case in Indian geography, because **the same technology appears twice with opposite signs**, and both appearances are documented in the same book.

🇮🇳 ✅ **First appearance — the cure.** Canal irrigation without adequate drainage raised the water table and waterlogged the command areas; ✅ **vertical drainage by tubewell pumping lowered the Punjab-Haryana water table and reclaimed waterlogged saline lands.** ✅ The salinity it was reclaiming was real and large: **about 1.21 million hectares in Punjab affected by *usar***, against **about 1.25 million hectares in Uttar Pradesh**.

🇮🇳 ✅ **Second appearance — the disease.** ⚠️ At Lesson 17 the same book records that **Haryana, Punjab and Rajasthan receive less than 40 centimetres of annual rainfall, are deficient in surface water, and consequently exploit more than 100 per cent of the available groundwater for irrigation**, with the level falling *"rather alarmingly"* and the warning that **"granary of today will be barren land tomorrow."**

⚠️ **The analytical point, and it is the best one in the lesson.** ❗ **The tubewell did not change. The hydrological objective did.** When the water table was too high, extraction was reclamation. When the water table was too low, the identical extraction became depletion. ❗ A technology is therefore **never a policy**, and the examinable discipline is to ask what the **target state of the water table** is before evaluating any intervention that changes it.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ The reclamation success and the over-abstraction crisis are **not asserted to be the same fields or the same decades**; the sources record both for the region without establishing a direct successional link at any one site, and this edition claims only that **the same instrument produced both outcomes in the same region**. ⚠️ Second, 🔁 **the policy half — energy pricing, procurement, crop incentives and the institutional response to depletion — is owned by the economy owner**, and this lesson teaches only why the physical system behaved as it did. ⚠️ Third, the **40-centimetre rainfall figure and the over-100-per-cent extraction claim are that book's**, and Lesson 17 sets them against a current official assessment with both dates attached rather than treating either as settled.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Salinity | Alkalinity |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Dominant chemistry | **Chlorides and sulphates** of sodium, calcium, magnesium | **Sodium salts, especially sodium carbonate** |
| ✅ Texture preference | **Loamy** soils (salinity-alkalinity) | **Sandy** soils |
| ⚠️ Effect on structure | Osmotic stress on plants; structure broadly retained | **Clay dispersion** — structure collapses, the soil seals |
| ⚠️ Remedy that works | **Leaching**, provided there is an outlet | **Gypsum first** to displace sodium with calcium, then leaching |
| ❗ Error it prevents | — | Prescribing drainage-and-leaching alone for alkali land |

**Strongest objection.** Reclamation is an agricultural engineering subject. A physical-geography topic has no business listing gypsum doses, bund slopes and Plan-period scheme coverage.

**Reply.** Two of the three bodies of material here are physical-geography content by any test. ⚠️ **The capillary mechanism** is pedogenesis running in reverse — it is Lesson 6's salinisation direction, worked at field scale. ⚠️ **The drainage choice** is a decision about **the water table**, which is the central object of Lessons 14 to 18. ❗ And the engineering thresholds are retained for a specific analytical reason: they are the only place in the sources where the **slope-to-practice relationship** is quantified, and slope is the control of Lesson 4 and the driving variable of Lesson 10. ⚠️ What is **not** retained is exactly what the objection fears — programme design, financing and administration are 🔁 routed by name, and the Plan-era institutional record is used only for its **completion ratio**, which is a geographical fact about the gap between prescription and treatment.

⚠️ **Residual.** The conservation-to-recharge coupling is **stated in the sources but nowhere quantified**: ✅ bunding *"increased the water recharging capacity of wells in the adjoining areas"*, and ✅ afforestation *"increases the seepage of water to the underground water table"*, but **no recharge volume, no coefficient and no measurement method accompanies either statement**. ❗ This edition therefore teaches the coupling as a **directional claim with a named source** and asserts no magnitude. ⚠️ A further residual: the coupling may not hold everywhere, since in a confined or deep aquifer, surface detention need not translate into recharge of the pumped horizon — a qualification the sources do not make and an answer should.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I); **"Disaster and disaster management" as support only** (General Studies Paper III).
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the **degradation mechanism** for the 2018 Prelims demand on soil organic matter, sulphur and salinisation, which is 🔁 **routed to the environment owner**; and it is the **bridge lesson** into the groundwater arc, since vertical drainage is the first time the file acts directly on the water table.
- **Probable framing:** as the second half of any soil-degradation answer, and as the causal paragraph in an answer about irrigation's unintended consequences.
- **Traps:** merging salinity and alkalinity; prescribing leaching for alkali land; treating salinity as a natural arid-zone phenomenon only; forgetting that leaching **relocates** salt; quoting the 33 per cent forest norm as current; presenting Plan-era scheme data as the present programme; asserting a recharge magnitude for bunding.
- **Answer use:** the strongest single move is the technology-is-not-a-policy line. *"The tubewell that reclaimed waterlogged saline land in the Punjab-Haryana plain is the same tubewell that now draws more than is replenished; the device did not change, the target state of the water table did."* It converts a descriptive answer into an analytical one in one sentence.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Salinity** = white crust **raised by capillary action**; **chlorides and sulphates of Na, Ca, Mg**. ✅ **Alkalinity** = **sodium salts, especially sodium carbonate**. ✅ Same areas, different chemistry.
- ✅ **Sandy soils → alkalinity; loamy soils → salinity-alkalinity.** ⚠️ Alkali needs **gypsum first** (calcium displaces sodium); leaching alone fails.
- ✅ **Extent: about 80 lakh ha = 2.43 % of India**, worst in **western UP**; canal tracts of Punjab and Haryana; arid Rajasthan; semi-arid Maharashtra, Gujarat, AP, Telangana, Karnataka; coastal Odisha, Gujarat, West Bengal.
- ✅ **Waterlogging: about 12 million ha — half coastal, half inland in irrigation command areas (Fig. 7.5).** ✅ Causes: flat surfaces, **saucer-like depressions**, **seepage from unlined canals**.
- ✅ ❗ **Waterlogging is itself a chief cause of salinity.** The chain: seepage → table rises → capillary continuity → evaporation → salt at the surface.
- ✅ **(a) Surface drainage** — open system with an **adequate outlet**; for **deep soils, low infiltration, high rainfall intensity, level terrain, high water table** · **(b) Vertical drainage** — any bore or well extracting the underlying water; needs **a favourable aquifer** and **re-usable water quality**.
- ✅ ❗ **Indo-Gangetic result: tubewell pumping lowered the Punjab-Haryana water table and reclaimed waterlogged saline land.** ⚠️ The same instrument becomes over-abstraction at Lesson 17.
- ✅ **Alkali chemistry:** **gypsum to the upper 15 cm**; sulphuric acid, **sulphur, pyrite**; **rice husk and straw** (mild acid on decomposition); flooding to flush — ❗ **caveat: this can accumulate saline water downstream** · **Salt-tolerant crops: rice, berseem, barley, jowar, sugarcane**, with farmyard and green manures, gypsum, lime, sulphur.
- ✅ **Doctrine:** **S.P. Chatterjee** — erosion is *"the greatest single evil to Indian agriculture and animal husbandry"*; **G.T. Renner** — *"the greatest good to the largest number for the longest time"*; **S.I. Kayastha** — *"with soil conservation people rise and with its destruction they fall"*. ✅ **About two-thirds of arable land needs conservation** · **Forest norm (dated):** **20-25 %** healthy, **raised to 33 % in the Second Five Year Plan — 20 % plains, 60 % hills.**
- ✅ **Practices:** crop rotation · **strip cropping (Fig. 7.6)** · early-maturing varieties · **contour ploughing (Fig. 7.7)** · **terracing and contour bunding (Fig. 7.8)** · checking shifting cultivation · **ploughing perpendicular to the wind**.
- ✅ ❗ **Thresholds: bunding expensive and less effective above 8 % (1 in 12); nothing above 20 % (1 in 5) should be terraced; fields steeper than 15 % (1 in 6) should leave the plough.**
- ✅ **Institutional record (to 2003-04):** First Plan adoption; Department of Agriculture and Cooperation division; Third Plan siltation scheme; **Flood-Prone Rivers from the Sixth Plan**; clubbed in the **Ninth Plan — 53 catchments, 27 states, 96.14 m ha total, 26 m ha priority, 5.86 m ha treated**. ✅ ***Usar*: Seventh Plan (Haryana, Punjab, UP) → Eighth Plan (Gujarat, MP, Rajasthan); 0.62 of 3.5 m ha reclaimed.** ✅ **Ravine reclamation 1987-88, to the state sector 1991-92 by the National Development Council, ~1.266 lakh ha treated.** ✅ **Shifting cultivation: 1986-87 Arunachal and Mizoram → 1987-88 nine states; 26,532 families by 1990-91; 67,000 ha in the Eighth Plan; 1.5 lakh ha to March 2002; 0.4 lakh ha in the Tenth Plan.** ✅ **All India Soil and Land Survey Organisation uses remote sensing** · ❗ The answer-worthy figure is the **completion ratio** — roughly one in six of alkali land and under a quarter of priority catchment, over decades.
- ✅ ❗ **THE COUPLING:** contour bunding gave **25 % yield gains** in Maharashtra, Karnataka, Telangana, AP, Gujarat and Punjab and ***"also increased the water recharging capacity of wells in the adjoining areas"***. ✅ **National Commission on Agriculture: 35 % from bunding, 60 % from levelling** (semi-arid alluvial UP); **15-65 %** potato gains from **bench terracing, Kunda catchment, Nilgiris**.
- ✅ **Afforestation reduces runoff intensity and increases seepage to the water table**; ✅ **shelterbelts** in drier **Gujarat, Haryana, Rajasthan, western MP** cut wind velocity.
- ❗ **No recharge magnitude is asserted** — the sources state the direction, never a volume or a coefficient.

### Concept check

**Question:** A command area in western Uttar Pradesh develops white surface crusting within a decade of canal commissioning. An engineer proposes heavy flooding to leach the salts away. Evaluate the diagnosis and the proposed remedy.

**Model answer:** The diagnosis is incomplete and the remedy may be the wrong one, for two separate reasons. **On diagnosis:** white crusting is the visible symptom, but the cause lies below it.
