Canal irrigation without adequate drainage, particularly where canals are unlined and the terrain is level or saucer-shaped, raises the water table until capillary continuity to the surface is established; dry-season evaporation then draws water upward and deposits its dissolved load at the surface. Waterlogging is therefore the chief cause of the salinity, and treating the crust without lowering the table guarantees recurrence. **On chemistry:** the proposal assumes the problem is salinity, meaning an excess of chlorides and sulphates of sodium, calcium and magnesium. If the dominant species is **sodium carbonate**, the land is **alkali**, not merely saline, and leaching will fail — sodium on the exchange complex disperses the clay and seals the soil, so water will not move through it. Alkali land requires **gypsum applied to the upper fifteen centimetres** to displace sodium with calcium, or acid-forming amendments such as sulphur or pyrite, before leaching can work at all. **On the remedy's side effect:** the source itself warns that flushing by flooding **can accumulate saline water in the downstream area**, so leaching without an adequate outlet relocates the problem rather than solving it. The correct sequence is: identify whether the salts are chloride-sulphate or sodium-carbonate dominated; install drainage with a genuine outlet, by surface drainage if the terrain is level and the soil deep with low infiltration, or by vertical drainage if a favourable aquifer exists and the pumped water is re-usable; then amend chemically if the soil is alkali; and only then leach.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat surface salt as a surface problem. It is the terminal deposit of an upward transport system whose engine is the water table and whose pump is evaporation, so any remedy that does not act on one of those two will be repeating itself next dry season.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Soil conservation in India is, in effect, groundwater policy conducted by other means." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is supported by the sources, though only directionally. Erosion lowers the water table: stripping the topsoil and sealing the surface reduce infiltration and raise runoff, and one source lists the lowering of the groundwater level and a decrease in soil moisture among the effects of soil erosion, a finding repeated in a second book's list. Conservation runs that chain backwards. The same source records that contour bunding, besides raising crop yields by about twenty-five per cent across six states, **also increased the water recharging capacity of wells in the adjoining areas**, and that afforestation increases the seepage of water to the underground water table. Vertical drainage makes the link explicit: reclaiming waterlogged saline land in the Punjab-Haryana plain was achieved by acting on the water table. The qualification is that no source quantifies the recharge gain, and surface detention need not recharge a deep or confined aquifer.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the erosion-to-water-table link quoted or paraphrased from the effects list with the infiltration mechanism supplied; 3 for the conservation-to-recharge coupling with at least one named practice and its recorded yield gain; 2 for vertical drainage used as the explicit water-table intervention; 1 for the honest statement that no recharge magnitude is given in the sources; 1 for the confined-aquifer qualification. Deduct 2 for asserting a recharge volume or coefficient, and 2 for treating conservation purely as an agricultural measure.

## Lesson 10 — Why slopes fail: force balance, shear strength and pore-water pressure

Progress: 10 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Driving against resisting force; the factor of safety; cohesion and internal friction; the three distinct things water does; and the perched water table

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: core owner §1 — *"Mass movement = down-slope movement of weathered material under gravity; speed depends on slope gradient, debris weight and rain lubrication"*, and the causal control that *"intense rain is a trigger, not a complete landslide cause"* with antecedent moisture, discontinuities, toe cutting and drainage to be tested before attribution; advanced owner §1 — weathering feeds soil formation, but once vegetation is stripped and slopes over-steepened the same loose material fails as landslides; G.C. Leong, local-scan pp.34-36 — the slump mechanism, in which permeable debris resting on impermeable clay produces a perched water body that lubricates a curved slip surface and allows rotational failure.
CA search: "slope stability pore water pressure landslide mechanism India official technical 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in the last six months at this file's first-party standard. ❗ Nil return recorded. ⚠️ The file's landslide current anchors are two institutional mapping programmes and one multi-institutional site investigation; all three are assigned to Lessons 12 and 13, where they bear on causation and geography rather than on mechanics.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
  THE FREE-BODY SLOPE DERIVATION -- draw it, then compute it

                              ^  N = W cos(b)   [normal component:
                             /|                  presses the block
           block of         / |                  ONTO the surface]
           weight W        /  |
           on a slope     /   |
           of angle b    /    |
            ___________ /     |
           |           X------+
           |  W sin(b) |\     |        W sin(b) = DRIVING FORCE
           |<----------| \    |                  (down the slope)
           |___________|  \   |
                \          \  |        W cos(b) = the component that
                 \ b        \ |                  generates FRICTION
                  \__________\|
         =====================================  potential SLIP SURFACE
                  slope angle b

  THE RESISTING FORCE (shear strength, S):
     S  =  c       +   (N - u) tan(phi)
           ^           ^   ^    ^
           |           |   |    +-- phi = angle of internal friction
           |           |   |           (how well grains interlock)
           |           |   +------- u = PORE-WATER PRESSURE
           |           |            ** it SUBTRACTS from N **
           |           +----------- N = W cos(b), the normal force
           +----------------------- c = COHESION (clay bonds, roots,
                                       cementation, apparent cohesion
                                       from moisture in sand)

  THE FACTOR OF SAFETY:
                      RESISTING       c + (W cos b - u) tan(phi)
       FoS   =        ---------  =    --------------------------
                       DRIVING                W sin b

       FoS > 1  stable        FoS = 1  limiting equilibrium
       FoS < 1  FAILURE       -- and nothing has to be ADDED for
                                 FoS to fall; u RISING is enough

  A WORKED NUMBER, ILLUSTRATIVE AND NOT AN INDIAN MEASUREMENT:
     take a slope where, in the dry state,
        driving = 100 units ; resisting = 150 units  -> FoS = 1.50
     now let prolonged rain raise pore pressure so that the
     (N - u) tan(phi) term falls by one third, and let the mass
     gain 5 % in weight from the water it absorbed:
        driving = 105 ; resisting = c + (2/3 of the friction term)
     if c was 30 and the friction term was 120:
        resisting = 30 + 80 = 110  ->  FoS = 110/105 = 1.05
     one more wet day, or one toe cut, and FoS crosses 1.

  WHAT WATER ACTUALLY DOES -- THREE DISTINCT THINGS, NOT ONE:
     (1) ADDS MASS      -> raises W -> raises DRIVING force
     (2) RAISES PORE PRESSURE u -> CUTS EFFECTIVE STRESS ->
         cuts FRICTION -> ** the dominant effect, and the one
         "lubrication" conceals **
     (3) DESTROYS APPARENT COHESION in sands, and softens clays
     ++ and in a PERCHED system, permeable debris on impermeable
        clay holds water exactly at the potential slip surface
```

A balance, an inequality and a worked number. ❗ The derivation exists to kill one word: **"lubrication"**. Water does three separable things, and the dominant one is not lubrication at all but the reduction of effective stress — which is why a slope can fail without any new load being added to it.

### 1. Plain intuition before the algebra

⚠️ Every slope is a competition between two things. **Gravity is trying to pull the material down the slope. Friction and cohesion are holding it in place.** If the first exceeds the second, the slope fails. That is the whole subject, and the algebra above is only a way of writing it precisely enough to see **which variable changed**.

⚠️ The intuition that needs installing is this: **the slope has not changed when it fails.** The rock is the same rock, the angle is the same angle. What changed is almost always **water** — which is why the typical Indian landslide happens during or just after the monsoon, and why it happens on a slope that stood through the previous forty monsoons.

### 2. The balance, term by term

⚠️ **Driving force** is the downslope component of weight, **W sin b**. Two ways to increase it: make the slope steeper, or make the material heavier. ⚠️ Both occur in India routinely — road cutting and quarrying steepen; saturation, construction loading and spoil dumping add weight.

⚠️ **Resisting force** is shear strength, and it has exactly two parts.

- ⚠️ **Cohesion (c)** is strength that exists **independently of the normal load**: clay mineral bonds, cementation, **root reinforcement**, and the *apparent cohesion* that surface tension gives damp sand. ❗ Root reinforcement is the term that makes ✅ Khullar's statement intelligible — that **increasing vegetation cover is the cheapest and most effective way of arresting landslides**, because it **binds the top layer of soil to the layers below**. That is a cohesion term, and it is the only one a land manager can add cheaply.
- ⚠️ **Friction**, written **(N − u) tan φ**, is strength that depends on how hard the surfaces are pressed together. **N** is the normal component of weight. **φ** is the angle of internal friction, which is a property of the material's grain shape, packing and interlock. **u** is pore-water pressure, and it **subtracts**.

⚠️ **The factor of safety** is simply resisting divided by driving. Above one, stable; at one, limiting equilibrium; below one, failure. ❗ The pedagogically decisive feature is in the algebra: **u appears with a minus sign inside the resisting term**, so **rising pore pressure reduces strength without adding anything to the slope**. A slope can therefore fail during rain **without receiving any new material and without being cut**, purely because the water table within it rose.

### 3. The three things water does, separated

⚠️ The core owner's own phrasing — *"speed depends on slope gradient, debris weight and rain lubrication"* — names three variables, and "lubrication" is the one that needs unpacking, because as ordinarily understood it is the **least important** of water's effects.

- ⚠️ **It adds mass.** Saturated regolith is heavier than dry regolith, so **W** rises and with it the driving force. ❗ Note the subtlety: **W cos b** also rises, so friction rises too; the net effect of mass alone is adverse but modest, because it acts on both sides of the balance.
- ⚠️ **It raises pore-water pressure.** Water standing in the pores **carries part of the load that the grain contacts would otherwise carry**, so the effective stress pressing the grains together falls, and with it the frictional strength. ❗ **This is the dominant mechanism**, it acts only on the resisting side, and it is strongly non-linear in antecedent conditions: a slope that is already wet from a week of rain responds to an additional day far more than a dry slope does.
- ⚠️ **It destroys apparent cohesion and softens clay.** Damp sand stands in a vertical face because surface tension between grains supplies apparent cohesion; **saturate it and that strength vanishes entirely**. Clays soften and lose strength as water content rises.

❗ **The answer-writing consequence is exact.** ⚠️ The phrase "rain lubricated the slope" scores poorly because it names an effect that is real but marginal and conceals the effect that matters. ❗ The phrase that scores is: *"prolonged rain raised pore-water pressure within the regolith, reducing the effective stress on the potential slip surface and therefore the frictional component of shear strength, while the added mass raised the driving force."*

### 4. The perched water table and the slump mechanism

✅ Leong supplies a specific and highly examinable configuration. **Permeable debris resting on impermeable clay produces a perched water body**, which lubricates a **curved slip surface** and allows **rotational failure** — a slump.

⚠️ Unpack why this geometry is so dangerous. Water percolating through the permeable upper layer is **stopped at the clay**, so it accumulates **exactly at the contact** — which is precisely where the slip surface will form. ⚠️ Three failures compound at that one horizon: pore pressure is highest there; the clay itself **softens** as it wets; and the contact is already a **mechanical discontinuity**. ❗ The result is **rotational** rather than planar because the strength contrast is at a defined horizon and the overlying mass is relatively thick, so the failure surface curves and the block **back-tilts** as it moves — which is the diagnostic field evidence for a slump.

⚠️ **The Indian relevance is immediate and reappears at Lesson 12.** A settlement built on **old landslide debris** — loose, permeable, unsorted material — resting on **less permeable material beneath**, with **surface drainage disturbed** and **wastewater discharged into the ground**, reproduces exactly this configuration at village scale.

### 5. What the balance explains that a list of causes cannot

⚠️ Four commonly observed facts fall straight out of the algebra, and deriving them rather than listing them is what an "examine" directive rewards.

- ⚠️ **Why failures cluster in the monsoon but not on its first day.** Pore pressure depends on **antecedent moisture** as well as on current rainfall; the profile must fill before pressures rise. ❗ Hence the core owner's causal control naming **antecedent moisture** as a factor to be tested.
- ⚠️ **Why toe cutting is disproportionately dangerous.** Removing material at the toe removes the **buttress** that was contributing resisting force to the whole mass above it, so the factor of safety of a large volume falls because of a small excavation. ❗ That is why ✅ Khullar's four-class checklist at Lesson 12 names **"excavation, especially at the slope toe"** first among man-made causes.
- ⚠️ **Why deforestation matters beyond erosion.** Root systems supply cohesion; remove them and **c** falls. ❗ And the loss is **delayed** — roots decay over several years after felling, so the strength reduction peaks well after the visible disturbance.
- ⚠️ **Why rapid drawdown fails slopes.** When an impounded water level falls quickly, the **external water pressure supporting the slope disappears** while the **internal pore pressure has not yet dissipated**, so the balance swings adversely. ❗ This is the physical reason **"rapid draw-down"** and **"reservoir draw-down"** both appear in the checklist at Lesson 12.

### 6. India hook — the Western Ghats scarp, a standing force-balance problem

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The Western Ghats' scarp face is India's clearest standing demonstration of the balance, because **every term in the equation is adverse at once**, and each one has been established earlier in this file.

⚠️ **Driving force:** the scarp is steep, so **sin b** is large; and the regolith is thick, because Lesson 4's dials — high rainfall, warm temperatures, dense cover, old surfaces — produce deep chemical alteration here. ⚠️ **Mass:** that lateritic regolith is clay-rich, so it **absorbs and retains a large mass of water**, and the region receives rainfall that ✅ one definition sets above **200 centimetres** for laterite to form at all. ⚠️ **Cohesion:** laterite's **low base-exchange capacity and poor fertility** mean the vegetation it supports is often plantation or degraded cover rather than deep-rooted forest, so the root-reinforcement term is weaker than the rainfall suggests. ⚠️ **Pore pressure:** intense monsoon rainfall on a thick, retentive mantle is precisely the condition that raises **u**. ⚠️ **Geometry:** where the weathered mantle rests on less altered rock beneath, the configuration is Leong's **permeable-over-impermeable** contact, and the slip surface has already been located by the weathering profile.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook, and it is a strong one.** ⚠️ **No measured value of any term is available to this edition for any Western Ghats slope** — no cohesion, no friction angle, no factor of safety, no regolith thickness. The worked number in this lesson's visual is explicitly illustrative. ❗ The hook is therefore a **structural argument about which terms are adverse**, not a stability analysis, and an answer should present it that way. ⚠️ Second, 🔁 **the risk-management half** — zonation programmes, early warning, regulation of hill-slope development — belongs to the disaster-management owner and appears here only as a route. ⚠️ Third, this lesson deliberately does **not** claim the Western Ghats laterite profile in the sense that Lesson 4 did: Lesson 4 used the profile as evidence about **weathering depth under a control set**, whereas this lesson uses the scarp as a **force-balance geometry**; they are different objects in the same region, and the coverage matrix records the overlap rather than hiding it.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What the force balance explains | What it does not |
|---|---|---|
| The two-term resisting force | Why root cover and toe support are both strength, and why removing either is dangerous | The **actual values** of c and φ for any Indian material — none is available here |
| Pore pressure as a minus sign | Why a slope fails in rain **without any new load**, and why antecedent moisture matters | **When** the threshold is crossed; that requires monitoring, not a model |
| Rapid drawdown | Why reservoir operation and rapid draw-down appear among landslide causes | The magnitude of the effect, which depends on permeability and rate |
| The perched system | Why failures locate at a permeability contrast and why slumps rotate | Whether a given site has such a contact; that is site investigation |

**Strongest objection.** This is soil mechanics, not geography. A General Studies candidate will never be asked to compute a factor of safety, so the derivation is wasted effort.

**Reply.** The computation is not the point; the **structure of the expression** is. ⚠️ Three examinable conclusions are visible in the algebra and invisible in any list of landslide causes. **One:** pore pressure appears **inside** the resisting term with a minus sign, which is why a slope fails without anything being added — and that single observation refutes the "lubrication" framing that costs marks. **Two:** cohesion appears as a **separate additive term**, which is why vegetation is a strength measure and not merely an erosion measure, and why ✅ the sources can call it the cheapest and most effective control. **Three:** the driving term contains **sin b** and the resisting term **cos b**, so **steepening hurts twice** — it raises the driving force and lowers the normal force simultaneously. ❗ A candidate who can say that has explained why hill-road cutting is the single most consequential human intervention on an Indian slope, which is a geography conclusion reached by a mechanics route.

⚠️ **Residual.** The limit-equilibrium framework used here treats the slope as a **rigid block on a defined surface**, and real failures are progressive: strength is mobilised unevenly, the surface develops rather than pre-existing, and materials lose strength after peak. ❗ Neither owner nor book available to this edition develops that refinement, and this lesson therefore presents the balance as an **explanatory frame, not a predictive tool** — which is also exactly why Lesson 13 insists that susceptibility mapping is not prediction.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I); **"Disaster and disaster management" as support only** (General Studies Paper III) — ❗ mechanism only.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the **mechanism** for the 2021 General Studies Paper I demand comparing Himalayan and Western Ghats landslide causation, and for the 2019 and 2021 General Studies Paper III landslide demands — ❗ **all three are 🔁 routed to the disaster-management owner**, and this file carries the physics and hands over the response.
- **Probable framing:** as the second paragraph of any landslide answer, immediately after the setting is established and immediately before causes are classified.
- **Traps:** writing "rain lubricated the slope"; treating added weight as water's main effect; forgetting that steepening reduces the normal force as well as raising the driving force; ignoring antecedent moisture; treating root cover as an erosion measure only; quoting a factor of safety for an Indian slope.
- **Answer use:** *"A slope fails when the factor of safety — resisting force over driving force — falls below one; prolonged rain achieves this without adding any load, because pore-water pressure reduces the effective stress on the slip surface and therefore the frictional component of strength."* Two clauses, and every subsequent cause can be attached to a term.

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Driving force = W sin b.** Increased by **steepening** and by **added weight** (saturation, construction, spoil) · **Resisting force = cohesion + friction = c + (N − u) tan φ**, with **N = W cos b**.
- ⚠️ **Factor of safety = resisting / driving.** > 1 stable · = 1 limiting equilibrium · **< 1 failure**.
- ❗ **u subtracts inside the resisting term** → a slope fails **without anything being added to it**.
- ⚠️ **Water does three things:** (1) **adds mass** → driving up (but friction up too, so net effect modest) (2) **raises pore pressure** → effective stress down → friction down — ❗ **the dominant effect** (3) **destroys apparent cohesion** in sand and **softens clay**.
- ❗ ❌ **"Lubrication" is the weakest of the three and conceals the strongest.** Write effective stress.
- ⚠️ **Cohesion includes root reinforcement** — which is why ✅ increasing vegetation cover is *"the cheapest and most effective way of arresting landslides"*, binding the top layer to the layers below.
- ⚠️ **φ**, the angle of internal friction, is a material property of grain shape, packing and interlock.
- ✅ **Slump mechanism:** **permeable debris on impermeable clay → perched water body → curved slip surface → rotational failure.** ⚠️ Three failures compound at one horizon: highest pore pressure, clay softening, pre-existing discontinuity. Diagnostic: the block **back-tilts**.
- ⚠️ **Antecedent moisture** explains why failures cluster late in the monsoon, not on its first day.
- ⚠️ **Toe cutting removes a buttress**, so a small excavation lowers the factor of safety of a large mass — ✅ which is why *"excavation, especially at the slope toe"* heads the man-made causes at Lesson 12.
- ⚠️ **Deforestation cuts c, and the loss is delayed** by several years as roots decay.
- ⚠️ **Rapid drawdown:** external support is removed before internal pore pressure dissipates.
- ⚠️ **Steepening hurts twice:** it raises **sin b** and lowers **cos b** in the same move.
- 🇮🇳 **Western Ghats scarp:** steep, thick retentive lateritic regolith, >200 cm rainfall, weaker root term under plantation or degraded cover, permeable-over-less-permeable contact. ❗ **No measured c, φ, FoS or regolith thickness is available or asserted.**
- ⚠️ **Residual:** limit equilibrium treats the slope as a rigid block on a pre-existing surface; real failure is progressive. ❗ The frame explains; it does not predict.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two hill slopes in the same district have identical rock, identical angle and identical rainfall. Slope A is under mature forest and its toe is untouched. Slope B was clear-felled four years ago and a road cutting was driven across its base last year. Using the force balance, state which terms have changed and why Slope B is now much more likely to fail — and explain why the felling's effect may be worse now than it was in the first year.

**Model answer:** Three terms have changed on Slope B, and the changes act on both sides of the balance. **Cohesion has fallen.** Root systems contribute directly to the cohesion term, binding the upper regolith to the material below, and clear-felling removes that reinforcement. **The normal and resisting geometry at the toe has been destroyed.** Cutting a road across the base removes material that was acting as a buttress, supporting the mass above it; because the resisting contribution of that buttress applied to the whole overlying volume, a small excavation lowers the factor of safety of a large mass, which is why excavation at the slope toe heads the list of man-made landslide causes in the standard checklist. **The driving geometry has worsened.** A cutting also steepens the local profile, and steepening acts twice — it increases the downslope component of weight, which varies with the sine of the angle, while reducing the normal component, which varies with the cosine, and the normal component is what generates friction. **Why the felling's effect may be worse now than in year one:** root reinforcement does not disappear at felling. The existing root network continues to provide cohesion while it remains intact and decays progressively over subsequent years, while regenerating vegetation has not yet developed a comparable root system. The cohesion term therefore passes through a **minimum some years after felling**, which is precisely where Slope B now is. The fourth variable, water, has not changed between the slopes — but its effect is now larger on Slope B, because with cohesion reduced and the toe removed, the same rise in pore-water pressure, which subtracts from the effective stress and so from frictional strength, is enough to carry the factor of safety below one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat deforestation as an erosion issue alone. Its landslide effect runs through the cohesion term of shear strength, and that effect is **delayed and non-monotonic** — the slope is often most vulnerable several years after felling rather than immediately.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Rain does not lubricate a slope; it unloads it." Examine the statement as an account of landslide mechanics. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is a deliberate overcorrection, and right about the substance. Shear strength on a potential slip surface is cohesion plus a frictional term proportional to effective stress — the normal force minus pore-water pressure. Prolonged rain raises pore pressure, which subtracts from the normal force, so the grains are pressed together less firmly and the frictional component falls. In that sense the slope is unloaded: strength is removed without anything being taken away. Rain also adds mass, raising the driving force, and destroys the apparent cohesion surface tension gives damp sand, so the account is threefold. Lubrication in the ordinary sense — a film reducing friction between surfaces — is the least important of the three and misleads, because it suggests an external agent rather than a change in effective stress within the material. The qualification is that its rhetorical inversion should not displace the mass and cohesion effects.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for shear strength written or described as cohesion plus a term in effective stress; 3 for pore pressure identified as subtracting from the normal force with the consequence for friction stated; 2 for the two further effects of water — added mass and loss of apparent cohesion — named; 1 for explaining why "lubrication" misleads; 1 for the qualification against over-correcting. Deduct 3 for presenting lubrication as the principal mechanism, and 2 for any unattributed numerical stability value.

## Lesson 11 — Mass-movement typology: creep, flow, slide and fall

Progress: 11 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: The classifying axes of velocity and water content; soil creep, soil flow, slide and fall; mass wasting defined; and the solifluction correction

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: core owner §1 and §4-§5 — soil creep as *"slow, near-continuous soil movement — tilts trees, fences, posts; debris piles at slope foot"*, landslides as *"rapid fall of a large soil/rock mass on steep or undercut slopes; triggered by rivers/sea undercutting, earthquakes, or man-made cuttings"*, and the trap that ❌ *"Soil creep is a rapid, visible movement"*; G.C. Leong, local-scan pp.34-36 — the three-fold typology of soil creep, soil flow ("solifluction", used loosely, including mud-flow and Irish bog-bursts) and landslides (slumping and sliding), with the perched-water slump mechanism; D.R. Khullar, printed p.268 (local scan p.151) — landslide defined as rapid downslope movement of rock, soil and vegetation under gravity, the natural removal of soil and rock from slopes named as **mass wasting**, and solifluction explained through overlying weight plus lubricating water.
CA search: "mass movement typology creep debris flow rockfall India official 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in the last six months. ❗ Nil return recorded for a typology lesson; the current anchors are at Lessons 12 and 13.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
  THE TWO-AXIS TYPOLOGY SCATTER
  x = WATER CONTENT (dry -> saturated) ; y = VELOCITY (slow -> fast)
  Every member of the family is located by TWO numbers, not by a name.

   FAST
   (m/s)   |                                      *ROCK FALL
           |                                       free fall / bounce
           |  *ROCK                                DRY, very fast,
           |   AVALANCHE                           no water needed
           |                        *DEBRIS
           |                         FLOW          *SLUMP
           |                                        rotational, on a
   m/min   |                                        CURVED surface,
           |                                        block BACK-TILTS
           |            *SLIDE (translational)      <- perched water
           |             planar, along a            at a permeability
           |             discontinuity              contrast
   m/day   |
           |                         *MUD FLOW
           |                          very high water content;
           |                          behaves as a FLUID
   m/year  |   *SOIL CREEP                      *SOLIFLUCTION
           |    imperceptible;                   (STRICT SENSE):
           |    TILTS trees, fences,             saturated regolith
           |    posts; debris piles              flowing over FROZEN
           |    at the slope foot                ground -- PERIGLACIAL
           |                                     + Irish BOG-BURSTS as
   SLOW    +-------------------------------------  an end-member of flow
           DRY                                   SATURATED

   THE DIAGNOSTIC QUESTION FOR EACH FAMILY:
     FALL   -- did the material leave the surface?            yes -> fall
     SLIDE  -- did a coherent mass move on a DEFINED surface? yes -> slide
                planar surface = translational ; curved = SLUMP
     FLOW   -- did the material DEFORM INTERNALLY like a fluid? yes -> flow
     CREEP  -- is the movement IMPERCEPTIBLE and continuous?   yes -> creep

   ** SUBSIDENCE sits OFF this scatter: it is predominantly VERTICAL,
      not downslope, and it is included in the advanced owner's
      classification table for India.  It is the hinge to L12. **

   ** SNOW is a medium of mass movement too -- one contrast sentence
      only; avalanche doctrine is ROUTED to the disaster owner. **
```

Two continuous axes, not a list of names. ❗ The scatter is the right grammar because **every member of the family is a position rather than a category**, and the boundaries between slide, flow and creep are gradational — which is exactly what makes a name-based classification unreliable in the field.

### 1. Plain intuition before terminology

⚠️ Everything on a slope is moving downhill. The only questions are **how fast** and **how the material behaves while it moves** — as a block, as a fluid, or as something in between. ⚠️ Those two questions are the two axes, and the familiar names are regions of that space.

⚠️ The second idea worth installing early is **the internal-deformation test**. If the moving mass arrives at the bottom still recognisable as the thing that left the top, it **slid**. If it arrives spread out, mixed and unrecognisable, it **flowed**. ❗ That single test separates the two largest families without any vocabulary at all.

### 2. The sources' typologies, reconciled

✅ **Leong gives three families:** **1. soil creep · 2. soil flow · 3. landslides (slumping and sliding)**. ✅ **Khullar** defines a **landslide** as the rapid downslope movement of rock, soil and vegetation under gravity, and names the natural removal of soil and rock from slopes as **mass wasting**. ✅ The core owner contributes creep's diagnostic signature and the landslide's trigger list.

⚠️ **Rockfall and topple** are not separately developed in either book's three-fold scheme but belong in any complete typology, and they matter in India because they are the dominant hazard along **hill road cuttings and river-undercut cliffs** — where a near-vertical face releases material that leaves the surface entirely. ⚠️ They are therefore included here as a fourth family, with that provenance stated: **the fall family is added for completeness, not quoted from either book's list.**

✅ **Creep, in detail**, because its signature is the most examinable thing in the lesson. ✅ It is **slow, near-continuous soil movement** that **tilts trees, fences and posts**, with **debris piling at the slope foot**. ❗ The core owner records the trap explicitly: ❌ *"Soil creep is a rapid, visible movement"* is wrong — **creep is slow and imperceptible, and landslides are the rapid form of mass movement.** ⚠️ The mechanism is worth one sentence: repeated **expansion and contraction** — by wetting and drying, by freezing and thawing, by heating and cooling — lifts particles perpendicular to the slope and lets them settle vertically, so each cycle displaces them a minute distance downhill. ❗ That is why creep needs **no single event** and leaves **no scar**, and why its evidence is read from **deformed vertical objects** rather than from a landform.

✅ **Slump, in detail**, because its mechanism is the one that reappears at Lesson 12. ✅ Permeable debris resting on impermeable clay produces a **perched water body**, which lubricates a **curved slip surface** and allows **rotational failure**. ⚠️ The field diagnostic is the **back-tilted block** and an arcuate scarp at the head.

### 3. The solifluction correction

❌ ✅ **Normalisation, declared, and this is the lesson's misconception note.** ✅ **Leong uses "solifluction" for the whole soil-flow family**, including mud-flow and Irish bog-bursts; ✅ **Khullar also uses it loosely**, explaining it through overlying weight plus lubricating water.

❗ **Modern usage is narrower and specific: solifluction is a periglacial process — the slow flow of saturated regolith over frozen ground.** ⚠️ The **permafrost or seasonally frozen substrate is not incidental; it is the mechanism.** It acts as an impermeable base that prevents downward drainage, so the thawed active layer above becomes saturated and flows over it at rates of centimetres per year, producing the lobate terraces of periglacial slopes.

❗ **Why the correction matters rather than being pedantry.** ⚠️ Used loosely, "solifluction" names any wet flow, which makes it a **synonym for debris flow** and destroys a genuinely informative term. ⚠️ Used strictly, it carries a **climatic diagnosis**: a slope showing solifluction lobes is telling you that **the ground freezes**. ❗ In India, strict solifluction is therefore a **high-Himalayan periglacial phenomenon**, not a Western Ghats or a general monsoon one — and a candidate who writes "solifluction" for a Kerala debris flow has made a climatic claim they did not intend.

⚠️ **The correct terms for the warm-wet cases are available and precise:** **debris flow** for a rapid, coarse, water-charged flow, and **mud flow** for one dominated by fine material at very high water content. ✅ Leong's **Irish bog-bursts** remain usable as a **vivid end-member of the flow family** — a saturated organic mass failing and flowing — and are worth exactly one sentence as such.

### 4. Subsidence — the member that is not downslope

✅ The advanced owner's classification table for India includes **subsidence** alongside sheet, rill and gully erosion, wind erosion, landslides, groundwater over-extraction, contamination and liquefaction, and defines it as occurring where **weathered, water-saturated debris is over-loaded by unplanned construction** — naming it **a slow mass-movement hazard**.

⚠️ **Subsidence sits off the scatter deliberately**, because its displacement is **predominantly vertical rather than downslope**. ❗ But it belongs in this lesson for two reasons. **One:** its **material conditions are identical** to those of slope failure — weathered debris, saturation, loading — so it is the same physics with a different displacement direction. **Two:** it is the advanced owner's **bridge to the Joshimath case** of Lesson 12, and dropping it would break that bridge.

⚠️ **Two distinct subsidence mechanisms must be kept apart**, because conflating them produces a common error. **Loading subsidence** occurs where construction load exceeds the bearing capacity of loose saturated material, which compacts under it. **Abstraction subsidence** occurs where **groundwater withdrawal** reduces pore pressure in a compressible aquifer system, so the sediment skeleton carries more of the overburden load and compacts — a direct consequence of Lesson 10's effective-stress principle operating vertically. ❗ The second is the link to Lesson 17's over-abstraction material, and it is why the advanced owner's table places subsidence and groundwater over-extraction in the same list.

🔁 ❗ **Liquefaction is named in the same advanced-owner table and is not taught here.** It is **owned by the vulcanism-and-earthquakes topic**, which holds seismicity, ground behaviour and liquefaction in full. ⚠️ This file's only statement about it is the cross-reference itself, and the observation that it shares with mass movement the dependence on **saturated, loose, uniformly graded material** — the material that Lessons 2 and 8 have been producing throughout.

### 5. Snow as a medium — one sentence, then routed

🔁 ✅ **Snow is also a medium of mass movement**, and avalanches — snow, debris and rock — are classified, zoned and managed in India as a distinct hazard family with their own impact-pressure zones, prone areas and control measures. ❗ **That is the whole of this file's treatment.** ⚠️ **Avalanche doctrine in all its parts — zone definitions, prone-area inventories, hardware and software control measures, preventive triggering and the event record — is owned by the disaster-management owner**, and is routed there by name rather than summarised here. ❗ The contrast is retained for one purpose only: to establish that **the typology of this lesson is a typology of material behaviour, not of rock and soil specifically**, and that snow occupies the same scatter at its own velocities.

### 6. India hook — the Kanara coast cliff fall

🇮🇳 ✅ Khullar records a cliff-fall case on the **Kanara coast**, and it is the right hook for this lesson because it is the family the books' three-fold scheme omits and the one whose mechanism is purest.

⚠️ The sequence is a four-step argument and every step is a term from Lesson 10. **One:** wave action **undercuts the cliff base**, which is toe removal — the buttress is destroyed by the sea instead of by an excavator. **Two:** an **overhang** develops, so part of the mass is now unsupported and the driving moment about the remaining contact grows. **Three:** tensile stress concentrates at the back of the overhang, where rock is weakest, and joints open. **Four:** the block **detaches and falls**, leaving the surface entirely, which is what distinguishes a fall from a slide.

⚠️ **Why this case earns its place:** it is the clearest Indian demonstration that **a mass movement can occur with no water in the material at all.** The water in this case is **external** — it is the agent removing support, not the agent reducing strength. ❗ That separation matters, because the standard monsoon narrative attributes every Indian slope failure to rainfall acting within the slope, and the fall family refutes the generalisation.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ The case is recorded in the source as a **descriptive instance** without dates, dimensions or a monitoring record, and **none is asserted here**. ⚠️ Second, 🔁 **coastal erosion as a process and as a management problem is owned by the coasts topic**; this lesson uses the case only as a **mass-movement mechanism**, in exactly the way Lesson 8 used the Kerala 32-kilometre figure only as an agent of soil loss. ⚠️ Third, the generalisation it supports is bounded: **undercut-and-fall is characteristic of resistant, jointed rock in cliffs**, and it would be wrong to extend it to the deep-regolith slopes that dominate Indian landslide statistics.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Family | ⚠️ Velocity | ⚠️ Water | ⚠️ Internal deformation | ⚠️ Diagnostic field evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Creep** | Imperceptible; years | Low to moderate; cyclic wetting matters | Continuous, distributed | ✅ **Tilted trees, fences and posts; debris piled at the slope foot** |
| **Flow** (debris, mud, ✅ strict solifluction) | Minutes to years | **High to saturated** | **Fluid-like; the mass is unrecognisable afterwards** | Lobate deposits; levees; ✅ periglacial lobes where the substrate freezes |
| **Slide** (translational; ✅ slump) | Seconds to days | Moderate to high; ✅ **perched water at a contrast** | **Coherent; the block survives the journey** | Defined slip surface; ✅ **back-tilted block and arcuate scarp** for a slump |
| **Fall** | Seconds | **May be zero** | None in transit | Scree or talus; fresh scar; overhang remnant |
| ✅ **Subsidence** | Slow | High — ✅ saturated debris, or abstraction-driven | Compaction | ⚠️ **Predominantly vertical**; cracked structures without downslope displacement |

**Strongest objection.** The typology is unstable. Real events begin as one type and become another — a slump that disintegrates into a debris flow is one event with two names — so classifying them is a bookkeeping exercise.

**Reply.** The objection describes a real phenomenon and draws the wrong conclusion from it. ⚠️ **Transitions are exactly why the two-axis scatter is the right grammar**: an event that begins as a coherent slump and disintegrates into a flow has **moved across the scatter** as its water content rose and its structure broke down, and that trajectory is observable and informative. ❗ A list of names cannot represent a trajectory; a position in a two-variable space can. ⚠️ Moreover, the classification has **operational consequences that differ sharply**: a fall needs catch structures and scaling, a slide needs drainage and toe support, a flow needs channel management and run-out zoning, and creep needs no emergency response at all but will destroy a structure over decades. ❗ Misclassification therefore produces the wrong engineering, which is a practical cost rather than a bookkeeping one.

⚠️ **Residual.** The **boundary between creep and very slow flow is genuinely arbitrary**, and no source available to this edition offers a velocity threshold separating them. ❗ Similarly, the books' three-fold scheme omits the fall family, and this edition has added it with that addition declared. ⚠️ An answer should therefore use the typology to **describe behaviour** and avoid asserting that a named Indian event belongs to one class unless the source it comes from says so.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I); **"Disaster and disaster management" as support only** (General Studies Paper III).
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. ⚠️ Foundation for the three 🔁 routed landslide demands of 2019, 2021 General Studies Paper III and 2021 General Studies Paper I; and the lesson that carries the advanced owner's **subsidence** row, which is the bridge to Lesson 12's worked case.
- **Probable framing:** as a short classifying paragraph that demonstrates precision — naming the family, the velocity order and the diagnostic evidence — before the answer moves to causation.
- **Traps:** calling creep rapid or visible; using "solifluction" for any wet flow; treating subsidence as a landslide; treating liquefaction as this topic's material; omitting the fall family; assuming every mass movement requires water in the material.
- **Answer use:** classify by behaviour, not by name. *"The event was a slump rather than a flow: the displaced mass remained coherent, moved on a curved surface and back-tilted, which indicates a perched water body at a permeability contrast rather than general saturation."* That sentence demonstrates mechanism, evidence and vocabulary at once.

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Two axes classify the family: velocity and water content.** Names are regions, not categories.
- ⚠️ **The internal-deformation test:** arrives recognisable → **slide**; arrives mixed and spread → **flow**.
- ✅ **Leong's three families:** soil creep · soil flow · landslides (slumping and sliding). ⚠️ **Fall and topple added here for completeness, with the addition declared.**
- ✅ **Khullar:** a **landslide** is rapid downslope movement of rock, soil and vegetation under gravity; natural removal of soil and rock from slopes = **mass wasting**.
- ✅ **Creep:** slow, near-continuous; ✅ **tilts trees, fences and posts**; debris piles at the slope foot. ❌ ❗ **Not rapid and not visible** — the owner's trap.
- ⚠️ **Creep mechanism:** repeated expansion and contraction lifts particles normal to the slope and lets them settle vertically → minute net downhill displacement per cycle. **No event, no scar.**
- ✅ **Slump:** permeable debris on impermeable clay → **perched water body** → **curved slip surface** → **rotational failure**. ⚠️ Diagnostic: **back-tilted block, arcuate scarp**.
- ❌ ✅ **SOLIFLUCTION CORRECTION:** both books use the term loosely for all soil flow. ❗ **Strictly it is periglacial — saturated regolith flowing over frozen ground**, where the frozen substrate is the mechanism. ❗ In India it is a **high-Himalayan** phenomenon; use **debris flow** or **mud flow** for warm-wet cases.
- ✅ **Irish bog-bursts** retained as a one-sentence end-member of the flow family.
- ✅ **Subsidence** (advanced owner's table): weathered, water-saturated debris **over-loaded by unplanned construction**; a **slow mass-movement hazard**. ⚠️ **Predominantly vertical**, so it sits off the scatter.
- ⚠️ **Two subsidence mechanisms:** **loading** (bearing capacity exceeded) and **abstraction** (pore pressure falls, the skeleton carries more load, the system compacts) — ❗ the second is Lesson 10's effective stress acting vertically, and the link to Lesson 17.
- 🔁 ❗ **Liquefaction is named in the same table and is owned by the vulcanism-and-earthquakes topic.** Cross-referenced only.
- 🔁 ✅ **Snow is a medium of mass movement** — one contrast sentence. ❗ **Avalanche doctrine is routed to the disaster-management owner in full.**
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Kanara coast cliff fall:** wave undercutting → overhang → tensile failure at the back → block detaches and falls. ❗ **Mass movement with no water in the material**; the water is the external agent removing support.
- ⚠️ **Residual:** no velocity threshold separates creep from very slow flow; transitions between families are real, which is why a scatter and not a list.

### Concept check

**Question:** A report describes a hillside event in Kerala as "solifluction caused by monsoon rain". Separately, it describes the slow tilting of electricity poles on an adjacent slope as "a small landslide". Correct both descriptions and explain what each correct term implies that the incorrect one does not.

**Model answer:** Both terms are wrong, and in each case the error carries a false implication. **"Solifluction" in Kerala.** Both of the books used here employ the word loosely for any flow of saturated soil, but modern usage restricts it to a **periglacial** process: the slow flow of a saturated active layer over **frozen ground**, where the frozen substrate is not incidental scenery but the mechanism, since it prevents downward drainage and so keeps the layer above saturated. Applying the term in Kerala therefore makes an unintended **climatic claim** — that the ground freezes — which is false for the Western Ghats. The correct terms are **debris flow** for a rapid, coarse, water-charged flow, or **mud flow** where fine material at very high water content dominates. What the correct term implies that the incorrect one does not is the **absence of a frozen base**, and therefore a different set of preconditions: saturation of a thick weathered mantle by intense rainfall, rather than seasonal thaw above permafrost. **"A small landslide" for tilting poles.** Tilting of vertical objects with no scar, no displaced mass and no defined failure surface is the signature of **soil creep**, which is slow, near-continuous and imperceptible, and which the core owner explicitly contrasts with landslides as the rapid form of mass movement. The implication that differs is decisive for management: a landslide is an **event** requiring emergency response and a located failure surface, whereas creep is a **continuous process** requiring no emergency response at all but guaranteeing progressive damage to any rigid structure founded in the moving layer over decades. Calling creep a landslide invites the wrong response in both directions — an unnecessary evacuation now, and no foundation design later.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat mass-movement terms as interchangeable intensity labels. Each name carries an implied mechanism, and in the case of solifluction an implied climate, so a misapplied term asserts something about the site that the evidence does not support.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Classify mass movements by behaviour rather than by name." Discuss, with Indian illustration. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Behaviour classification uses two axes — velocity and water content — and one test: did the mass deform internally? Names become positions rather than categories, which matters because real events migrate across the space: a slump that disintegrates downslope becomes a debris flow, and only a two-variable scheme represents that trajectory. The test also fixes the field diagnosis. Creep is identified by tilted trees, fences and posts with no scar; a slump by a curved surface and a back-tilted block, which indicates perched water at a permeability contrast rather than general saturation; a fall by material that left the surface entirely, as in the undercut cliff of the Kanara coast, where no water need be inside the mass. Misclassification has engineering cost: falls need catch structures, slides drainage and toe support, flows run-out zoning. The qualification is that creep and very slow flow have no agreed boundary.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for velocity and water content named as the classifying axes with the internal-deformation test; 3 for three families correctly diagnosed by field evidence, including creep's tilted objects; 2 for one Indian illustration used mechanically rather than decoratively; 1 for the differing engineering responses; 1 for the creep-to-flow boundary acknowledged as arbitrary. Deduct 2 for describing creep as rapid or visible, and 2 for using solifluction outside a periglacial setting.

## Lesson 12 — Landslide causation: four classes, and why a trigger is not the complete cause

Progress: 12 / 19  |  Stage: Applied  |  Subtopic: The four-class causal checklist; preparatory against triggering factors; the hill-road debris anchor; elements at risk; and the four-term risk vocabulary

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: core owner's causal control — *"intense rain is a trigger, not a complete landslide cause ... Antecedent moisture, discontinuities, toe cutting, drainage, recharge and demand must be tested before attribution"* — and its terminology control separating **susceptibility, hazard, vulnerability and risk**; advanced owner §2 and §6 — subsidence where weathered, water-saturated debris is over-loaded by unplanned construction, and the Joshimath anchor at lines 46-47 and 64-67, stating that **old landslide material, poor drainage, construction loading and slope instability** are jointly implicated and that *"no single trigger explains the whole crisis"*; D.R. Khullar, printed pp.268-269 (local scan p.151) — **"A Checklist of Causes of Landslides", sourced to the *Manual on Natural Disaster Management in India* (2001), National Centre for Disaster Management, p.68**; the hill-road debris anchor; and the elements-at-risk list with **Fig. 8.17**.
CA search: "Joshimath subsidence expert committee reports CBRI NIH Wadia GSI IIT Roorkee CGWB findings Uttarakhand USDMA" — run at the time of writing.
CA found: 📰 **Uttarakhand State Disaster Management Authority, "Joshimath Reports" document category** — a first-party repository of the multi-institutional expert reports on the Joshimath subsidence, confirmed to exist at `usdma.uk.gov.in` at the time of writing. ❗ **Access limitation disclosed:** the repository page body is JavaScript-rendered and the underlying report documents could not be retrieved directly in this environment. The findings summarised below are therefore attested through reporting that cites the first-party reports and the institutions named in them, and are stated **qualitatively, with each finding attributed to the institution that is reported to have made it**. ❗ **No casualty figure, no building count, no displacement measurement and no percentage is quoted anywhere in this lesson.**

```text
  THE FOUR-CLASS CAUSE TREE
  Source: "A Checklist of Causes of Landslides", from the Manual on
```
