Geometry, geometry and an accounting identity. ❗ The budget is the lesson's most important asset, because **it is what makes "over-exploitation" a measurable claim rather than a complaint** — and because its two inflow-outflow items quietly establish that an aquifer's boundaries are hydrological, which is the deepest problem in Lesson 17.

### 1. Springs: one event, six geometries

✅ The core owner defines a spring exactly: a **natural outlet where the water table meets the surface**. ⚠️ Everything else is a question of **how the geometry arranges that meeting**, and the six types are therefore six configurations rather than six phenomena.

✅ **Springs issuing from tilted strata** — a permeable bed dipping between impermeable ones emerges at the surface, and water issues along the outcrop line. ✅ **Joint springs** — water travels through a joint system in otherwise solid rock and emerges where the joint reaches the surface. ⚠️ This is Lesson 14's "pervious along joints" observed as a landform. ✅ **Dyke springs** — an impermeable dyke cutting across permeable rock acts as an **underground dam**, forcing water to the surface on its upstream side. ⚠️ Note the Indian relevance: ❗ **this is the natural analogue of the "subsurface dyke" that appears in Lesson 18 as a rural recharge structure**, and the fact that the technique copies a natural form is worth a sentence. ✅ **Scarp-foot springs** — water emerges at the junction of permeable and impermeable beds at the base of a scarp. ✅ **Dip-slope springs** — the water table is intersected by the dip slope. ✅ **Vauclusian or resurgence springs** — water that sank underground re-emerges as a large single outlet. 🔁 **Named only; the landform assemblage it belongs to is owned by the karst topic and is not developed here.**

⚠️ **Perched water** completes the set and connects the groundwater block back to the mass-movement block. A **lens of impermeable material** within a permeable formation supports a saturated zone **above the regional water table**, with its own local water table and its own springs. ❗ Two consequences follow: a spring may issue **high on a hillside** with the valley water table far below; and — this is Lessons 10 and 11 restated — a slope may be **saturated at a contact while the regional table is tens of metres lower**, which is exactly the slump configuration.

### 2. Artesian systems: four conditions, and the limitation

✅ Leong's **Fig. 4.11** sets out the artesian basin, and all four conditions are necessary: ✅ a **downfolded synclinal basin**; ✅ a **permeable aquifer of chalk or limestone confined between impermeable clays**; ✅ **recharge at the exposed basin edges**; and ✅ **sufficient hydraulic head**.

✅ The cases are the **Great Artesian Basin of Australia**, supporting Queensland sheep farming, and the **Great Plains of the United States**.

❗ **The core owner's trap belongs here:** ❌ *"All wells are artesian"* is false — **only where a confined aquifer lies between two impermeable strata does water rise under natural pressure.** ⚠️ An artesian well is therefore a **geometry, not a depth**: a very deep well in an unconfined aquifer is not artesian, and a shallow well in a confined one may be.

⚠️ **The refinement that is worth a mark:** a **flowing** artesian well is one where the piezometric surface lies **above the ground at the well's position**. A well in the same confined aquifer at a higher elevation taps the same pressure but must still be pumped, because the ground there stands above the piezometric surface. ❗ **"Artesian" describes the aquifer's confinement and head; "flowing" describes the relationship between that head and the local ground elevation.**

✅ **And the limitation examiners like: artesian water may be hot or saline and therefore unsuitable for irrigation.** ⚠️ The reasons are structural: water that has travelled far and deep from a distant recharge edge has had **long residence time** to dissolve minerals, and has been warmed by the geothermal gradient. ❗ So an artesian system can be simultaneously **abundant and unusable**, which is a general lesson about groundwater that Lessons 16 and 17 both need — ✅ exactly as the coastal and deltaic tracts of Lesson 16 are recorded as **highly productive but quality-constrained**.

### 3. The budget: four in, four out

✅ Khullar's budget is reproduced in full above and is the frame on which Lesson 17 depends.

✅ **Items of supply to the groundwater reservoir:** (i) **precipitation infiltration to the water table**; (ii) **natural recharge from streams, lakes and ponds**; (iii) **groundwater inflow into the area under consideration**; (iv) **recharge from irrigation, reservoirs and other schemes especially designed for artificial recharge**.

✅ **Items of disposal from the reservoir:** (i) **evaporation from the capillary fringe where the water table is shallow, plus transpiration by vegetation**; (ii) **natural discharge by seepage and spring flow to streams, lakes and ponds**; (iii) **groundwater outflow**; (iv) **artificial discharge by pumping or flowing wells and drains**.

⚠️ **Five observations convert this list into the analytical tool it is.**

- ⚠️ **"Over-exploitation" acquires a definition.** It means that **disposal item (iv) has grown until total disposal exceeds total supply, for the assessment unit and period considered.** ❗ Without that frame the word is rhetoric; with it, it is a measurable claim that requires a stated unit and a stated period — which is exactly why the owner's date-and-data control demands **agency, assessment year, methodology and scale**.
- ⚠️ **Supply item (iv) is where Lesson 18 lives.** Artificial recharge is not an exotic addition to the system; it is a **named supply term in the standard budget**, and rainwater harvesting works by enlarging it.
- ⚠️ **Disposal item (iv) is where Lesson 9 lived.** Vertical drainage reclaimed waterlogged land by **deliberately increasing artificial discharge** — the same term that Lesson 17 will find in excess.
- ❗ **Items (iii) in both columns are the subtlest and most consequential.** Inflow and outflow mean that **an aquifer has boundaries, and they are hydrological, not administrative.** ⚠️ A block whose budget balances may be importing water from a neighbouring block whose budget does not; a block in deficit may be exporting. ❗ This is why aquifer-level management is the structurally correct scale and why assessment-unit accounting, while necessary, can mislead.
- ⚠️ **Disposal item (i) connects the whole budget to Lesson 9.** Evaporation from the capillary fringe **where the water table is shallow** is both a loss term in the budget and the **engine of salinisation** — the same physical process counted twice, once as water lost and once as salt gained.

❗ **An arithmetic exclusion, declared.** ⚠️ The same source presents worked groundwater arithmetic alongside this budget, and **the arithmetic does not close**: a subtraction of one quoted figure from another does not produce the quoted result; a stated percentage of a stated total does not equal the figure given for it; and a "net recharge as 90 per cent of the gross" is quoted against a gross that does not produce it. ❗ **No part of that arithmetic is reproduced in this file.** ⚠️ The passage may well be **truncated across a column break** in the two-pages-per-scan-page layout of the local copy, and this edition does **not** attempt to reconstruct it. ❗ What is taught instead is the **budget structure**, which is sound and complete, and the **units problem** itself — ⚠️ the source works in **million hectare metres** while current official reporting works in **billion cubic metres**, and mixing the two is a reliable way to produce an impossible number. ❗ That is a method lesson worth more than the arithmetic would have been.

### 4. The historical hinge

✅ Leong, writing decades before the modern groundwater literature, states the conclusion that Lesson 17 will confirm: ***"All wells bored by Man tend to deplete groundwater resources"*** — because water is extracted **faster than natural discharge and faster than rainfall replenishes it**.

⚠️ **Three observations about that sentence.** **One:** it is a **1970s-era statement that prefigures the modern over-abstraction framing exactly**, which is useful evidence that the problem was understood long before it was measured. **Two:** it is **stated as a tendency, not as a law**, and the budget shows why the qualification is correct — a well that extracts less than the natural recharge in its unit does not deplete anything. **Three:** ❗ as an **unqualified generalisation it is too strong**, and this edition says so: the budget's supply item (iv) permits a system in which extraction is matched by deliberate recharge, which is precisely the design principle of Lesson 18.

❗ **And the sentence is doing one more job.** ⚠️ It establishes that the depletion framing is **not a recent policy fashion**; the physical understanding has been stable for half a century, and what has changed is the **scale of abstraction and the quality of measurement** — which is Lesson 17's subject.

### 5. India hook — the bhabar-tarai spring line

🇮🇳 ✅ India's clearest natural spring system runs along the Himalayan foot, and the source describes it as a single hydrological machine: in the **bhabar** belt, streams emerging from the mountains **disappear into unassorted material**; in the **tarai** belt downslope, that water **seeps out and the water table is high**.

⚠️ **The mechanism is this lesson's mechanism, worked at regional scale.** The bhabar consists of **coarse, unsorted, extremely permeable** debris deposited where mountain streams lose competence at the break of slope. Surface water entering it **infiltrates completely** — which is supply item (ii) of the budget, natural recharge from streams, operating so efficiently that the stream itself disappears. ⚠️ Downslope, the material **fines** and permeability falls, so transmission capacity is exceeded, the saturated zone rises to intersect the surface, and water re-emerges — which is disposal item (ii), natural discharge by seepage and spring flow. ❗ **The tarai spring line is therefore a permeability boundary made visible**, and it is the regional-scale version of the scarp-foot spring in this lesson's visual.

⚠️ **Why it earns the hook:** the bhabar-tarai system demonstrates **four things at once** — that infiltration can equal or exceed runoff; that a stream can be a recharge source rather than a discharge sink; that the budget's inflow and outflow terms are physically real and spatially separated; and that a water table's depth is set by the **permeability structure** rather than by rainfall alone. ❗ It also previews Lesson 16 directly, where the **Ganga-Brahmaputra alluvial province with its well-defined bhabar, tarai and axial belts is recorded as the richest groundwater province in the country.**

❗ **Limitations, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ **One:** no discharge measurement, no spring-line length and no recharge volume is available to this edition for the bhabar-tarai system, and **none is asserted**. ⚠️ **Two:** the pedological consequences of the high tarai water table were taken at Lesson 5 and the province-scale groundwater consequences will be taken at Lesson 16; this lesson claims only the **spring-line mechanism**. ⚠️ **Three:** 🔁 **the land-use, agricultural and settlement history of the tarai belongs to other owners** and is not touched.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Unconfined system | Confined / artesian system |
|---|---|---|
| Upper boundary | ✅ The **water table**, free to rise and fall | A **confining layer**; the water is under pressure |
| Relevant surface | Water table — **physical** | **Piezometric surface** — a pressure statement, may lie above ground |
| Recharge | **Directly from above**, over the whole aquifer area | ✅ Only at the **exposed edges** of the aquifer |
| Response to pumping | Water table falls locally; the cone of depression is visible in nearby wells | **Pressure** falls over a wide area; the aquifer may not dewater at all |
| ❗ Characteristic limitation | Vulnerable to **surface contamination** (Lesson 17) | ✅ Water may be **hot or saline and unsuitable for irrigation** |
| Recharge response time | Fast — this is where Lesson 18's structures work | **Slow**, because recharge enters only at the edges |

**Strongest objection.** The artesian material is foreign. The type cases are Australian and American, India has no major artesian basin of that kind, and the space would be better spent on Indian aquifers.

**Reply.** Three of the four artesian conditions are **conditions India plainly has**, and the fourth is the one worth teaching. ⚠️ ✅ The source itself records that **friable semi-consolidated sandstones form moderate-yielding aquifers in India in which auto-flowing zones are not uncommon** — so confined systems with sufficient head do occur, and ✅ the **Cenozoic fault basin of the Narmada, Purna and Tapi** at Lesson 16, with **80 to 160 metres of alluvial sand, silt and clay**, is exactly the layered confining geometry the figure describes. ⚠️ More importantly, the **concept** is indispensable regardless of the type case: **confinement is what makes the piezometric surface necessary**, and the piezometric surface is one of the six terminological pairs the owner requires to be kept apart. ❗ And the **limitation** — abundant water that is hot or saline and therefore unusable — is the general lesson that Lesson 16's coastal aquifers and Lesson 17's quality material both depend on.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ **Leong's figure numbering cannot be relied on across this scan.** The chapter read here uses figures numbered 4.1 to 4.11, while the opening page of the following chapter uses a different numbering series, which suggests the scan merges sections of different editions. ⚠️ **A figure number is therefore cited only where it was visible on the rendered page that was read.** ⚠️ A second residual: the budget as presented is a **steady-state accounting identity** with no treatment of **storage change, lag or the time required for a change in recharge to appear at a well** — and those are precisely the dynamics that make depletion hard to detect early. ❗ The sources do not supply them, and this edition does not invent them.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ **foundation for the 2023 Prelims demand on India's share of global groundwater withdrawal**, because that demand is a **statistics-and-dating** question and the budget is what makes any such statistic interpretable; ❗ **the official key is unavailable locally and no answer is recorded or inferred.** Also the foundation for the **2018 General Studies Paper I demand on urban water harvesting**, since artificial recharge is a named supply term in this budget.
- **Probable framing:** the budget appears in Mains as the **definitional move** that converts a complaint into an argument — *"over-exploitation means that artificial discharge has grown until total disposal exceeds total supply, for the unit and period assessed."*
- **Traps:** saying all deep wells are artesian; treating the piezometric surface as water; assuming artesian water is good water; forgetting that confined aquifers recharge only at their edges; reproducing non-closing budget arithmetic; mixing million hectare metres with billion cubic metres; treating an assessment unit as a hydrological boundary.
- **Answer use:** *"A groundwater budget has four supply items and four disposal items; over-exploitation is the condition in which artificial discharge has grown until total disposal exceeds total supply for the unit and period assessed — which is why every such statement needs an agency, an assessment year and a scale attached."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **A spring is the saturated zone intersecting the surface.** Six geometries: **tilted strata · joint · dyke · scarp-foot · dip-slope · Vauclusian (resurgence)**. 🔁 The karst assemblage is routed.
- ⚠️ **A dyke spring is the natural analogue of the subsurface dyke** used as a recharge structure at Lesson 18.
- ⚠️ **Perched water:** an impermeable lens supports a saturated zone **above the regional water table** → high hillside springs, and ❗ **a slope saturated at a contact while the valley table is far lower** — the slump configuration of Lessons 10 and 11.
- ✅ **Artesian conditions, all four required:** **downfolded syncline · permeable aquifer (chalk/limestone) confined between impermeable clays · recharge at the exposed edges · sufficient hydraulic head.**
- ✅ **Cases:** **Great Artesian Basin, Australia** (Queensland sheep farming); **Great Plains, USA**.
- ❌ ❗ **"All wells are artesian" is false.** An artesian well is a **geometry, not a depth**. ⚠️ **Flowing** artesian = the piezometric surface lies **above the ground at that point**.
- ✅ ❗ **Limitation: artesian water may be hot or saline and therefore unsuitable for irrigation.** ⚠️ Long residence time and the geothermal gradient explain both.
- ✅ **BUDGET — supply:** (i) **precipitation infiltration** (ii) **natural recharge from streams, lakes, ponds** (iii) **groundwater inflow** (iv) **recharge from irrigation, reservoirs and artificial-recharge schemes** · **BUDGET — disposal:** (i) **evaporation from the capillary fringe where the table is shallow + transpiration** (ii) **natural discharge by seepage and spring flow** (iii) **groundwater outflow** (iv) **artificial discharge by pumping or flowing wells and drains**.
- ❗ ⚠️ **"Over-exploitation" = disposal exceeds supply, FOR A STATED UNIT AND PERIOD.** Without the frame it is rhetoric.
- ❗ **Items (iii) in both columns:** an aquifer's boundaries are **hydrological, not administrative** — a balanced block may be importing from a deficit neighbour.
- ⚠️ **Disposal item (i) is also the salinisation engine of Lesson 9** — one process counted as water lost and as salt gained.
- ❗ ❌ **EXCLUDED: the source's worked groundwater arithmetic does not close** (a subtraction, a percentage and a "net recharge as 90 per cent of gross" are each inconsistent with the figures given). ❗ **Not reproduced and not reconstructed** — it may be truncated across a column break. ⚠️ **Teach the structure and the units problem instead: million hectare metres against billion cubic metres.**
- ✅ **Leong, decades earlier:** *"All wells bored by Man tend to deplete groundwater resources"* — extraction faster than natural discharge and faster than rainfall replenishes. ⚠️ Stated as a **tendency**; ❗ too strong unqualified, because supply item (iv) permits a balanced system.
- 🇮🇳 **Bhabar-tarai spring line:** ✅ streams **disappear into unassorted bhabar material** and **seep out in the tarai where the water table is high**. ⚠️ A **permeability boundary made visible** — the regional-scale scarp-foot spring. ❗ No discharge or recharge volume asserted.
- ⚠️ **Residual:** Leong figure numbers are unreliable across this scan — cite only where visible. ⚠️ The budget is a **steady-state identity** with no storage change, lag or detection time.

### Concept check

**Question:** An official states that a block "is not over-exploited, because its annual extraction is less than its annual recharge". A hydrologist objects that the statement may be true and still misleading. Explain the hydrologist's three strongest grounds, using the structure of the groundwater budget.

**Model answer:** The hydrologist has three grounds, and all three are visible in the budget's own structure. **First, the boundary.** The budget contains a supply item for **groundwater inflow into the area under consideration** and a disposal item for **groundwater outflow**. An assessment unit is an administrative polygon, while an aquifer's boundaries are hydrological, so a block may balance only because it is importing water from a neighbouring block that is in deficit — in which case the favourable statement describes the accounting unit rather than the resource. **Second, the composition of the supply side.** Supply includes **recharge from irrigation and reservoirs**, which is return flow from water that was itself applied from somewhere. A block whose recharge is dominated by canal seepage and irrigation return is not independently sustainable: it is balanced by an imported surface-water supply, and if that supply is reduced the recharge term falls with it. The same item also links the statement to waterlogging, since recharge of that kind is exactly what raises water tables in command areas. **Third, the period and the statistic's provenance.** The budget is an **accounting identity for a stated unit and a stated period**, so an annual comparison conceals both within-year behaviour — extraction concentrated in the dry season against recharge concentrated in the monsoon — and longer-term trend, since a single balanced year says nothing about a declining series. Any such claim therefore requires the **agency, the assessment year, the methodology and the scale**, because the categorisation depends on all four. A fourth, subsidiary ground is that the statement addresses **quantity only**: a block may be in quantitative balance while its water is becoming unusable through salinity or contamination, and disposal item (i) reminds us that where the table is shallow, evaporation from the capillary fringe is concentrating salts even as the volume balances.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat an assessment-unit category as a physical description of an aquifer. It is an accounting outcome for an administrative polygon over a stated period, and its boundaries are not the resource's boundaries.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Without a budget, 'groundwater over-exploitation' is a complaint rather than a claim." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is sound. A groundwater budget has four supply items — precipitation infiltration, natural recharge from streams, lakes and ponds, groundwater inflow, and recharge from irrigation, reservoirs and artificial-recharge schemes — and four disposal items: evaporation from the capillary fringe with transpiration, seepage and spring flow, groundwater outflow, and artificial discharge by pumping wells and drains. Over-exploitation then means something precise: the artificial-discharge term has grown until total disposal exceeds total supply, **for a stated assessment unit and a stated period**. That definition carries three disciplines. It requires an agency, assessment year, methodology and scale, since the result depends on all four. It names the remedy: artificial recharge is a supply item, not an addendum. And it warns that the unit may be administrative while the resource is not. The qualification is that the identity is steady-state and omits storage change and lag.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for both sets of four budget items reproduced accurately; 3 for over-exploitation defined against the budget with the unit-and-period condition; 2 for one consequence drawn — the dating discipline, or artificial recharge as a named supply term, or the administrative-against-hydrological boundary; 1 for the steady-state qualification; 1 for using no non-closing arithmetic and no mixed units. Deduct 3 for treating over-exploitation as self-evident, and 2 for quoting a national figure without agency and year.

A province key read against a lithology column, and designed to be laid beside Lesson 7's soil strip. ❗ **The pairing is the argument**: six parent materials produce eight soil groups and eight groundwater provinces, because weathering is the common cause of both.

### 1. The sentence that integrates the topic

✅ Here is the single most important sentence in this file, quoted exactly: in India's hard-rock terrain, ***"the near surface, weathered mantle forms the all important ground water reservoir, and the source for circulation of ground water through underlying fracture systems."***

⚠️ **Unpack it in three moves**, because the sentence is doing three different jobs.

- ⚠️ **It identifies the reservoir.** In crystalline terrain the water is **not in the rock**; it is in the **weathered mantle on top of the rock** — the saprolite of Lesson 4's depth profile, in which hydrolysis has converted feldspar to clay and opened the rock along its joint grid, creating **secondary porosity** where none existed.
- ⚠️ **It identifies the conduit.** The mantle is also the **source for circulation through underlying fracture systems**, which means the mantle **stores** while the fractures **transmit** — a two-part system in which Lesson 14's two properties are supplied by two different parts of the same profile. ❗ This is why a well in hard rock needs **both**: mantle thickness for storage and a fracture intersection for yield.
- ⚠️ **It makes weathering a resource process.** ❗ Lesson 1 argued that weathering is not merely preparatory; this sentence is the evidence. **Over half of India's geographical area has no primary porosity at all, and its groundwater exists because its rock has rotted.**

✅ Three supporting statements complete the picture: **deep weathered pediments, low-lying valleys and abandoned river channels contain adequate thicknesses of porous material**; **potentially saturated systems generally occur down to about 100 metres depth**; and **friable semi-consolidated sandstones form moderate-yielding aquifers in which auto-flowing zones are not uncommon** — ⚠️ the last confirming that Lesson 15's confined-system concept has Indian application.

⚠️ **The 100-metre statement deserves a note.** It establishes that the hard-rock resource is **finite in depth as well as in area**: drilling deeper does not reach a new resource, because below the weathered and fractured zone the rock is fresh and holds nothing. ❗ That is why deepening wells is not a sustainable response to falling levels in crystalline terrain, which is a conclusion Lesson 17 needs. ⚠️ The figure is **that source's characterisation**, with no assessment year attached, and is quoted as such.

### 2. The eight provinces, with their yield logic

✅ **Dr. R.L. Singh's 1971 scheme**, reproduced in **Fig. 15.5**, divides India into eight groundwater provinces. ⚠️ Each is given below with the **reason** for its verdict, because the reason is what converts a list into reasoning.

✅ **1. Pre-Cambrian Crystalline — deficient.** Covers **over half the country's geographical area**: **Tamil Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Dandakaranya, Bundelkhand and the Aravalli range**. ⚠️ The reason is Lesson 14's bottom-left cell: crystalline rock has **no primary porosity**, so the entire resource is the weathered mantle plus fractures. ❗ **This single province is the reason the integrating sentence matters**, and it is also why the same half of India that Lesson 7 gave red and yellow soils is the half with the weakest aquifers — one weathering history, two consequences.

✅ **2. Pre-Cambrian Sedimentary — not much suitable, with inadequate ground water.** The **Cuddapah and Vindhyan basins**. ⚠️ Sedimentary rock is not automatically a good aquifer: these are **old, compacted and cemented**, so their original intergranular porosity has largely been destroyed.

✅ **3. Gondwana Sedimentary — good aquifers.** The **Barakar and Godavari river basins**. ⚠️ Younger and less cemented, so **intergranular porosity survives**.

✅ **4. Deccan Trap — deficient.** About **1,200 metres of impermeable basalt obstructing percolation**, with aquifers preserved only in **fractures**, in **secondary porosity developed in weathered *moorum***, in **intertrappean beds sandwiched between impermeable strata**, and in **vesicles and amygdales**.

✅ **5. Cenozoic Sedimentary — good aquifers.** The **Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu, Kerala and Gujarat coasts**; **tertiary sandstones**. ⚠️ Young, poorly consolidated, with **primary intergranular porosity intact**.

✅ **6. Cenozoic Fault Basin — good resource.** The **rift zone of the Narmada, Purna and Tapi**, with **80 to 160 metres of alluvial sand, silt and clay**. ⚠️ Note the **layering**: alternating sands and clays is the geometry that produces **confined aquifers**, which is where Lesson 15's auto-flowing zones would be expected.

✅ **7. Ganga-Brahmaputra Alluvial — the richest province of the country.** With well-defined **bhabar, tarai and axial belts**, where **streams disappearing into the unassorted bhabar materials seep out in the tarai**, and **the water table is high**. ⚠️ The reason is a combination no other province has: **great thickness of unconsolidated, well-sorted, permeable material**, with **continuous recharge** from a large Himalayan river system. ❗ This is Lesson 15's spring-line mechanism operating as a province.

✅ **8. Himalayan — a complex structural and geographic unit of little significance; local springs are common but wells are a rare feature.** ⚠️ Relief and structure prevent the accumulation of storage: water drains rapidly through fractured rock on steep gradients and emerges as springs rather than accumulating in a water table a well could reach. ❗ **The Himalayan groundwater problem is therefore a spring problem, not a well problem**, which is a geography an answer should state rather than treating the mountains as merely "difficult".

### 3. The four controls, and their overlap with Lesson 4

✅ The source states the controls on groundwater availability explicitly: **climate, particularly rainfall and temperature; relief features; geological structure; and the hydrological setup.**

⚠️ **The overlap with Lesson 4's six weathering controls is not a coincidence, and saying why is an answer-grade observation.** ❗ **Three of the four are the same controls**: climate, relief and structure appear in both lists. ⚠️ The reason is the integrating sentence — if the reservoir **is** the weathered mantle, then whatever controls the depth and character of weathering **controls the size of the reservoir**. ⚠️ Climate decides how intensely the rock rots; relief decides whether the mantle is retained or stripped, which is why ✅ **deep weathered pediments and low-lying valleys** hold water while scarps do not; structure decides whether fractures exist to transmit it.

⚠️ **The fourth control — the hydrological setup — is the one that is not shared**, and it is what makes groundwater availability a different question from weathering depth. ❗ It covers whether a river recharges or drains the aquifer, whether a stream disappears into a bhabar or flows over clay, and where the discharge boundaries lie — the budget's inflow and outflow terms of Lesson 15 made geographical.

### 4. Coastal aquifers: productive and quality-constrained

✅ The source records that the **coastal and deltaic tracts form a narrow linear strip around the peninsular plateau**, and that the **eastern coastal and deltaic tract and the estuarine areas of Gujarat are receptacles of thick alluvium with highly productive aquifers — but that salinity hazards impose quality constraints on development.**

✅ The core owner supplies the mechanism as a sequence: **over-pumping or reduced recharge → the freshwater head falls → the saline interface moves landward and upward → wells and soils become saline.**

⚠️ **Why the physics works, stated once so that the sequence is explanatory.** Fresh water is **less dense than sea water**, so in a coastal aquifer the fresh water floats on the saline water as a **wedge with an interface sloping inland and downward**. The position of that interface is held by the **hydraulic head of the fresh water**; reduce the head by pumping or by cutting recharge, and the interface **advances inland and rises**. ❗ Two consequences follow that candidates miss. **One:** the damage is **disproportionate to the head loss**, because a small reduction in freshwater head displaces the interface by a much larger distance. **Two:** the process is **slow to reverse**, because restoring the head does not immediately flush the salt that has entered the aquifer matrix.

⚠️ **The connection to Lesson 7 is worth making explicit.** ✅ Lesson 7 recorded that the **Gulf of Khambhat is affected by salt-laden sea tides, with the Narmada, Tapi, Mahi and Sabarmati estuaries rendered infertile**, and ✅ that saline and alkaline soils occur in **coastal Odisha, Gujarat and West Bengal**. ❗ Those are the **surface expression of the same coastal salinity system** whose subsurface expression is described here — soil and aquifer again proving to be two interfaces onto one physical situation.

🔁 ❗ **Boundary, stated precisely.** ⚠️ The **2025 General Studies Paper III demand on seawater intrusion into India's coastal aquifers is owned by the environment-and-ecology owner.** ❗ **This file teaches the saline-wedge hydraulics — and nothing else.** ⚠️ The **coastal-management response** — regulation of extraction in coastal zones, managed aquifer recharge policy, desalination, coastal regulation instruments and the institutional architecture that delivers them — is 🔁 **routed to that owner by name**, and the physical half above is offered to it as support.

### 5. India hook — the intertrappean beds and weathered *moorum* of the Deccan

🇮🇳 ✅ Within the deficient Deccan Trap province, the water that exists is found in exactly four settings, and ✅ two of them — **intertrappean beds sandwiched between impermeable strata**, and **secondary porosity developed in weathered *moorum*** — are this lesson's best Indian demonstration of the integrating sentence.

⚠️ ***Moorum*** is weathered basaltic material: the product of the hydrolysis and oxidation of Lesson 3 acting on the Deccan lava, at the weathering front of Lesson 4. ❗ **It is a weathering product that functions as an aquifer**, which is the integrating sentence stated at outcrop scale — in a province where the fresh rock holds nothing, **the water is precisely where the weathering reached**.

⚠️ **Intertrappean beds** are the complementary case and arise from a different history: **sedimentary layers deposited between successive lava flows**, later buried by further flows. ❗ Their hydrological significance is that they are **permeable layers confined above and below by impermeable basalt**, which is Lesson 15's confined geometry — so the Deccan contains confined aquifers of limited extent whose head is derived from recharge at their outcrop edges.

⚠️ **The pairing makes a general point.** ❗ Within one deficient province, water occurs in **two entirely different ways** — one created by **weathering after the rock formed**, the other inherited from **the sequence in which the rock formed** — and a well that misses both finds nothing. ❗ That is why the scale control matters so acutely here: **a province-level verdict of "deficient" is compatible with locally excellent yields and with total failure three hundred metres away.**

❗ **Limitations, stated with the hook, and there are four.** ⚠️ **One:** ❗ **no yield, depth, thickness or extent figure is available to this edition for intertrappean beds or for *moorum***, and **none is asserted**. ⚠️ **Two:** the province classification is from a **1971 scheme**, carries **no assessment year or methodology** in the source, and is quoted as **R.L. Singh's scheme with its date** — not as a current official hydrogeological map. ⚠️ **Three:** the 1,200-metre basalt thickness is that source's figure, likewise undated. ⚠️ **Four:** 🔁 **the stratigraphy of the Deccan flows and the origin of intertrappean sediments belong to the crust-and-rocks topic**; this lesson claims only their hydraulic consequence.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | ✅ Hard-rock province (1, 4) | ✅ Alluvial province (7) |
|---|---|---|
| Where the water is | **The weathered mantle plus fractures** — all secondary | **Intergranular pores of thick unconsolidated sediment** — primary |
| Storage | Small; ✅ generally limited to about **100 m** depth | **Very large**, through great thickness |
| Yield predictability | **Very low** — depends on intersecting structure | **High** — the formation is laterally continuous |
| Recharge | Local rainfall; constrained by mantle permeability | ✅ **Rainfall plus river seepage**, continuously |
| Response to over-pumping | Rapid local failure; **deepening does not help** | Progressive regional decline; deepening works **for a time** |
| ❗ Policy consequence | **Recharge structures must be matched to the mantle and the fracture system** | The resource can mask over-draft **for years** before failing |

**Strongest objection.** An eight-province scheme from 1971 is obsolete. Half a century of aquifer mapping has superseded it, and teaching it is teaching the history of Indian hydrogeology rather than its present.

**Reply.** The objection is right that it must be **dated**, and this lesson dates it in every reference. ⚠️ But it is wrong that the scheme is obsolete in what it asserts, for one structural reason: ❗ **the scheme is a lithological classification, and lithology has not changed since 1971.** The Deccan still has about that thickness of basalt; the Ganga plain still has great thicknesses of sorted alluvium; the Peninsular shield still has no primary porosity. ⚠️ What **has** changed, and changed enormously, is the **stage of extraction** — which is a statement about **use, not about rock**, and which is exactly why it belongs to Lesson 17 and not here. ❗ **The division is the point: this lesson teaches what the resource is, the next teaches what has been done to it**, and conflating the two is how candidates end up asserting that a province is "water-scarce" when they mean "over-extracted", which are different diagnoses with different remedies.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ The provinces are **qualitative classes with no stated methodology, no thresholds and no assessment year**, and no source available to this edition reconciles them with current hydrogeological mapping units. ⚠️ **A verbal verdict such as "deficient" or "good aquifers" cannot be converted into a yield**, and this edition does not attempt the conversion. ❗ An answer should therefore use the scheme for its **lithological reasoning**, attribute it to R.L. Singh with its 1971 date, and attach any quantitative claim to a **dated official assessment** instead.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ **foundation for two bridged demands.** The **2024 General Studies Paper I demand on the Gangetic valley's declining groundwater potential and its effect on food security** is 🔁 **owned by the agricultural-regions-and-food-security topic**; this lesson supplies the **physical identity of province 7**, and the core owner's reusable fifteen-mark spine for that demand is previewed at Lesson 17 and worked at Lesson 19 as a **declared bounded bridge**. The **2025 General Studies Paper III demand on seawater intrusion** is 🔁 **owned by the environment-and-ecology owner**; this lesson supplies the **saline-wedge hydraulics** only.
- **Probable framing:** as the paragraph that converts "India's groundwater" into a **differentiated geography** — the move that separates a strong answer from a generic one.
- **Traps:** treating India's groundwater as homogeneous; assuming sedimentary rock is always a good aquifer; calling the Deccan water-rich because it is vesicular; treating the Himalaya as a groundwater region rather than a spring region; using the 1971 scheme undated; converting a verbal verdict into a yield; trespassing into coastal management.
- **Answer use:** lead with the integrating sentence. *"In hard-rock India the near-surface weathered mantle is the groundwater reservoir and the route to the underlying fracture systems — so over half the country's area depends for its water on the fact that its rock has rotted, and on a saturated zone that generally extends to about a hundred metres."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ ❗ **THE INTEGRATING SENTENCE:** *"the near surface, weathered mantle forms the all important ground water reservoir, and the source for circulation of ground water through underlying fracture systems."*
- ⚠️ **The mantle stores; the fractures transmit.** A hard-rock well needs **both** — mantle thickness for storage, a fracture intersection for yield.
- ✅ **Deep weathered pediments, low-lying valleys and abandoned river channels** hold adequate thicknesses of porous material. ✅ **Saturated systems generally occur down to about 100 m.** ❗ Deepening does not reach a new resource in crystalline rock.
- ✅ **Friable semi-consolidated sandstones** give moderate yields with **auto-flowing zones not uncommon** — Lesson 15's confined concept, in India.
- ✅ **P1 Pre-Cambrian Crystalline — DEFICIENT.** Over **half the country's geographical area**: Tamil Nadu, AP, Telangana, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Dandakaranya, Bundelkhand, **Aravalli**. ⚠️ No primary porosity.
- ✅ **P2 Pre-Cambrian Sedimentary — not much suitable, inadequate.** **Cuddapah and Vindhyan** basins. ⚠️ Old, compacted, cemented · **P3 Gondwana Sedimentary — GOOD AQUIFERS.** **Barakar and Godavari** basins. ⚠️ Younger, less cemented.
- ✅ **P4 Deccan Trap — DEFICIENT.** **~1,200 m of impermeable basalt.** Water only in **fractures · weathered *moorum* · intertrappean beds · vesicles and amygdales** — ⚠️ all **secondary**.
- ✅ **P5 Cenozoic Sedimentary — GOOD AQUIFERS.** **AP, Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Gujarat coasts; tertiary sandstones** · **P6 Cenozoic Fault Basin — GOOD RESOURCE.** **Narmada, Purna, Tapi rift zone; 80-160 m** of alluvial sand, silt and clay. ⚠️ Layering → **confined** systems.
- ✅ **P7 Ganga-Brahmaputra Alluvial — THE RICHEST PROVINCE OF THE COUNTRY.** **Bhabar, tarai and axial belts**; streams **disappear into the bhabar** and **seep out in the tarai**; **water table high** · **P8 Himalayan — little significance; local springs common, wells a rare feature.** ❗ A **spring problem, not a well problem**.
- ✅ **Four controls on availability:** **climate (rainfall and temperature) · relief features · geological structure · the hydrological setup.** ⚠️ **Three of four are also weathering controls** — because the reservoir **is** the weathered mantle.
- ✅ **Coastal and deltaic tracts:** a narrow linear strip around the peninsular plateau; the **eastern coastal and deltaic tract and the estuarine areas of Gujarat** hold thick alluvium with **highly productive aquifers**, ✅ but **salinity hazards impose quality constraints on development**.
- ✅ **Saline-wedge sequence:** **over-pumping or lower recharge → freshwater head falls → the saline interface moves landward and upward → wells and soils become saline.** ⚠️ Small head loss, large interface movement; **slow to reverse**.
- 🔁 ❗ **The 2025 coastal-intrusion demand is owned by the environment owner.** Teach the hydraulics; route the management.
- 🇮🇳 **Deccan *moorum* and intertrappean beds:** ⚠️ *moorum* is a **weathering product functioning as an aquifer**; intertrappean beds are **permeable layers confined by basalt** → a confined geometry. ❗ **No yield, thickness or extent figure asserted.**
- ❗ **Date the scheme: R.L. Singh, 1971.** ⚠️ Lithology has not changed; **stage of extraction has** — which is Lesson 17, not this lesson. ❗ Do not convert a verbal verdict into a yield.

### Concept check

**Question:** A state in the Pre-Cambrian crystalline province responds to falling well yields by subsidising deeper boreholes. A state in the Ganga-Brahmaputra alluvial province adopts the same policy. Assess the policy in each province, using the physical character of each.

**Model answer:** The same policy has entirely different consequences, because the two provinces store water in physically different ways. **In the crystalline province** the policy is close to futile. Crystalline rock has no primary porosity, so the resource consists wholly of **secondary** voids: the near-surface weathered mantle, which stores, and the underlying fracture systems, which transmit and which the mantle feeds. The source records that potentially saturated systems generally occur **down to about a hundred metres**; below the weathered and fractured zone the rock is fresh and holds nothing. Deeper drilling therefore does not reach a new resource — it penetrates rock that was never a reservoir — and whatever additional yield it obtains comes from intersecting another fracture, which is a matter of chance rather than of depth. The correct response in this province is to enlarge the **storage** term by recharging the mantle and the fracture system, which is why recharge structures must be matched to the local weathered profile. **In the alluvial province** the policy will work, and that is the danger. The province is the richest in the country, with great thicknesses of unconsolidated, well-sorted permeable sediment, so deeper drilling genuinely reaches more saturated material and yields will recover. But the recovery is drawn from **storage rather than from recharge**, so the policy converts a visible yield problem into an invisible depletion problem and postpones the adjustment. It also raises two further risks that the crystalline case does not have: pumping from greater depth in a layered sequence can induce **downward leakage** and, in the coastal and deltaic parts of alluvial India, reducing the freshwater head allows the **saline interface to advance landward and upward**. The general conclusion is that a depth response is a response to a **yield** symptom, and that only in a province with thick storage does it even appear to succeed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat depth as a proxy for resource. In crystalline terrain there is a floor below which no aquifer exists, and in alluvial terrain apparent success at depth is consumption of storage, not discovery of a new supply.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "India does not have a groundwater problem; it has eight of them." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The eight-province scheme of R.L. Singh, 1971, shows that the resource is governed by lithology and differs in kind, not merely degree. The Pre-Cambrian crystalline province, covering over half the country's area, is deficient because the rock has no primary porosity; its water lies in the weathered mantle and in fractures, which is why the mantle is described as the all-important reservoir and the route to underlying fracture systems. The Deccan trap is deficient for a different reason: about 1,200 metres of impermeable basalt obstruct percolation, and water survives only in fractures, in weathered *moorum*, in intertrappean beds and in vesicles. The Ganga-Brahmaputra alluvium is the richest province, with bhabar, tarai and axial belts and a high water table. The Himalaya is a spring region rather than a well region. The qualification is that the scheme classifies rock, not use, so extraction stress is a separate and current question.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for three provinces correctly characterised with the physical reason for each verdict; 3 for the weathered-mantle reservoir statement used to explain the crystalline case; 2 for the Deccan's secondary-porosity settings named; 1 for the Himalayan spring-against-well distinction; 1 for dating the scheme and separating lithology from extraction stress. Deduct 2 for treating India's groundwater as homogeneous, and 2 for converting a verbal verdict into a yield figure.
