Two ladders and a chain. ❗ The two datasets are **not a before-and-after**; they are two differently constructed statements about different things, and the discipline of saying so is the single most transferable skill in this lesson.

### 1. The assessment lineage — why the numbers have a history

✅ Indian groundwater assessment has a documented lineage, and knowing it is what allows a candidate to date a figure instead of quoting it bare.

- ✅ **Dr. A.N. Khosla (1949)** estimated the **total average annual run-off of all Indian river systems at 167.4 million hectare metres**, using an empirical formula covering both surface and ground waters.
- ✅ The **National Commission on Agriculture (1976)** assessed **total ground water at 67 million hectare metres**, **usable resource at 35 million hectare metres**, of which **26 million hectare metres** was considered available for irrigation.
- ✅ **The first attempt on a scientific basis came in 1979**, when a High Level Committee — the **Ground Water Over Exploitation Committee** — was constituted by the **Agriculture Refinance and Development Corporation**.
- ✅ The norms of the **Ground Water Estimation Committee (1984)** are the ones the source records as **currently used by the Central Ground Water Board and the State Ground Water Departments**.
- ✅ The **Planning Commission** placed total water resources at **about 178 million hectare metres**, of which only a fraction is usable because of **physiography, topography, geology, dependability, quality and the state of technology**.

⚠️ **Three observations that make the lineage worth learning.** **One:** the figures are **not comparable with one another**, because each rests on a different definition and method — which is the historical demonstration of the owner's date-and-data control. **Two:** the units are **million hectare metres**, which is **not** the unit current reporting uses; ❗ **mixing million hectare metres with billion cubic metres is a reliable way to produce an impossible number**, and this file does not do it. **Three:** ❗ the statement that the **1984 norms are currently used** is **itself a book-era statement with no date of its own**, and is quoted as such rather than asserted as present practice.

### 2. Basin asymmetry — the structural fact that outlives the dataset

✅ **Table 15.4 Basin-wise Ground Water Resource Potential**, sourced in the book to ***India 1992: A Reference Annual*, p.419**, is reproduced for its **level of ground water development** column.

| ✅ Basin | ✅ Level of ground water development |
|---|---:|
| **Indus** | **79.29 %** |
| Madras composite and south Tamil Nadu | 45.94 % |
| Cauvery | 44.72 % |
| Ganga | 30.79 % |
| **All-India** | **27.82 %** |
| Brahmani-Baitarni | 5.16 % |
| Mahanadi | 4.32 % |
| Meghna | 3.21 % |
| **Brahmaputra** | **2.12 %** |

⚠️ ❗ **The table is decisive not for its totals but for its asymmetry.** **79.29 per cent in the Indus against 2.12 per cent in the Brahmaputra is a thirty-seven-fold range within one country**, and against an all-India figure of 27.82 per cent **both extremes are invisible**. ❗ That is the owner's scale control demonstrated in a single table: **a national value diagnoses neither basin.**

⚠️ **And the asymmetry is explicable**, which is what makes it an argument rather than a curiosity. The Indus basin states are the low-rainfall, surface-water-deficient, intensively irrigated states of section 4 below; the Brahmaputra basin has **abundant rainfall, abundant surface water and a rich alluvial aquifer** that there has been no economic reason to develop. ❗ **High development is not a measure of resource and low development is not a measure of scarcity** — the ratio measures **use against availability**, and both terms vary.

✅ **The reporting shift, recorded.** The source states that **CGWB has since stopped computing river-basin-wise data and now publishes state and union-territory data**, and gives its **2009** figures: total replenishable resource **above 433 billion cubic metres per year**; irrigation availability **221.42 billion cubic metres per year**; provision for other uses **21.89 billion cubic metres per year**; and a **level of ground water development of 61 per cent**.

❗ **Uncertainty disclosed.** ⚠️ The statement that basin-wise computation has stopped is **itself a book-era statement**, and this edition presents it as **the book's statement with its own vintage**, not as a confirmed description of current practice; no first-party confirmation of that procedural point was obtainable in this environment.

### 3. The current assessment, and the dating lesson

📰 ✅ **Re-verified at the time of writing:** the **Central Ground Water Board**, under the **Department of Water Resources, River Development and Ganga Rejuvenation, Ministry of Jal Shakti**, published the ***National Compilation on Dynamic Ground Water Resources of India, 2025***, reporting **total annual ground water recharge of 448.52 billion cubic metres**, an **annual extractable ground water resource of 407.75 billion cubic metres**, **total annual ground water extraction of 247.22 billion cubic metres**, and a **national stage of ground water extraction of 60.63 per cent**.

📰 ✅ Of **6,762 assessment units**, the same 2025 compilation categorises **73.14 per cent as Safe**, **11.21 per cent as Semi-critical**, **2.97 per cent as Critical**, **10.80 per cent — 730 units — as Over-exploited**, and **1.88 per cent as Saline**. 📰 The over-exploited share is reported to have **fallen from 17.2 per cent in 2017 to 10.8 per cent in 2025**, with over-exploited units **concentrated in the north-west (Punjab, Haryana, Delhi and western Uttar Pradesh), the west (Rajasthan and Gujarat) and the south (Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Telangana and Andhra Pradesh)**.

❗ **Access limitation, restated at the point of use.** ⚠️ The first-party Central Ground Water Board page for this compilation returned a transport-level failure in this environment and the ministry's findings document returned HTTP 403, so the **document body was not read directly**. Every figure above is **corroborated through reporting that cites first-party ministry releases**, is carried **with its issuing body, document title and assessment year**, and is **as reported at the time of writing**. ❗ **No figure that could not be corroborated at the time of writing appears here.**

⚠️ ❗ **Now the method lesson, which is the real reason this lesson carries both datasets.** The book's **2009 level of development of 61 per cent** and the **2025 stage of extraction of 60.63 per cent** look almost identical. ❗ **They are not the same statistic and must not be compared as a trend.** They differ in **agency-year**, in **methodology generation**, in the **number and definition of assessment units**, and in **definitional base** — the first is computed against a replenishable resource stated as above 433 billion cubic metres per year, the second against an annual extractable resource of 407.75 billion cubic metres. ❗ **Subtracting one from the other, or concluding that nothing changed in sixteen years, would be a methodological error of exactly the kind the owner's date-and-data control exists to prevent.**

❗ **State both, with both dates, and teach the gap.** ⚠️ That is also the correct answer-writing move: a candidate who writes *"the Central Ground Water Board reported a level of groundwater development of 61 per cent for 2009 and a stage of extraction of 60.63 per cent in its 2025 Dynamic Ground Water Resources compilation; the figures are not directly comparable because the methodology and the definitional base changed"* has demonstrated something an examiner can check and that most candidates cannot do.

⚠️ **One further note on the category trend.** 📰 The reported fall in over-exploited units from 17.2 per cent in 2017 to 10.8 per cent in 2025 is attributed in reporting to increased recharge and to management and recharge interventions. ❗ **This edition reports the figures and the attribution as reported**, and notes the obvious methodological caution: ⚠️ **a change in the number of assessment units, or in the method of categorising them, can move a category share without any change in the aquifers** — which is the same caution this lesson applies to the 61-against-60.63 comparison, applied consistently.

⚠️ **Prelims precision target (answer-neutral).** The 2023 Prelims demand concerns **India's share of global groundwater withdrawal according to a United Nations report**. ❗ The distinctions a candidate must hold, with **no answer recorded, implied or inferred anywhere in this file**: any such share is **agency-specific, year-specific and methodology-specific**; a **withdrawal** share is not an **extraction-stage** figure and not a **depletion** figure; and India's position in such rankings follows from the **scale of irrigated agriculture**, not from scarcity. ❗ The lesson teaches the dating discipline the question tests and stops there. ❗ **The official key for that year is unavailable locally and nothing is inferred.**

### 4. The core India-groundwater argument

✅ The source's own passage is the best available statement of the argument and is quoted at length because its structure is the answer's structure:

> ✅ *"Entirely different picture emerges when we look at the stage of ground water development. Haryana, Punjab and Rajasthan receive less than 40 cm annual rainfall and are deficient in surface water resources. As such, these states exploit more than 100 per cent of the available ground water for irrigation. Large scale exploitation of ground water is done with the help of tube wells. The demand for ground water for irrigation started increasing in Punjab and Haryana with the advent of Green Revolution in 1960s. The change in cropping pattern has further increased demand for ground water to irrigate the fields. The region is climatically more suited to wheat but farmers' preference for rice crop has changed the entire scenario. This has led to over exploitation of ground water resources and the ground water level is falling rather alarmingly. In fact these three states draw more water, especially for irrigation, than the amount of water replenished in natural course. If this trend of over exploitation of ground water continues unabated, these states will be left with no ground water. Experts have expressed apprehension that **granary of today will be barren land tomorrow**."*

✅ The supporting distribution: **Uttar Pradesh has the largest irrigation groundwater resource at 46.00 billion cubic metres per year**; **Uttar Pradesh and Bihar have developed over 72 per cent of their resource and Gujarat over 75 per cent**; and **the total replenishable resource ranges from 75.25 billion cubic metres per year in Uttar Pradesh to 0.044 billion cubic metres per year in Mizoram.**

⚠️ **Four analytical observations that an answer should add to the quotation.**

- ⚠️ **The argument is not about scarcity; it is about substitution.** These states are **deficient in surface water**, so groundwater is not a supplement but the **primary** irrigation source. Depletion is therefore not a failure of conservation but the **predictable arithmetic of substituting a slow-recharging stock for an absent flow**.
- ⚠️ **The cropping clause is the causal core, and it is a climatic mismatch.** ✅ The region is **climatically more suited to wheat**, and **farmers' preference for rice has changed the entire scenario**. ❗ Rice in a sub-40-centimetre rainfall belt is **irrigation-dependent by construction**, so the crop choice fixes the water demand before any efficiency measure is considered. 🔁 **Why farmers prefer rice — procurement, assured prices, energy pricing and the policy architecture behind all three — is owned by the economy owner**, and is named and routed here, not explained.
- ⚠️ **"More than 100 per cent" is an accounting statement, and it is Lesson 15's budget.** Extraction exceeding the annual replenishable resource means **disposal item (iv) has grown until total disposal exceeds total supply for that unit and period** — the deficit being met from **storage**, which is finite.
- ❗ **The warning's form matters.** *"Granary of today will be barren land tomorrow"* is **a conditional, not a prediction**: the source states it as what follows *"if this trend ... continues unabated"*. ⚠️ Quoting it as a forecast overstates it, and the examiner-facing version keeps the condition attached.

⚠️ **The counterpoint that earns the balance mark, and the owner supplies it.** ✅ *"groundwater irrigation is also what made the region's productivity and drought resilience possible. The answer must not read as an argument against tube wells; the failure is of governance of the commons, energy pricing and crop choice, not of irrigation itself."* ❗ **Any answer on this subject that omits the counterpoint has written a one-sided case**, and the owner says so explicitly.

⚠️ **The bounded bridge, named in the text as the owner requires.** ✅ The core owner's section 10.2 holds a **reusable seven-step fifteen-mark spine on groundwater decline and food security**, and the repository record states plainly that this spine lives in this topic's owner as a **deliberate bounded bridge** to the agricultural-regions-and-food-security owner, which owns the **2024 General Studies Paper I demand**. ❗ **The bridge is named here, previewed in its logic, and worked in full at Lesson 19.** ⚠️ Its shape: thesis, physical system, imbalance, transmission to food security, the counterpoint, response logic matched to mechanism, and a graded conclusion. ❗ **Topic 04 holds the mechanism and the spine; the regional-agriculture framing is 🔁 routed.**

### 5. Quality — the half that quantity conceals

✅ Groundwater quality is governed by **waste disposal and land use**. ✅ Water-soluble substances dumped, spilled, spread or stored on the land surface **eventually infiltrate**; ✅ **disposal of fluids through wells and sinkholes injects contaminants directly into aquifers**; ✅ once polluted, groundwater may remain **unusable or hazardous for decades or even centuries**, and ✅ **the nature and source of the pollutant are often difficult to identify**.

✅ And the framing that makes quality a structural rather than an incidental problem: in a monsoon country where rainfall is **seasonal, erratic, undependable and variable**, and where surface water is always in short supply, **most drought-prone areas depend largely on ground water**.

⚠️ **Three consequences worth stating in an answer.** **One:** ❗ **quality failure is effectively irreversible on a policy timescale** — decades to centuries — which makes it categorically different from a quantity problem that recharge can reverse. **Two:** ⚠️ **direct injection through wells and sinkholes bypasses the soil**, which is the system's principal natural filter, so the same structures that recharge an aquifer can contaminate it — ❗ a warning that bears directly on Lesson 18's recharge designs and is the reason their **desilting chambers, graded filters and first-flush diversion** exist. **Three:** ⚠️ the drought-dependence clause means that **quality failure falls hardest exactly where there is no alternative source**.

✅ ⚠️ **And the quality half is also where the advanced owner's over-extraction and contamination rows meet**: ✅ the owner records that **groundwater problems in India are not only about quantity** — ❌ treating them as such is its recorded trap — because **both quantity (over-extraction) and quality (aquifer contamination) are critical concerns.**

🔁 ❗ **Boundaries, stated.** ⚠️ **The 2025 General Studies Paper III demand on the causes of groundwater depletion and the steps the government has taken is owned by the economy owner.** ❗ **This lesson teaches *why* the resource depletes; it does not teach *what the state does about it*.** ⚠️ Likewise, **industrial effluent chemistry, fertiliser-consumption series, river pollution loads and agricultural nutrient economics are owned by the environment and agriculture owners** and are 🔁 routed; only the **contamination mechanism** above is retained.

### 6. India hook — Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan: "granary of today, barren land tomorrow"

🇮🇳 ✅ The three north-western states are this file's terminal case, and the whole file converges on them.

⚠️ **Assemble what every earlier lesson has contributed.** ⚠️ **Lesson 7** gave the soils — alluvial, with **usar** affecting about **1.21 million hectares in Punjab**. ⚠️ **Lesson 9** gave the canal-seepage waterlogging, the capillary salinisation and the **tubewell vertical drainage that reclaimed the waterlogged saline land by lowering the water table**. ⚠️ **Lesson 8** gave the cascade — erosion lowers the ground water level and reduces soil moisture — the supply-side half of the deficit this lesson measures. ⚠️ **Lesson 16** gave the aquifer — ✅ the **Ganga-Brahmaputra alluvial province, the richest in the country**. ⚠️ **Lesson 15** gave the budget. ✅ And this lesson gives the outcome: **less than 40 centimetres of annual rainfall, deficiency in surface water, extraction of more than 100 per cent of the available groundwater for irrigation, a cropping pattern shifted from climatically suitable wheat to water-intensive rice, and a water level falling "rather alarmingly"**.

📰 ✅ **And the current assessment places them precisely:** the Central Ground Water Board's 2025 compilation reports over-exploited assessment units **concentrated in the north-west — Punjab, Haryana, Delhi and western Uttar Pradesh** — among other clusters, which corroborates at the assessment-unit scale what the book argues at the state scale.

❗ **The structural irony, stated once and carried into the answer.** ⚠️ **The richest aquifer in India is the one being drawn down fastest.** That is not a paradox; it is the **predictable consequence of a thick, reliable, easily accessible resource meeting a cropping pattern that is not climatically matched to its rainfall**. ❗ **Abundance permitted the mismatch; it did not cause it.** And the same abundance is what allowed the depletion to proceed for decades before it became visible — because a province with great storage **masks over-draft for years**, exactly as Lesson 16's comparison predicted.

❗ **Limitations, stated with the hook, and there are four.** ⚠️ **One:** the **"more than 100 per cent" claim and the sub-40-centimetre rainfall figure are the book's**, undated, and are stated as that source's characterisation; the current assessment is stated separately with its own agency and year, and **the two are not merged**. ⚠️ **Two:** ❗ **the national stage of extraction of 60.63 per cent does not diagnose Punjab**, and the owner's scale control forbids using it that way; the assessment-unit categorisation does the diagnostic work. ⚠️ **Three:** 🔁 **the policy half — procurement, energy pricing, crop incentives, regulation and the institutional response — is owned by the economy owner** and is routed by name. ⚠️ **Four:** the **counterpoint is not optional**: groundwater irrigation produced the region's productivity and drought resilience, and an answer that omits this has mis-stated the problem as one of irrigation rather than of governance, pricing and crop choice.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | ✅ Book-era basin data | 📰 Current national assessment |
|---|---|---|
| Issuing body | Compiled in the book from ***India 1992: A Reference Annual*, p.419** | 📰 **Central Ground Water Board, Ministry of Jal Shakti** |
| Assessment year | **1992-era** | 📰 **2025** |
| Unit | **River basin** | 📰 **Assessment unit — 6,762 of them** |
| Measure | **Level of ground water development**, per basin | 📰 **Stage of extraction**, plus a **five-class categorisation** |
| Units of volume | ⚠️ The lineage works in **million hectare metres** | 📰 **Billion cubic metres** |
| What it shows best | **Structural asymmetry** — a 37-fold range | 📰 **Where the stress is concentrated**, at a usable administrative scale |
| ❗ Comparability | — | ❗ **Not comparable. Quote both with both dates; do not subtract.** |

**Strongest objection.** Carrying a 1992-era table alongside a 2025 assessment is clutter. The old figures are superseded and quoting them risks a candidate using an obsolete number in an answer.

**Reply.** The old table is retained for something the new assessment does not provide, and the retention is deliberate. ⚠️ **The 2025 compilation reports at assessment-unit and state level; the 1992-era table reports by river basin**, and the **basin is the hydrological unit** in which the inflow and outflow terms of Lesson 15's budget are closed. ❗ The thirty-seven-fold Indus-against-Brahmaputra range is a statement about **the hydrological geography of India** that no administrative-unit dataset makes as cleanly. ⚠️ More importantly, **the pair is the teaching instrument**: the near-identity of 61 per cent in 2009 and 60.63 per cent in 2025 is the most instructive trap available in this topic, because it looks like a trend and is not one. ❗ A candidate who has been shown that two similar-looking percentages are incommensurable has learned the owner's date-and-data control in a way no abstract statement of it could teach.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ **The 2025 figures could not be read first-party in this environment**, and the file says so at every point of use. ⚠️ Three further uncertainties are carried openly: the **procedural claim that basin-wise computation ceased** is book-era and unconfirmed; the **reported fall in the over-exploited share** could in principle reflect methodological or unit-count change as well as physical improvement, and this edition does not adjudicate; and ❗ **no source available to this edition states the methodology generation behind either the 2009 or the 2025 figure**, which is precisely why they are not compared.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I); **"Disaster and disaster management" as support only** (General Studies Paper III).
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ **mechanism for three bridged Mains demands, all 🔁 routed.** The **2024 General Studies Paper I demand on Gangetic groundwater decline and food security** is owned by the agricultural-regions-and-food-security owner, with the fifteen-mark spine held here as a **declared bounded bridge**. The **2025 General Studies Paper III demand on seawater intrusion** is owned by the environment owner. The **2025 General Studies Paper III demand on depleting groundwater and government steps** is owned by the economy owner — ❗ this lesson teaches **why**, never **what the state does**. ⚠️ Also the **concept anchor for the 2023 Prelims demand on India's share of global groundwater withdrawal**; ❗ answer-neutral, key unavailable locally, nothing inferred.
- **Probable framing:** as the evidence-and-causation core of any groundwater answer, and as the place where a candidate demonstrates data discipline.
- **Traps:** quoting a percentage without agency and year; comparing the 2009 and 2025 figures as a trend; using the national stage of extraction to diagnose a state; omitting the counterpoint; treating the "granary" line as a prediction rather than a conditional; mixing million hectare metres with billion cubic metres; explaining why farmers prefer rice; drifting into government schemes.
- **Answer use:** *"Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan receive under forty centimetres of rainfall and are deficient in surface water, so groundwater is the primary irrigation source rather than a supplement; a cropping shift from climatically suited wheat to rice fixed demand above replenishment, and the deficit is met from storage. The Central Ground Water Board's 2025 Dynamic Ground Water Resources compilation places over-exploited assessment units in exactly this north-western cluster."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Lineage:** **Khosla 1949** — total average annual run-off **167.4 m ha m**. **National Commission on Agriculture 1976** — total groundwater **67 m ha m**, usable **35**, irrigation **26**. **1979** — first scientific attempt, the **Ground Water Over Exploitation Committee** under the **Agriculture Refinance and Development Corporation**. **Ground Water Estimation Committee 1984** — norms recorded as those currently used by CGWB and state departments. **Planning Commission — about 178 m ha m** total water resources.
- ❗ The lineage's figures are **not mutually comparable**; units are **million hectare metres**, ❗ **not** billion cubic metres.
- ✅ **Table 15.4 (source given as *India 1992: A Reference Annual*, p.419) — level of ground water development:** **Indus 79.29 %** · Madras composite + south Tamil Nadu 45.94 % · Cauvery 44.72 % · Ganga 30.79 % · **all-India 27.82 %** · Brahmani-Baitarni 5.16 % · Mahanadi 4.32 % · Meghna 3.21 % · **Brahmaputra 2.12 %**.
- ❗ ⚠️ **The asymmetry is the argument: a 37-fold range, with both extremes invisible in the national figure.** High development ≠ resource; low development ≠ scarcity.
- ✅ **The book records that CGWB stopped basin-wise computation and moved to state and UT data**, giving **2009**: replenishable **> 433 BCM/yr**, irrigation **221.42 BCM/yr**, other uses **21.89 BCM/yr**, **level of development 61 %**. ❗ The procedural claim is **book-era and unconfirmed**.
- 📰 ✅ **CGWB, Ministry of Jal Shakti, *National Compilation on Dynamic Ground Water Resources of India, 2025*:** recharge **448.52 BCM** · extractable **407.75 BCM** · extraction **247.22 BCM** · **stage of extraction 60.63 %**. Of **6,762 assessment units**: **Safe 73.14 %** · **Semi-critical 11.21 %** · **Critical 2.97 %** · **Over-exploited 10.80 % (730 units)** · **Saline 1.88 %**. Over-exploited share reported down from **17.2 % (2017)** to **10.8 % (2025)**; concentrated in the **north-west, west and south**.
- ❗ **61 % (2009) and 60.63 % (2025) are NOT the same statistic.** Different agency-year, methodology generation, unit count and definitional base. ❗ **Quote both with both dates; never subtract.**
- ✅ **The argument:** Haryana, Punjab and Rajasthan get **under 40 cm** rain, are **deficient in surface water**, and **exploit more than 100 per cent** of available groundwater for irrigation via **tube wells**; demand rose with the **Green Revolution in the 1960s**; ✅ **the region is climatically more suited to wheat but farmers' preference for rice changed the entire scenario**; levels fall *"rather alarmingly"*; ✅ **"granary of today will be barren land tomorrow"** — ❗ **a conditional, not a prediction**.
- ✅ **Distribution:** **UP largest irrigation resource at 46.00 BCM/yr**; **UP and Bihar over 72 %** developed, **Gujarat over 75 %**; replenishable resource ranges **75.25 BCM/yr (UP)** to **0.044 BCM/yr (Mizoram)**.
- ✅ ❗ **The counterpoint is mandatory:** groundwater irrigation made the region's **productivity and drought resilience** possible; the failure is of **governance of the commons, energy pricing and crop choice**, not of irrigation.
- ✅ ❗ **Declared bounded bridge:** the owner's **seven-step fifteen-mark spine** on groundwater decline and food security lives here and is routed to the food-security owner. **Worked in full at Lesson 19.**
- ✅ **Quality:** governed by **waste disposal and land use**; surface substances **eventually infiltrate**; ✅ **disposal through wells and sinkholes injects contaminants directly**; ✅ pollution may persist **decades or even centuries**; the pollutant is **often difficult to identify**. ✅ **Most drought-prone areas depend largely on groundwater.**
- ❗ Quality failure is **effectively irreversible on a policy timescale**, and direct injection **bypasses the soil filter** — the reason Lesson 18's designs carry **desilting chambers, graded filters and first-flush diversion**.
- ❌ ✅ **Owner's trap:** groundwater problems are **not only about quantity** — **both quantity and quality** are critical.
- 🔁 ❗ **Routed:** the 2025 depletion-and-government-steps demand (economy owner); coastal intrusion response (environment owner); why farmers prefer rice (economy owner); effluent chemistry and nutrient economics (environment and agriculture owners).

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India's stage of groundwater extraction was 61 per cent in 2009 and 60.63 per cent in 2025, so the situation has been stable for sixteen years and the crisis is overstated." Identify every error in the inference and state what the two figures can legitimately be used for.

**Model answer:** The inference contains three errors, and together they invert the evidence. **The comparability error.** The two figures are not the same statistic. The 2009 figure is a **level of ground water development** reported against a total replenishable resource stated as above 433 billion cubic metres per year; the 2025 figure is a **stage of extraction** reported by the Central Ground Water Board in its *National Compilation on Dynamic Ground Water Resources of India, 2025* against an annual extractable resource of 407.75 billion cubic metres. They differ in agency-year, in methodology generation, in the number and definition of assessment units, and in definitional base. Two percentages computed on different denominators by different methods cannot be differenced, and their near-identity is a coincidence of construction rather than evidence of stability. **The aggregation error.** A national figure averages over a country in which basin-level development has ranged from about 79 per cent in the Indus to about 2 per cent in the Brahmaputra, and in which the 2025 compilation classifies 730 of 6,762 assessment units as over-exploited, concentrated in the north-west, west and south. A national average near 60 per cent is therefore consistent with severe and worsening local depletion, and the controlling rule is explicit that national values cannot diagnose one aquifer or one district. **The category error.** Stage of extraction is a **ratio of use to availability**, not a measure of aquifer health. A falling water table, rising pumping depth, declining well yields, quality deterioration and seawater intrusion can all worsen while the ratio stays flat — particularly if the denominator itself is re-estimated. **What the figures can legitimately be used for:** each, quoted **with its issuing body, document title and assessment year**, states the national ratio of extraction to availability as assessed on that occasion; and the pair, presented side by side with both dates and an explicit note that they are not comparable, is an excellent demonstration of why groundwater statistics require agency, year, methodology and scale before they mean anything.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat two similar-looking percentages as a time series. In groundwater reporting the denominator, the method and the assessment unit all change between compilations, and apparent stability is frequently an artefact of re-estimation rather than a finding about water.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The depletion of groundwater in north-western India is a cropping decision expressed hydrologically." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim captures the causal core but overstates a single term. The hydrology is not in doubt: Haryana, Punjab and Rajasthan receive under forty centimetres of annual rainfall and are deficient in surface water, so groundwater is the primary irrigation source, and these states are recorded as exploiting more than a hundred per cent of the available resource through tube wells. The cropping clause is decisive — the region is climatically suited to wheat, and the shift to rice fixed demand above replenishment before any efficiency measure could act, which is why the Central Ground Water Board's 2025 compilation still places over-exploited units in this cluster. But the claim understates three things. Groundwater irrigation created the region's productivity and drought resilience. The crop choice responds to procurement and energy incentives owned elsewhere. And the deficit is met from storage, so the constraint is cumulative rather than annual.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the rainfall-and-surface-water-deficiency premise stated as the reason groundwater is primary; 3 for the wheat-to-rice cropping shift identified as the causal core; 2 for the counterpoint that groundwater irrigation produced the region's productivity and that the failure is one of governance and pricing; 1 for one dated official figure quoted with its issuing body and year; 1 for routing the policy explanation rather than supplying it. Deduct 3 for omitting the counterpoint, and 2 for using a national percentage to diagnose a state.

                         designed to be LIFTED AND CLEANED. An
                         unmaintained structure stops recharging

                         re-extracted faster than it arrives

                         way to know whether it worked

                         created by a CROPPING PATTERN (L17)
   ===================================================================

Three buildable designs, one derivation, and a definition of "effective". ❗ The 2018 demand's whole weight sits on that last word, and the five conditions above are what separate an answer that lists techniques from one that answers the question asked.

### 1. The definitions, stated exactly

✅ **Rainwater harvesting** is the **collection and storage of rain water at the surface or in a sub-surface aquifer before it is lost as surface run-off.**

✅ **Artificial recharge** is **a process by which the ground water reservoir is augmented at a rate exceeding that under natural conditions of replenishment.**

⚠️ **Three observations that make the pair precise rather than interchangeable.** **One:** harvesting is defined by **capture before loss**; recharge is defined by **rate exceeding the natural**. ⚠️ A structure may harvest without recharging — a storage tank — and recharge without harvesting rain, as when canal water is used. **Two:** ❗ **artificial recharge is supply item (iv) of Lesson 15's budget**, named there as *"recharge from irrigation, reservoirs and other schemes especially designed for artificial recharge"*. It is therefore **not an addition to the hydrological system but an enlargement of a term already in it** — which is why it is the structurally correct response to a disposal-side excess. **Three:** the phrase *"at a rate exceeding that under natural conditions"* carries the measurement obligation: ❗ **a claim that a structure recharges requires a before-and-after, which is why monitoring belongs in the definition and not in the afterthoughts.**

### 2. The need — seven items, with the urban one that carries the demand

✅ Khullar's seven-item need list: **overcome the inadequacy of surface water**; **arrest the decline in ground water levels**; **enhance availability of ground water at a specific place and time**; ❗ **increase infiltration of rain water into the subsoil, which has decreased drastically in urban areas because open ground has been paved**; **improve ground water quality by dilution**; **increase agricultural production**; and **improve the ecology of the area through increased vegetation cover.**

⚠️ **The fourth item is the one the 2018 demand turns on, and it deserves unpacking.** ❗ Urbanisation changes the **partition** between the two limbs of the hydrological cycle that Lesson 14 identified. Paving converts a surface on which a substantial share of rainfall infiltrated into one on which almost none does, so the **runoff limb grows and the recharge limb shrinks** — simultaneously producing urban flooding and urban groundwater decline **from the same cause**. ❗ That is why urban water harvesting is not a water-supply measure bolted onto a city: it is the **restoration of a partition that construction removed**, and saying so is the strongest opening a candidate can give the 2018 answer.

⚠️ **The third item is the other high-value one.** *"Enhance availability at a specific place and time"* is a statement about **distribution**, not about volume: India's problem is not only how much water there is but **where it is and when**, and an aquifer is a **distribution system as well as a store**, which is the fifth of the advantages below.

### 3. The advantages — eleven items, organised

✅ The source's advantage list: **recharge to a sub-surface reservoir costs less than a surface reservoir**; **the aquifer itself serves as the distribution system**; **no land is wasted for storage and no population is displaced**; **ground water is not directly exposed to evaporation and pollution**; **storing water underground is environment friendly**; **the productivity of the aquifer increases**; **flood hazards are reduced**; **ground water levels rise**; **drought effects are mitigated**; and **soil erosion is reduced.**

⚠️ **Group them, because grouped they become an argument rather than a list:**

| ⚠️ Group | ✅ Items | ⚠️ Why the group matters in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| **Cost and land** | Costs less than a surface reservoir · **no land wasted** · **no population displaced** | ❗ The three objections that defeat large surface storage **do not apply** — this is the comparison that wins marks |
| **Physical performance** | **The aquifer is the distribution system** · not exposed to evaporation or pollution · aquifer productivity rises · levels rise | An aquifer **stores and conveys**, so no canal network is needed |
| **Hazard reduction** | **Flood hazards reduced** · drought effects mitigated · **soil erosion reduced** | ❗ The same structure addresses **both tails** of the rainfall distribution — and the erosion item closes Lesson 9's coupling |
| **Environmental** | Storing water underground is environment friendly | The weakest item; ⚠️ state it as the source's characterisation |

❗ **The second group's first item is the single strongest line available.** ⚠️ *"The aquifer itself serves as the distribution system"* means that recharge **requires no conveyance infrastructure at all** — water enters where it falls and is drawn where it is needed, with the formation doing the transport. ❗ That is a structural advantage no surface scheme can match, and it is the reason the cost comparison below is so lopsided.

⚠️ **A bounded cost comparator, and only this.** ✅ A cost comparison is recorded in the source, attributed to **Jayanta Bandhopadhyay of the Centre for Development and Environment Policy, Indian Institute of Management Kolkata**, citing the Central Ground Water Board: **37 billion cubic metres of groundwater can be recharged locally at only about Rs 24,500 crore**, against **Rs 5,60,000 crore to obtain a mere 175 billion cubic metres from river interlinking**. ❗ **It is used here as a recharge cost-effectiveness comparator and for nothing else.** 🔁 **River interlinking — the National Perspective Plan, the development agency, the named links and the debate about them — is owned by the water-resources owner and is not expounded, assessed or described in this file.** ⚠️ The figures carry no assessment year on the page and are quoted as that attributed estimate.

❗ **And one claim that is deliberately not quantified.** ⚠️ The same page records an estimate attributed to the **Centre for Science and Environment, New Delhi**, that capturing rain water and run-off on **2 per cent of India's land area** could supply a stated number of **gallons of water per person**. ❗ **The period over which that volume would be supplied is not stated on the page** — per day, per year or per lifetime is undetermined — and **a volume per person without a period is not a quantity**. ❗ **This edition therefore does not quantify the claim.** ⚠️ It is noted only as a widely cited order-of-magnitude argument that **a small fraction of India's land area, used for harvesting, would yield a very large volume**, and a candidate should state it in exactly that qualitative form or omit it.

### 4. Potential areas, and the technique split

✅ **Four potential-area criteria:** **declining ground water levels**; **substantially de-saturated aquifers**; **inadequate availability in lean months**; and **urbanisation-driven loss of infiltration and recharge.**

⚠️ The criteria are a **diagnosis before a prescription**, and that ordering is itself the answer-writing discipline. ❗ Note that the second criterion — a **de-saturated aquifer** — is a statement about **space available to receive water**, which is the physical precondition the enthusiasm for harvesting usually skips: **a saturated aquifer cannot be recharged**, and an impermeable formation will not accept water however much is directed at it.

✅ **Urban techniques:** **recharge pit · recharge trench · tubewell · recharge well.**

✅ **Rural techniques:** **gully plug · contour bund · gabion structure · percolation tank · check dam, cement plug or nala bund · recharge shaft · dugwell recharge · ground water dams and subsurface dykes.**

⚠️ **Three observations on the split.** **One:** ❗ **the rural list is Lesson 9's conservation practice set under a different heading.** **Gully plugs and contour bunds are soil-conservation structures**, and ✅ Lesson 9 recorded that contour bunding *"also increased the water recharging capacity of wells in the adjoining areas"*. ❗ **The same structure is a conservation measure when the objective is soil and a recharge measure when the objective is water — which is the third structural coupling of this file, now closed.** **Two:** the **subsurface dyke** is the engineered version of Lesson 15's **dyke spring**: an impermeable barrier placed across the flow of groundwater to hold it upstream. ❗ **The technique copies a natural form**, which is worth a sentence. **Three:** the urban list is **small-catchment and point-source** — a roof, a courtyard — while the rural list is **catchment-scale**, and that difference follows directly from where the water lands and who controls the surface.

### 5. The three designs, and what their details are for

✅ **Fig. 15.7, recharge pit** — suitable for buildings with a **roof area of about 100 square metres** in **alluvial areas where permeable rock is exposed at or near the surface**; **1 to 2 metres wide and 2 to 3 metres deep**; **back-filled in graded form with boulders of 5 to 20 centimetres at the bottom, gravels of 5 to 10 millimetres in between and coarse sand of 1.5 to 2 millimetres at the top**, ✅ **so that silt arriving with runoff is deposited on top of the sand and can be removed**; with a **roof mesh to exclude leaves** and a **desilting chamber at ground level**.

✅ **Fig. 15.8, recharge trench** — suitable for **roof areas of 200 to 300 square metres where permeable strata occur at shallow depth**; **0.5 to 1 metre wide, 1 to 1.5 metres deep and 10 to 20 metres long**, with fill proportions of **15 per cent coarse sand, 20 per cent 20-millimetre gravel and 50 per cent 40-millimetre boulder**.

✅ **Fig. 15.9, recharge through existing tubewells** — for **areas where shallow aquifers have dried up and tubewells tap deeper aquifers**; **10-centimetre-diameter PVC roof drains**; **first-flush diversion through the bottom of the drain pipe**; then an **inline PVC filter 1 to 1.2 metres long, 15 centimetres in diameter for roofs under 150 square metres and 20 centimetres for larger roofs**, fitted with a **6.25-centimetre reducer at each end** and **divided into three chambers by PVC screens so that filter materials do not mix.**

⚠️ **Why the details are retained when most figures in this file are not.** ❗ **Each specification encodes a design principle, and the principles are the answer.**

- ⚠️ **The graded fill is a maintenance design, not a filter design.** ✅ The sand sits on top **precisely so that silt is deposited where it can be removed**. ❗ A structure whose silt load settles at the bottom cannot be cleaned and will fail permanently; this one can be, and that is the difference between a structure that works for one monsoon and one that works for twenty.
- ⚠️ **The roof-area thresholds are a matching rule.** ❗ A pit is sized for about 100 square metres, a trench for 200 to 300. **Structure capacity must match catchment yield**, or the structure overflows and the harvest is lost.
- ⚠️ **The strata condition is the aquifer-appropriateness rule.** ❗ A pit requires **permeable rock at or near the surface**; a trench requires **permeable strata at shallow depth**; tubewell recharge is explicitly for where the **shallow aquifer has dried up**. ⚠️ On a clay-rich surface — Lesson 14's high-porosity, low-permeability case — **none of the three will accept water**, and no amount of construction changes that.
- ⚠️ **First-flush diversion and the three-chamber filter are contamination controls.** ❗ Lesson 17 recorded that **disposal of fluids through wells injects contaminants directly into aquifers** and that pollution may persist for **decades or even centuries**. ❗ **A recharge structure that bypasses the soil without a filter is a contamination structure**, and the first flush — which carries the accumulated dust, droppings and debris of the dry season — is exactly the water that must not be injected.

### 6. The harvest table and its derivable coefficient

✅ **Table 15.10, "Availability of Rain Water through Roof Top Rain Water Harvesting"**, is sourced to the **Central Ground Water Board, Ministry of Water Resources, *Rainwater Harvesting Techniques to Augment Ground Water*, 2003**. ✅ It tabulates **rainfall from 20 to 3,000 millimetres against roof areas from 100 to 2,000 square metres**, giving **harvested volume in cubic metres**.

⚠️ **The derivation, shown rather than asserted.** Gross volume is rainfall depth multiplied by roof area. For **100 millimetres over 100 square metres**: 0.1 metre × 100 square metres = **10 cubic metres gross**. ✅ **The table gives 8.** ⚠️ The implied ratio is **8/10 = about 0.8**.

❗ **This coefficient is tagged as an inference, and the tag is not a formality.** ⚠️ **The table does not state a runoff coefficient anywhere.** The value of about 0.8 is **recovered from the table's own arithmetic by this edition**, and it is reported with the derivation shown so that a reader can check it. ❗ **It must never be quoted as a published Central Ground Water Board coefficient**, and an answer using it should say *"a runoff coefficient of about 0.8 is implied by the table's own arithmetic"* rather than *"the Central Ground Water Board uses 0.8"*.

⚠️ **What the coefficient physically represents** is the share of rainfall **not** captured: water absorbed in wetting the roof surface, evaporated, discarded by first-flush diversion, and lost at gutter joints and overflows. ❗ About a fifth, which is a useful order for an answer and a reminder that **harvest is not rainfall**.

⚠️ **And the table's real value is the arithmetic a candidate can do with it.** ❗ Any roof, any rainfall: multiply, apply about four-fifths, and the answer is in cubic metres — which converts an abstract advocacy point into a **computable claim about a specific building**, and that is precisely what a fifteen-mark "how can it be made effective" answer needs.

### 7. The demand-side half — without which the answer is incomplete

✅ The source supplies the urban demand-side items that complete the picture: **about 12.5 litres per flush in most urban areas against cisterns needing only 5 to 7 litres**, and **reuse of kitchen-sink, wash-basin and bathroom water for flushing and gardening.** ✅ It also records **World Water Day, 22 March**, arising from a **1992 United Nations resolution**.

⚠️ **Why the demand-side half is not decoration.** ❗ The 2018 demand asks how harvesting **can be made effective**, and a supply-side-only answer cannot answer it, for an arithmetic reason: ⚠️ **a roof of 100 square metres under 1,000 millimetres of rainfall yields, at about four-fifths, roughly 80 cubic metres a year** — a real but bounded quantity. ❗ If the household's consumption is rising faster through avoidable flush volumes and unreused greywater, the harvest is **absorbed by growth in demand** and the aquifer sees nothing. ⚠️ The flush figures make the point concrete: **12.5 litres against 5 to 7** is a **halving** available at the cistern, which is a larger and cheaper intervention than most roof structures.

❗ **The structural statement for the answer:** ⚠️ **supply-side recharge and demand-side reduction are complements, not alternatives** — recharge enlarges budget supply item (iv), demand reduction restrains disposal item (iv), and **a deficit can be closed from either side**. ❗ An answer that moves on both sides has used the full budget of Lesson 15 and will outscore one that builds structures only.

### 8. India hook — Atal Bhujal Yojana's community water-security plans

🇮🇳 📰 ✅ **Re-verified at the time of writing:** the **Atal Bhujal Yojana (Atal Jal)**, a scheme of the **Ministry of Jal Shakti** implemented with **World Bank support**, is reported to carry a **total outlay of Rs 6,000 crore**, **half as a World Bank loan and half as central contribution**, covering **seven states — Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Rajasthan and Uttar Pradesh** — across **8,203 water-stressed gram panchayats in 229 blocks of 80 districts**. 📰 Its distinguishing instrument is reported to be **community-prepared Water Budgets and Water Security Plans**, with a **Program-for-Results** disbursement model tying funds to verified performance; and an impact assessment is reported to have found **improvement in groundwater levels in 180 of the 229 blocks**.

⚠️ **Why it is the right hook for this lesson, in three steps.**

- ⚠️ **It operates at the correct scale.** ❗ Lesson 15 established that **an aquifer's boundaries are hydrological, not administrative**, and Lesson 17 that **national figures cannot diagnose a district**. 📰 A scheme whose planning unit is the **gram panchayat and the block**, and whose instrument is a **locally prepared water budget**, is working at a scale at which the budget can actually be closed by the people who draw on it.
- ⚠️ **Its instrument is the budget itself.** ❗ A **water budget** is precisely Lesson 15's four-in, four-out accounting applied locally; a **water security plan** is the decision that follows from it. 📰 That the scheme's core deliverable is an accounting document rather than a structure is the most interesting thing about it, and it corroborates this file's claim that **the budget is what makes "over-exploitation" actionable**.
- ⚠️ **It pairs demand-side with supply-side.** ❗ That pairing is exactly what section 7 argued is necessary, and it is the half of the 2018 answer that the word *effective* demands.

❗ **Limitations, stated with the hook, and there are five.** ⚠️ **One:** ❗ **the ministry's own documents returned HTTP 403 in this environment**, so every figure above is **search-attested with named first-party sources** and is given **as reported at the time of writing**, with **anything uncorroborated dropped**. ⚠️ **Two:** 🔁 ❗ **the scheme's governance design, financing architecture, incentive mechanics and administration are owned by the economy and governance owners and are named here, not taught.** This file takes from it only the **scale argument and the budget instrument**. ⚠️ **Three:** the reported improvement in 180 of 229 blocks is an **assessment finding as reported**, not a measurement this edition can verify, and ❗ **a reported improvement in level is not by itself evidence of attribution** — rainfall variation between the assessment years could produce part of it. ⚠️ **Four:** the scheme covers **seven states**, while Lesson 17's over-exploitation cluster extends beyond them, so it is **not a national solution and is not presented as one**. ⚠️ **Five:** the hook is about **governance scale**, not about the physical designs of section 5; the two halves of this lesson are complementary and neither substitutes for the other.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | ⚠️ Supply side — recharge | ⚠️ Demand side — reduction |
|---|---|---|
| Budget term acted on | ✅ **Supply item (iv)**, artificial recharge | ✅ **Disposal item (iv)**, artificial discharge by pumping |
| Physical precondition | ❗ A **de-saturated, permeable** formation able to accept water | None — it works anywhere |
| Cost | ✅ **Less than a surface reservoir**; no land, no displacement | ✅ A cistern change: **12.5 litres to 5-7** |
| Scale of effect | Bounded by **roof or catchment area** and by rainfall | Bounded by **consumption**, which may be large |
| Failure mode | **Silting, mismatch to strata, contamination, no maintenance** | Behavioural reversion |
| ❗ Verdict | **Necessary and insufficient alone** | **Necessary and insufficient alone** |

**Strongest objection.** Rainwater harvesting is an urban gesture. The volumes are trivial beside irrigation demand, and devoting an entire lesson to pits, trenches and filters in a Mains edition is disproportionate.

**Reply.** Three answers, in increasing order of force. ⚠️ **One, on proportion:** this is **the one Mains demand this topic owns**, routed to this owner by name — *"water harvesting for depleting groundwater in urban areas, how can it be made effective, fifteen marks"* — and the depth here is calibrated to that fact, as the file states at its opening. ⚠️ **Two, on volumes:** the objection measures the wrong thing. ✅ The fourth need item is **the loss of infiltration in urban areas because open ground has been paved**, so urban harvesting is **restoring a recharge pathway that urbanisation destroyed**, and the relevant comparison is with **what the surface used to infiltrate**, not with national irrigation demand. ⚠️ **Three, on the detail:** the specifications are retained precisely because the demand asks about **effectiveness**, and ❗ **every known failure mode of a recharge structure is visible in the specifications** — wrong strata, wrong size for the catchment, no silt trap, no maintenance path, no first-flush diversion. ❗ An answer that can name those five failure modes has answered the question asked; one that lists techniques has answered a different and easier one.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ **No source available to this edition quantifies what recharge structures have actually achieved at aquifer scale in India.** ⚠️ The advantages list is a statement of **design intent**, not of measured outcome; the cost comparator is an attributed estimate without an assessment year; 📰 the one reported impact assessment is at **block level and could not be read first-party**; and the runoff coefficient is **this edition's inference** from a table's arithmetic. ❗ The honest position, and the one an answer should take, is that **the physical case for artificial recharge is strong and well specified, while the evidence on delivered aquifer-scale outcomes is thin and mostly reported rather than measured** — which is itself a reason for the monitoring that the definition of artificial recharge already implies.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Important Geophysical phenomena ... geographical features and their location-changes" (General Studies Paper I).
- **Verified linkage:** ❗ **This lesson carries the one Mains demand this topic owns: 2018, General Studies Paper I, Question 14 — water harvesting as a response to depleting groundwater in urban areas, "how can it be made effective", 15 marks, 250 words.** ⚠️ It is taught to full answer depth here, and its complete spine is reproduced at Lesson 19. ⚠️ The lesson also supplies the **supply-side half** of the response logic in the owner's fifteen-mark groundwater-and-food-security spine, which is 🔁 bridged to the food-security owner.
- **Probable framing:** the demand is a **"how can it be made effective"** question, which is a **conditions-of-success** directive, not a **list-the-techniques** directive. ❗ Allocate accordingly: roughly a third to why urban recharge is needed and what it is, and roughly two-thirds to the conditions under which it works.
- **Traps:** listing techniques without conditions; recommending a pit on clay; omitting the silt trap and the maintenance path; omitting first-flush diversion and so proposing a contamination pathway; quoting the runoff coefficient as published; quantifying the gallons-per-person claim; expounding river interlinking; teaching the scheme's governance design.
- **Answer use:** *"Urban harvesting is not a water-supply addition but the restoration of a recharge pathway that paving destroyed; it is effective only where the structure is matched to a de-saturated permeable formation, sized to the roof it serves, fitted with a desilting chamber, a graded fill that can be lifted and cleaned, and first-flush diversion to prevent the dirtiest water being injected directly into the aquifer — and only where demand-side reduction prevents the harvest from being absorbed by consumption growth."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Rainwater harvesting** = collection and storage of rain water **at the surface or in a sub-surface aquifer before it is lost as surface run-off**. ✅ **Artificial recharge** = augmenting the reservoir **at a rate exceeding that under natural conditions of replenishment**.
- ❗ **Artificial recharge is supply item (iv) of the budget** — an enlargement of an existing term, not an addition to the system.
- ✅ **Need, seven items:** overcome surface-water inadequacy · arrest level decline · **enhance availability at a specific place and time** · ❗ **increase infiltration, which has decreased drastically in urban areas because open ground has been paved** · improve quality by dilution · increase agricultural production · improve ecology through vegetation cover.
- ✅ **Advantages, eleven items:** cheaper than a surface reservoir · ❗ **the aquifer itself serves as the distribution system** · **no land wasted, no population displaced** · not exposed to evaporation and pollution · environment friendly · aquifer productivity rises · **flood hazards reduced** · levels rise · drought effects mitigated · **soil erosion reduced**.
- ✅ **Potential areas, four criteria:** declining levels · **substantially de-saturated aquifers** · inadequate availability in lean months · **urbanisation-driven loss of infiltration**.
- ✅ **Urban techniques:** **recharge pit · recharge trench · tubewell · recharge well** · **Rural techniques:** **gully plug · contour bund · gabion · percolation tank · check dam / cement plug / nala bund · recharge shaft · dugwell recharge · ground water dams and subsurface dykes.** ❗ The first two are **Lesson 9's conservation structures** — the coupling closed. ⚠️ The **subsurface dyke** copies Lesson 15's **dyke spring**.
- ✅ **Fig. 15.7 recharge pit:** roof **~100 sq m**, alluvial, permeable rock at or near surface; **1-2 m wide, 2-3 m deep**; graded fill — **boulders 5-20 cm** bottom, **gravel 5-10 mm** middle, **coarse sand 1.5-2 mm** top, ✅ **so silt deposits on the sand and can be removed**; roof mesh; **desilting chamber** · **Fig. 15.8 recharge trench:** roof **200-300 sq m**, permeable strata at shallow depth; **0.5-1 m wide, 1-1.5 m deep, 10-20 m long**; **15 % coarse sand, 20 % 20 mm gravel, 50 % 40 mm boulder**.
- ✅ **Fig. 15.9 tubewell recharge:** where **shallow aquifers have dried up**; **10 cm PVC roof drains**; **first-flush diversion through the bottom of the drain pipe**; inline PVC filter **1-1.2 m long**, **15 cm** diameter for roofs under **150 sq m**, **20 cm** for larger; **6.25 cm reducer at each end**; **three chambers** divided by PVC screens so filter materials do not mix.
- ❗ **All three share a silt trap, a graded filter and a maintenance path.** ⚠️ A recharge structure without a filter is a **contamination structure** (Lesson 17).
- ✅ **Table 15.10**, source **CGWB, *Rainwater Harvesting Techniques to Augment Ground Water*, 2003**: rainfall **20-3,000 mm** against roof areas **100-2,000 sq m**, giving volume in cubic metres.
- ⚠️ ❗ **DERIVED, NOT STATED:** 100 mm over 100 sq m = **10 cu m gross**, tabulated as **8** → an implied runoff coefficient of **about 0.8**. ❗ **Tag as an inference; the table states no coefficient.** ⚠️ It represents wetting, evaporation, first flush and spillage — about a fifth.
- ✅ **Demand side:** **about 12.5 litres per flush** in most urban areas against cisterns needing only **5-7 litres**; **reuse of kitchen-sink, wash-basin and bathroom water** for flushing and gardening. ✅ **World Water Day, 22 March**, from a **1992 United Nations resolution**.
- ✅ **Bounded cost comparator only:** attributed to **Jayanta Bandhopadhyay, Indian Institute of Management Kolkata**, citing CGWB — **37 BCM recharged locally at about Rs 24,500 crore** against **Rs 5,60,000 crore for 175 BCM from interlinking**. 🔁 ❗ **Interlinking itself is owned elsewhere and is not expounded** · ❗ **NOT QUANTIFIED:** the Centre for Science and Environment claim about harvesting on **2 per cent of India's land area** — ❗ **the page states no period for the per-person volume**, so it is cited qualitatively or omitted.
- 📰 🇮🇳 **Atal Bhujal Yojana (Atal Jal), Ministry of Jal Shakti, with World Bank support:** reported **Rs 6,000 crore** outlay, half loan and half central; **seven states** — Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh; **8,203 gram panchayats, 229 blocks, 80 districts**; **community Water Budgets and Water Security Plans**; **Program-for-Results** disbursement; improvement reported in **180 of 229 blocks**. ❗ Ministry documents returned 403; figures are search-attested with named first-party sources, as reported at the time of writing. 🔁 **Governance design routed.**
- ❗ ⚠️ **"Effective" means: aquifer-appropriate · maintained · protected from contamination and re-extraction · monitored · matched to demand.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A municipality mandates a recharge pit of 1.5 metres by 2.5 metres for every building above 100 square metres of roof area across its whole jurisdiction, which spans an alluvial tract, a clay-rich floodplain and a hard-rock upland. Assess the mandate, and state what a defensible version would require.

**Model answer:** The mandate applies one structure uniformly to three hydrogeologically different settings, and it will succeed in one, fail in the second and underperform in the third. **In the alluvial tract it is correct.** A recharge pit of those dimensions is specified for buildings of about a hundred square metres of roof in alluvial areas where permeable rock is exposed at or near the surface, so the structure, the catchment and the formation are matched. **On the clay-rich floodplain it will not work at all.** Clay is the standard case of high porosity with very low permeability, so the formation will not accept water at any useful rate; the pit will fill, stand full and overflow, achieving nothing except a maintenance liability and, if unscreened, a mosquito hazard. The defensible alternative there is either surface storage, or conveyance to a location where permeable strata exist, or tubewell recharge if a deeper aquifer is accessible beneath the clay. **On the hard-rock upland it will work only if sited on structure.** Crystalline terrain has no primary porosity, so the water must enter the weathered mantle and then circulate through fracture systems; a pit sited on fresh, unfractured rock will not recharge, while one sited over a weathered zone or a fracture may recharge well. **A defensible mandate would require five things.** First, **aquifer appropriateness** established by local investigation rather than assumed, since the specification itself names a strata condition. Second, **sizing matched to the catchment**, since a pit is specified for about a hundred square metres and a trench for two to three hundred. Third, **a desilting chamber and a graded fill with coarse sand on top**, so that silt is deposited where it can be lifted and removed — the design's maintenance path. Fourth, **first-flush diversion and filtration**, because injecting unfiltered water bypasses the soil, which is the system's natural filter, and contamination of an aquifer may persist for decades or centuries. Fifth, **monitoring of water levels**, because artificial recharge is defined as augmentation at a rate exceeding the natural, and that is a claim nobody can make without measurement.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a recharge structure as a self-evidently beneficial object. It is a device for moving water into a specific formation, and if that formation will not accept water, or if the water arriving is dirty, the structure achieves nothing or does harm.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Urban rainwater harvesting fails more often from maintenance than from design." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is well founded, and the designs anticipate it. A recharge pit is back-filled in graded form, with boulders of five to twenty centimetres at the base, gravel of five to ten millimetres above, and coarse sand at the top, specifically so that silt arriving with runoff is deposited on the sand and can be removed. That is a maintenance provision built into the design, and it implies the failure mode: a structure whose sand layer is never lifted and cleaned silts up and stops accepting water within a few seasons, while remaining intact. The same applies to roof meshes, desilting chambers and the three-chamber inline filter used for tubewell recharge. But two design failures are equally common and terminal rather than reversible: siting a pit where the formation is impermeable, and omitting first-flush diversion, which injects the dry season's accumulated contamination directly into the aquifer.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the graded fill explained as a maintenance provision with the silt-on-sand rationale; 2 for the failure mode stated as silting while the structure remains visibly intact; 3 for two terminal design failures named — impermeable formation and absent first flush — with their consequences; 1 for one accurate dimension or fill specification; 1 for a graded verdict rather than endorsement. Deduct 2 for recommending a structure without a strata condition, and 2 for quoting the runoff coefficient as a published value.
